Global periodic-data rigidity for irreducible toral automorphisms Thanks: 1 Based on research supported by NSF grant DMS-2452194 Thanks: Key words and phrases: Hyperbolic toral automorphism, conjugacy, global rigidity, linear cocycle
Zhenqi Jenny Wang 1

Abstract
. We prove a global C 1 + Hölder C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}} -rigidity theorem for Anosov diffeomorphisms of tori with irreducible linearization. Let f : 𝕋 N → 𝕋 N f:\mathbb{T}^{N}\to\mathbb{T}^{N} be a C 2 C^{2} Anosov diffeomorphism with linearization A ∈ G ​ L ​ ( N , ℤ ) A\in GL(N,\mathbb{Z}) , and assume that A A is irreducible. If, for every periodic point p = f n ​ p p=f^{n}p , the linear maps D ​ f p n Df_{p}^{n} and A n A^{n} are conjugate, then the Franks–Manning conjugacy between f f and A A is C 1 + Hölder C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}} . Thus, in the irreducible case, periodic data completely characterize global C 1 + Hölder C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}} -rigidity. The proof does not assume conformality, uniform quasiconformality, simplicity of the spectrum, or any restriction on Lyapunov multiplicities. The main ingredient is a new partial-to-global rigidity mechanism combining geometric and analytic arguments. We first obtain partial cocycle rigidity on canonical conformal layers inside the Lyapunov blocks by geometric methods, and then promote this partial rigidity to full regularity of the conjugacy along the Lyapunov blocks by analytic methods. The same method yields a local rigidity theorem for C 1 C^{1} -small C 1 + Hölder C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}} perturbations of A A .



1.
Introduction

Hyperbolic toral automorphisms are the fundamental algebraic models of
uniformly hyperbolic dynamics. By the Franks–Manning classification theorem
[
7
]
,
[
33
]
,
every Anosov diffeomorphism
f:\mathbb{T}^{N}\to\mathbb{T}^{N}
is topologically
conjugate to its linearization
A\in GL(N,\mathbb{Z})
. More precisely, there exists a homeomorphism
H
such that

\displaystyle H\circ f=A\circ H.

Any two such conjugacies differ by an affine automorphism of
\mathbb{T}^{N}

commuting with
A

[
41
]
; in particular, they have the same regularity. The conjugacy is always bi-Hölder, but in general it need not be
C^{1}
.

The central rigidity problem is to decide when this topological conjugacy has
higher regularity. The most elementary
C^{1}
-obstruction is given by
periodic data. Suppose that
H
is a
C^{1}
conjugacy between
f
and

A
. If
p=f^{n}p
, then
H(p)
is an
n
-periodic point of
A
, and
differentiating

H\circ f^{n}=A^{n}\circ H

at
p
gives

DH_{p}\circ Df_{p}^{n}=A^{n}\circ DH_{p}.

Thus
Df_{p}^{n}
and
A^{n}
must be linearly conjugate. We say that
f
and

A
have
the same periodic data
if this condition holds for every
periodic point
p=f^{n}p
.

The main question
is whether, for irreducible toral automorphisms, this necessary condition is
also sufficient. The irreducibility assumption is essential: in the reducible case, de la
Llave constructed examples showing that periodic data alone do not imply

C^{1}
rigidity. The periodic-data rigidity problem for Anosov automorphisms goes back to the
foundational work of de la Llave-Marco-Moriyón in the 1980s. In the
irreducible case it has led to the following conjecture.

Conjecture 1.1
.

Let
f:\mathbb{T}^{N}\to\mathbb{T}^{N}
be a
C^{s}
,
s>1
Anosov diffeomorphism with
linearization
A
. Suppose that
A
is irreducible. Then:

(1)

Local rigidity.
If
f
is a
C^{1}
-small perturbation of
A
, and if
f
and
A
have
the same periodic data, then
f
is

C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
conjugate to
A
.

(2)

Global rigidity.
If
s
is sufficiently large and if
f
and
A
have
the same periodic data, then the topological conjugacy between
f
and
A

is
C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
.

(3)

C^{\infty}
bootstrapping.
If
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{N})
and the conjugacy is
C^{1}
, then the conjugacy is in fact
C^{\infty}
.

The
C^{\infty}
bootstrapping statement, item
3
, was recently proved in

[
40
]
. In this paper we prove the periodic-data rigidity statements.
Our first main theorem is the following global rigidity result.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
f
be a
C^{2}
Anosov diffeomorphism on
\mathbb{T}^{N}
, and let
A
be its
linearization. Suppose that
A
is an irreducible automorphism of

\mathbb{T}^{N}
. If
f
and
A
have the same periodic data, then
f
is

C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
conjugate to
A
.

This theorem is global: no smallness assumption is imposed on
f
. The
C^{2}

regularity assumption is used to guarantee the existence of the dominated
splittings needed in the proof. In the local setting, this regularity
assumption can be reduced.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
A
be an irreducible Anosov automorphism of
\mathbb{T}^{N}
, and let
f

be a
C^{1}
-small
C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
perturbation of
A
. If
f
and
A
have the same periodic data, then
f
is

C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
conjugate to
A
.

Together with the
C^{\infty}
bootstrapping theorem of
[
40
]
, these results
settle Conjecture
1.1
in the stated regularity classes.

1.1.
History and motivation

In dimension two, the
C^{\infty}
local periodic-data rigidity problem for
Anosov automorphisms was solved by de la Llave–Marco–Moriyón in their
pioneering work
[
12
,
15
,
14
]
, building on
[
13
]
. In higher dimensions the situation is
more subtle: reducibility creates genuine obstructions that are invisible to
periodic data.

Recall that a toral automorphism is irreducible if its characteristic
polynomial is irreducible over
\mathbb{Q}
, equivalently, if it has no
nontrivial rational invariant subspace. De la Llave constructed examples of
reducible Anosov automorphisms and
C^{\infty}
perturbations with the same
periodic data that are only Hölder conjugate. Moreover, for every

k\in\mathbb{N}
and every
N\geq 4
, there are reducible Anosov
automorphisms on
\mathbb{T}^{N}
with
C^{\infty}
perturbations having the same
periodic data but whose conjugacies are only
C^{k}
, and not
C^{k+1}

[
15
]
. These counterexamples show that irreducibility is an essential
hypothesis and led to Conjecture
1.1
.

The regularity problem naturally splits into two parts. The first asks whether
the periodic-data obstruction implies
C^{1}
, or more precisely

C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
, regularity of the conjugacy. The second asks whether
a
C^{1}
conjugacy between
C^{\infty}
systems automatically bootstraps to a

C^{\infty}
conjugacy. The
C^{\infty}
bootstrapping problem is the third
part of Conjecture
1.1
. A positive answer on
\mathbb{T}^{3}
was given in

[
32
]
. The result of
[
39
]
gives such a bootstrapping theorem when

f
is sufficiently
C^{l}
-close to
A
, for very large
l
, under a
weak irreducibility assumption on
A
. Recently, a complete answer was given
in
[
40
]
under a much weaker irreducibility assumption. The present
paper addresses the periodic-data rigidity parts of Conjecture
1.1
.

There has been substantial partial progress toward the local rigidity part of
Conjecture
1.1
for large classes of irreducible automorphisms
A
;
see
[
21
,
17
,
16
,
29
,
22
,
30
,
23
,
38
]
. More recently, some of these
local results were globalized in
[
19
]
. These results typically require
additional spectral or geometric assumptions, often ensuring that the relevant
Lyapunov blocks admit invariant conformal structures or are uniformly
quasiconformal; for instance, such assumptions are often satisfied when the
relevant Lyapunov blocks are one- or two-dimensional.

1.2.
Comparison with previous methods

The present results remove all of these additional assumptions: no smallness
assumption, no simplicity assumption on the spectrum, no restriction on
Lyapunov multiplicities, and no full invariant conformal structure are
imposed. The
only algebraic hypothesis is irreducibility of
A
.

We next explain why the previously available methods do not suffice in this
generality.

1.2.1.
Geometric obstruction: full conformality

A common strategy, which we call
the geometric method
, is first to prove

C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
regularity of the conjugacy along invariant Lyapunov
foliations and then to use Journé’s lemma to obtain global regularity.
Conformal or uniformly quasiconformal structures on the Lyapunov subspaces
play a crucial role in this approach, because they allow one to control the
growth rates of
Df^{n}
along the corresponding Lyapunov foliations. This
control leads to
C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
regularity of the conjugacy along those
foliations. To the best of the author’s knowledge, previously known approaches to this
periodic-data rigidity problem rely, at the decisive first step, on such a
geometric mechanism.

Consequently, this approach is especially effective when the Lyapunov blocks
are one- or two-dimensional, or more generally when they carry invariant
conformal structures. In the general irreducible case, however, Lyapunov blocks
may have higher dimension and need not be conformal. To ensure the existence
of such conformal structures, one has to impose restrictive spectral
assumptions, for instance that the automorphism has no more than three
eigenvalues with the same modulus. Little was known without such geometric or
spectral assumptions.

1.2.2.
Analytic methods obstruction: twisted negative-time series

The main analytic ingredient in the proof concerns twisted cohomological
equations. After passing from tangent-bundle cocycles to the manifold-level
conjugacy equation, the component
H_{i}=p_{i}\circ H
of the Franks–Manning
conjugacy satisfies an equation of the form

\displaystyle H_{i}=p_{i}+h_{i},\qquad A_{i}h_{i}-h_{i}\circ f=R_{i}

where
A_{i}=A|_{E_{i}}
and
R_{i}
is the
E_{i}
-component of the nonlinear
error. Thus the regularity of the conjugacy is reduced, in part, to the
regularity of solutions of a twisted cohomological equation.

For an unstable Lyapunov block, this equation gives the positive-time
representation

h_{i}=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}R_{i}\circ f^{m}.

This representation is well adapted to differentiating
h_{i}
along the
stable foliations of
f
, because the positive iterates of
f
contract
stable directions. To obtain differentiability along the unstable foliations,
one would like to use instead the formal negative-time expression

h_{i}^{-}:=-\sum_{m=-1}^{-\infty}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}R_{i}\circ f^{m}.

The desired strategy is first to prove that
h_{i}^{-}
is a well-defined
distribution and has the required distributional derivatives along unstable
directions, and then to prove that

h_{i}^{-}=h_{i}\qquad\text{as distributions}.

Once this is achieved, differentiability in both stable and unstable
directions can be combined by an elliptic-regularity-type theorem for
distributions along foliations. This step generally needs
the error term
R_{i}
to have sufficiently high regularity. For this reason, existing analytic approaches usually require

f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{N})
, or at least sufficiently high regularity.

This type of analytic strategy is common in the study of
C^{\infty}
rigidity
for higher-rank actions
[
26
]
,
[
27
]
, but its application to rank-one systems is not
standard in the literature. In higher-rank settings, one often has the freedom
to choose a group element for which the twist in the cohomological equation is
neutral, or nearly neutral, relative to the relevant directions. In that case,
convergence of the negative-time distribution can often be obtained from decay
of correlations.

For rank-one actions, this flexibility is absent. The twist
A_{i}
is tied to
a chosen Lyapunov block and in general it cannot be replaced by
a more favorable, nearly neutral twist. In
attempting to prove convergence of
h_{i}^{-}
, one must balance the growth of

\bigl\|A_{i}^{-(m+1)}\bigr\|\qquad(m\to-\infty)

against the decay of correlations for
f
. For a nonlinear
f
, the
available decay estimates are obtained through the Hölder conjugacy with

A
, and the resulting rate generally does not dominate the growth coming
from the twist. Even for the algebraic automorphism
A
, standard exponential mixing
arguments compensate for the twist only when the observables have sufficiently
high regularity. The required order depends on
A
, and in general

C^{2}
regularity is far from sufficient. Consequently, it is difficult in general to prove that

h_{i}^{-}
is even a distribution by this method.

There is a further obstruction. Even if
h_{i}^{-}
is known to be a
distribution, proving its differentiability along unstable foliations by this
analytic approach remains problematic. One difficulty is that the
elliptic-regularity-type argument requires control of distributional
derivatives of high order. In the present setting, however,
f
is only
assumed to be
C^{2}
, so the error term
R_{i}
has limited regularity. Even in the
C^{\infty}
setting, higher regularity of
f
alone does not
remove the obstruction caused by the twist. Existing techniques for overcoming this twist require an a priori

C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
regularity assumption on the conjugacy
H
. This
assumption is used to transfer growth estimates from the linear iterates

A^{n}
to the nonlinear iterates
Df^{n}
; see
[
40
]
. However,
obtaining such growth estimates for
Df^{n}
is precisely one of the core
difficulties in proving
C^{1}
rigidity.

Thus the usual analytic approach does not provide a viable starting point for
rank-one rigidity in this setting. New ingredients, substantially different
from the standard higher-rank method, are needed to handle the negative-time
series and to prove the desired rigidity.

1.2.3.
The linear cocycle obstruction: non-abelian Livšic failure

The main geometric ingredient in the proof concerns establishing linear
cocycle rigidity. The cocycle-theoretic input is closely related to
Livšic theory for linear cocycles. We recall this background in order to
explain why the argument cannot rely on a standard cocycle-rigidity theorem.

Let
g:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}
be a hyperbolic system and let
G
be a
topological group. Given two Hölder cocycles

\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2}:\mathcal{X}\to G
, one asks whether they are
cohomologous, namely whether there exists a map

\mathcal{C}:\mathcal{X}\to G
such that

\mathcal{C}(gx)\cdot\mathcal{F}_{1}(x)=\mathcal{F}_{2}(x)\cdot\mathcal{C}(x),\qquad x\in\mathcal{X}.

A necessary condition is conjugacy of periodic data: for every periodic point

p=g^{n}p
, there should exist
\mathcal{C}(p)\in G
such that

\mathcal{C}(p)\cdot\mathcal{F}_{1}^{n}(p)=\mathcal{F}_{2}^{n}(p)\cdot\mathcal{C}(p),

where

\mathcal{F}_{i}^{n}(p)=\mathcal{F}_{i}(g^{n-1}p)\cdot\mathcal{F}_{i}(g^{n-2}p)\cdots\mathcal{F}_{i}(p),\qquad i=1,2.

For abelian groups, the classical Livšic theorem gives a positive answer:
the periodic obstruction is sufficient for cohomology

[
9
,
10
]
. The case in which one cocycle is trivial is also well
understood in several important settings, including compact groups

[
35
]
and some non-abelian groups
[
20
,
1
]
.

The general non-abelian problem is much more delicate. It cannot be reduced to
the case of a trivial cocycle, and Livšic theory fails in this generality
even under boundedness assumptions on the conjugacies over periodic orbits

[
37
]
. Moreover, even for cocycles that are close to one another,
conjugate periodic data need not imply continuous cohomology. Indeed, examples
of
SL(3,\mathbb{R})
-valued cocycles with conjugate periodic data which are
not continuously cohomologous were constructed in
[
30
]
.

This obstruction is directly relevant to the present paper. In our setting,
one would like to compare the nonlinear cocycle
Df|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}}
with
the linear cocycle
A_{i}
over a Lyapunov block
\mathcal{E}_{i}
. The
preceding discussion shows that conjugate periodic data are not sufficient, in
general, to obtain full cohomology of such cocycles. Thus full cocycle
rigidity on the whole Lyapunov block cannot be used as a black box.

The first new ingredient of the paper is therefore a partial cocycle rigidity
theorem. Although the full cocycle
Df|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}}
need not be
cohomologous to
A_{i}
, we show that the periodic-data assumption implies
rigidity on a canonical conformal layer inside this block.

At first sight, obtaining such partial cocycle rigidity is not easier than
obtaining full cocycle rigidity. First, the conformal layer arises from an
abstract construction. Hence it is not a priori clear that it is close to any

A
-invariant subspace, even when
f
is
C^{1}
-close to
A
. This makes
it unclear where such a conformal subbundle should be sent by a transfer map,
and therefore makes it difficult even to formulate the relevant linear cocycle
equation. Second, as explained above, standard Livšic theory does not
extend to general linear cocycles. Thus it is not reasonable to expect to solve
a cohomology equation without first identifying an appropriate solvability
condition.

Consequently, a new scheme, substantially different from the traditional
cocycle-rigidity approach, is needed. The partial rigidity result obtained here
is the starting point of the partial-to-global mechanism used in the current
paper.

1.3.
The new partial-to-global mechanism

We introduce
a different mechanism for proving periodic-data rigidity, which combines geometric and analytic methods. The phrase
“partial-to-global” refers to two distinct transitions. The first transition
is from partial linear cocycle rigidity to partial differentiability of the
conjugacy. More precisely, we prove cocycle rigidity for
Df
only on a
canonical conformal subbundle, and then show that this subbundle integrates to
a foliation along which
H
is
C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
. This is the main
geometric input of the paper. Up to this stage, the proof uses only the
conformality of the subbundle, the periodic-data assumption, and the
corresponding conformal structure for
A
; irreducibility of
A
is not
used.

The second transition occurs only after passing from tangent-bundle cocycles
to the manifold-level cohomological equation for the component

H_{i}=p_{i}\circ H
of the conjugacy. At this level, irreducibility of
A

enters. The first transition gives partial
C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}

regularity of
H^{-1}
along a linear foliation tangent to an

A
-invariant subspace
W\subset E_{i}
. Irreducibility of
A
implies
that
W
is Diophantine. This Diophantine property controls the small
divisors that arise when one integrates by parts against Fourier modes. Since
the small-divisor loss is only polynomial, the contraction of
A^{-n}
along
the same foliation gives the exponential gain needed to control the formal
negative-time series.
This allows us to identify

h_{i}^{-}=h_{i}\qquad\text{as distributions}.

Differentiating this identity along an arbitrary Hölder vector field

\mathcal{V}
tangent to
\mathcal{E}_{i}
, we obtain

D_{\mathcal{V}}h_{i}=D_{\mathcal{V}}h_{i}^{-}

as distributions. Thus the
\mathcal{E}_{i}
-directional distributional derivatives of
h_{i}

have both positive- and negative-time representations, not merely the
representation coming from the initial conformal subbundle.

The remaining analytic step is to upgrade this distributional information to
Hölder regularity. Using the triangular reduction of

Df|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}}
, the positive-time representation gives translation
estimates in stable directions, while the negative-time representation gives
the corresponding estimates in unstable directions. Since the two
distributional derivatives above are equal, these one-sided estimates combine
to give the two-sided estimates required by the distribution-to-Hölder
criterion. Hence
D_{\mathcal{V}}h_{i}
is represented by a Hölder map. Since

\mathcal{V}
is arbitrary in
\mathcal{E}_{i}
, it follows that
h_{i}
, and
therefore the corresponding component
H_{i}
, is

C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
along the whole Lyapunov foliation

\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f}
. This is the analytic promotion from the initial conformal
layer to the entire Lyapunov block.

2.
Proof strategy

We now describe the main ideas in the proof of Theorems
1.2
and

1.3
. We first reduce the problem to leafwise regularity. Let

E_{A}^{u}=E_{i_{0}}\oplus E_{i_{0}+1}\oplus\cdots\oplus E_{\ell}

be the unstable subspace of
A
, decomposed according to the moduli of its
eigenvalues, and let

\mathcal{E}_{f}^{u}=\mathcal{E}_{i_{0}}\oplus\mathcal{E}_{i_{0}+1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{E}_{\ell}

be the corresponding dominated splitting for the unstable subspace of
f
.

The key step in the proof is the following. Suppose that

H(\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f})=\mathcal{W}_{i}^{A}\qquad\text{for some }i_{0}\leq i\leq\ell.

Then
H
is
C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
along
\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f}
. Since

H_{i}=p_{i}\circ H=p_{i}+h_{i}
, this amounts to proving that the solution
h_{i}

of the twisted cohomological equation

A_{i}h_{i}-h_{i}\circ f=R_{i}

is
C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
along
\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f}
. As explained in
Section
1.2.2
, the proof has two main parts.

\hypertarget{o.1}{(\mathcal{P}_{1})}

We prove that
h_{i}^{-}
is a well-defined distribution and that

h_{i}^{-}=h_{i}\qquad\text{as distributions}.

\hypertarget{o.2}{(\mathcal{P}_{2})}

For every
\alpha
-Hölder vector field
\mathcal{V}
taking values in

\mathcal{E}_{i}
, we prove that the distributional derivative

D_{\mathcal{V}}h_{i}=D_{\mathcal{V}}h_{i}^{-}

is represented by a Hölder map. Therefore
h_{i}
is

C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
along
\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f}
.

The proof has three main stages. First, we obtain partial cocycle rigidity on
a canonical conformal layer inside a Lyapunov block. Second, we convert this
partial cocycle rigidity into partial differentiability of the conjugacy and
use irreducibility to control the negative-time distributional series. Third,
we use a distribution-to-Hölder argument and an induction over the canonical
flag to promote this partial regularity to full

C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
regularity along the whole Lyapunov block. Repeating
this over all Lyapunov blocks and applying Journé’s lemma gives the global
result.

2.1.
Proof of
\mathcal{P}_{1}

In this part, we explain how
(\mathcal{P}_{1})
is proved.

2.1.1.
Establishing partial linear cocycle rigidity inside a subbundle of
\mathcal{E}_{i}

This part summarizes the contents of Sections
4
and
5
.

Although the full cocycle
Df|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}}
need not be conformal and, in
general, cannot be cohomologous to the linear cocycle
A_{i}
, there is a
canonical invariant flag

0=\mathcal{F}_{i,0}\subset\mathcal{F}_{i,1}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{F}_{i,j_{i}}=\mathcal{E}_{i}

such that each quotient
\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}/\mathcal{F}_{i,j}
carries an
invariant conformal structure; see (
5
) of
Section
3.1
. The first step is to prove cocycle rigidity on the first
conformal layer. More precisely, for a suitable
A
-invariant subspace

V_{i,1}\subset E_{i}
, we construct a Hölder bundle isomorphism
\mathcal{C}_{i,1}:\mathcal{F}_{i,1}\longrightarrow V_{i,1}

satisfying

(2.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(fx)\circ Df_{x}|_{\mathcal{F}_{i,1}(x)}=A_{i}|_{V_{i,1}}\circ\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(x).

The method is different from the standard cocycle-rigidity approach. Instead
of prescribing
V_{i,1}
in advance and trying to solve a linear cocycle
cohomology equation with values in
GL(V_{i,1})
, we write

\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(x)=p_{i}|_{\mathcal{F}_{i,1}(x)}+q(x)

and reduce the problem to the twisted cohomological equation

A_{i}q(x)-q(fx)\circ Df_{x}|_{\mathcal{F}_{i,1}(x)}=(\mathcal{R}_{i})_{\mathcal{F}_{i,1}(x)}.

This formulation avoids the need to know the target subspace
V_{i,1}
in
advance. Once
q
is constructed, the subspace
V_{i,1}
is obtained as the
image of
\mathcal{C}_{i,1}
.

The construction of
q
is explicit. The periodic-data assumption allows us
to start from a solution at a fixed point. Stable and unstable holonomies then
extend this solution along the corresponding invariant manifolds, and
conjugacy of periodic data implies that the stable and unstable extensions
agree on homoclinic points. This gives a global Hölder solution of the
partial cocycle equation; see the proof of Proposition
4.1
.

We then apply a similar construction to each conformal quotient

\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}/\mathcal{F}_{i,j}
. This yields a bundle isomorphism

\mathcal{C}_{i}(x):\mathcal{E}_{i}(x)\to E_{i}

under which
Df|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}}
takes a block upper triangular form

\widetilde{A}_{i}
at every
x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
, with diagonal blocks
coinciding with the corresponding diagonal blocks of
A_{i}
; see
Theorem
5.1
.

2.1.2.
Establishing partial regularity of
H

This part summarizes the contents of Sections
6
and
7
.
In this step, we convert partial cocycle rigidity into partial regularity of the conjugacy. More precisely, we show that
\mathcal{F}_{i,1}
is uniquely integrable to a foliation with uniformly
C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
leaves, denoted by
\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{F}_{i,1}}
, and that
H
is
C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
along
\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{F}_{i,1}}
. Moreover, if
W\subset E_{i}
is the corresponding
A
-invariant subspace and
W^{L}
denotes the linear foliation tangent to
W
,

H\bigl(\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{F}_{i,1}}\bigr)=W^{L}.

The precise statement is given in Theorem
7.1
.

Since the integrability of
\mathcal{F}_{i,1}
is not known a priori, we
cannot begin with leafwise differentiability. Instead, we introduce the notion
of curve differentiability; see Section
6.2
. We prove that, for any

C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
curve
\gamma(t)
satisfying

\gamma^{\prime}(t)\in\mathcal{F}_{i,1}
, there exists
\delta>0
such that, for
every
C^{1}
function
\omega
compactly supported in

(-\delta,\delta)
,

(2.2)

\displaystyle\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}H\circ\gamma(t)\omega^{\prime}(t)\,dt=-\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\mathcal{D}_{i}(\gamma(t))(\gamma^{\prime}(t))\omega(t)\,dt.

Here
\mathcal{D}_{i}
is
\mathcal{C}_{i,1}
, up to a constant linear
isomorphism.

The main difficulty in proving (
2.2
) is that the relevant estimates
come from the partial cocycle rigidity relation for
\mathcal{C}_{i,1}
, while
the expression above is supported on a one-dimensional curve. Formally, one
would like to differentiate the positive-time series for
H_{i}=p_{i}+h_{i}
along

\gamma
. Since

\gamma^{\prime}(t)\in\mathcal{F}_{i,1}(\gamma(t))
, the partial cocycle rigidity
relation (
2.1
) should control the resulting derivatives. However, the series is
difficult to estimate directly on the curve. The key idea is therefore to
approximate the curve distribution by normalized averages over small plaques
in a foliation box. This converts the curve-level problem into an averaged
problem on
\mathbb{T}^{N}
, where the previously established estimates can be
applied.

Identity (
2.2
) implies that
DH|_{\mathcal{F}_{i,1}}
is given,
up to a constant linear isomorphism, by
\mathcal{C}_{i,1}
. It follows that

\mathcal{F}_{i,1}
is uniquely integrable and that
H
is

C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
along the resulting foliation.

Up to this point, the argument works at the tangent-bundle level and does not
use irreducibility of
A
. The subspace
W
obtained here will be used in
the next step: irreducibility of
A
implies that
W
has the Diophantine
property needed to control the negative-time distribution.

2.1.3.
Identifying
h_{i}^{-}
with
h_{i}

This part summarizes the contents of Section
8
. The negative-time
series
h_{i}^{-}
is handled using the partial
C^{1+\alpha}
regularity of

H^{-1}
along the linear foliation tangent to the subspace
W\subset E_{i}

obtained above. The Diophantine property of
W
then allows us to integrate
by parts against Fourier modes and to control the small divisors that appear.
This shows that the negative endpoint in the telescoping argument tends to
zero. Consequently,

h_{i}^{-}\circ H^{-1}=h_{i}\circ H^{-1}\qquad\text{as distributions}.

Changing variables back through
H
, we obtain

h_{i}^{-}=h_{i}\qquad\text{as distributions}.

This is one place where irreducibility of
A
is used in an essential way: it guarantees the Diophantine property for the invariant subspaces that arise in the proof. A new feature of the argument is that the decay needed to control the formal negative-time expression is not obtained from standard exponential mixing estimates for toral automorphisms. Instead, partial
C^{1+\alpha}
regularity along a Diophantine linear foliation is combined with a Fourier integration-by-parts argument. The Diophantine property controls the small divisors with only polynomial loss, while the contraction of
A^{-n}
along the same foliation provides the exponential gain needed to realize the formal negative-time expression as a distribution and identify it with
h_{i}\circ H^{-1}
.

2.2.
Proof of
\mathcal{P}_{2}

Section
9
proves
\mathcal{P}_{2}
. We take distributional
derivatives along an
\alpha
-Hölder vector field
\mathcal{V}
taking
values in
\mathcal{E}_{i}
. The equality
h_{i}^{-}=h_{i}
implies

D_{\mathcal{V}}h_{i}=D_{\mathcal{V}}h_{i}^{-}

as distributions. Hence the derivative of
h_{i}
along the full Lyapunov
block
\mathcal{E}_{i}
has two distributional representations: a positive-time
one and a negative-time one. More precisely,

\lim_{m\to\infty}A_{i}^{-m}p_{i}\circ Df_{z}^{m}(\mathcal{V}_{z})=p_{i}(\mathcal{V}_{z})+D_{\mathcal{V}}h_{i}

and

\lim_{m\to-\infty}A_{i}^{-m}p_{i}\circ Df_{z}^{m}(\mathcal{V}_{z})=p_{i}(\mathcal{V}_{z})+D_{\mathcal{V}}h_{i}^{-}

in the sense of distributions.

The triangular reduction of
Df|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}}
is then used inductively to
control these two limits. The positive-time expression gives stable-direction
translation estimates, while the negative-time expression gives
unstable-direction translation estimates. After conjugating by
H
, these
become translation estimates along the linear stable and unstable foliations
of
A
. Since the positive- and negative-time distributions are equal, these
one-sided estimates combine to give the two-sided estimates required by the
distribution-to-Hölder criterion. Therefore
D_{\mathcal{V}}h_{i}
is represented by a Hölder map. Since

\mathcal{V}
was arbitrary in
\mathcal{E}_{i}
, this gives

C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
regularity of
H
along
\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f}
.

2.3.
Scope of the method

The method developed in this paper may be viewed as an important step toward the broader
rank-one rigidity program, including both periodic-data rigidity and Lyapunov
rigidity on general nilmanifolds. A notable feature of the argument is that it does not rely on a full
conformal structure. Instead, it combines partial cocycle rigidity with an
analytic promotion mechanism for twisted cohomological equations. This provides
a new perspective on rigidity problems in which conformality or uniform
quasiconformality is not available.

In the nilmanifold setting, however, new difficulties arise. Existing rigidity
results often require stronger spectral assumptions, such as simplicity of the
spectrum
[
18
]
. One reason is that, when the spectrum is not simple, the
intermediate foliations used in the toral case may no longer be available in a
natural form. Since these intermediate foliations are the starting point of the
present argument, this creates a genuine obstruction to a direct extension of
the method.

The curve-differentiability approach introduced here may provide a way to
address this difficulty. It allows one to extract differentiability information
from distributional identities without assuming, at the outset, the existence
of all the intermediate smooth foliations that appear in the toral proof. This
suggests that the partial-to-global mechanism developed in this paper could be
useful in future work on rigidity problems beyond tori.

The same mechanism may also be relevant to rigidity problems for higher-rank
actions. In higher rank, several known rigidity results rely on semisimplicity
assumptions; see, for example,
[
25
,
28
]
. The strategy developed here,
which combines partial cocycle rigidity with an analytic promotion argument
for twisted cohomological equations, suggests a possible route toward treating
more general nonsemisimple actions.

Acknowledgements.

The author is deeply grateful to A. Gogolev for numerous clarifying
conversations. The author is also grateful to B. Kalinin and V. Sadovskaya for
referring her to their paper on partial conformal structures. The author thanks
J. DeWitt, F. Yang, and H. Hu for many helpful conversations.

3.
Preliminaries

We work in one of the following two settings:

(1)

Global setting.
Let
f
be a
C^{2}
Anosov diffeomorphism on

\mathbb{T}^{N}
, and let
A\in GL(N,\mathbb{Z})
be its linearization.

(2)

Local setting.
Let
A\in GL(N,\mathbb{Z})
be an Anosov
automorphism of
\mathbb{T}^{N}
, and let
f
be a

C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
diffeomorphism on
\mathbb{T}^{N}
which is a

C^{1}
-small perturbation of
A
.

In either case, we assume that
A
is irreducible and that
f
and
A
have
the same periodic data. Let

\rho_{1}<\cdots<\rho_{i_{0}-1}<1<\rho_{i_{0}}<\cdots\rho_{\ell}

be the distinct moduli of the eigenvalues of
A
and let

(3.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{N}=E_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus E_{\ell}

be the corresponding
A
-invariant splitting.

Then there is a bi
\eta
-Hölder conjugacy
H
between
f
and
A

[
7
]
, i.e.,

(3.2)

\displaystyle A\circ H=H\circ f.

By
[
41
]
, any two such conjugacies differ by an affine automorphism of

\mathbb{T}^{N}
commuting with
A
. Consequently, the regularity of one such
conjugacy implies the same regularity for all of them.

In what follows,
C
will denote any constant that depends only
on the given action
f
and the manifolds
\mathbb{T}^{N}
.
C_{x,y,z,\cdots}
will denote any constant that in addition to the
above depends also on parameters
x,y,z,\cdots
. We point out that the constant
C
has been changing throughout the proof.

3.1.
Same periodic data

We list several consequences of the assumption that
f
and
A
have the same
periodic data. We use the same notation in both the global and local
settings. Items
1
–
4
are consequences of
[
19
]
in
the global setting and of
[
30
]
in the local setting. The flag statement
in item
5
follows from Theorems 3.9 and 3.10 of
[
23
]
.

(1)

Dominated splitting.
There exists
\alpha>0
such
that
f
admits a
Df
-invariant splitting

T\mathbb{T}^{N}=\mathcal{E}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{E}_{\ell}

into
\alpha
-Hölder continuous subbundles. Moreover, for every sufficiently
small
\epsilon>0
, there is a Hölder metric on each
\mathcal{E}_{i}
such
that, for every
v\in\mathcal{E}_{i}
,

(3.3)

\displaystyle\rho_{i}e^{-\epsilon}\|v\|\leq\|Dfv\|\leq\rho_{i}e^{\epsilon}\|v\|.

(2)

Unique integrability of the bundles
\mathcal{E}_{i}
.
Each
\mathcal{E}_{i}

is uniquely integrable to a foliation with uniformly
C^{1+\alpha}
leaves,
which we denote by
\mathcal{W}^{f}_{i}
. The weak flag of bundles

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{i,j}=\mathcal{E}_{i}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{E}_{j},\quad i\geq i_{0}

is uniquely integrable to a weak foliation with uniformly

C^{1+\alpha}
leaves, which we denote by

\mathcal{W}^{f}_{i,j}
.

(3)

Intertwining of foliations.
For each
i\geq i_{0}
, the conjugacy
H
maps the foliation

\mathcal{W}^{f}_{i_{0},i}
to the linear foliation
\mathcal{W}^{A}_{i_{0},i}
tangent to

E_{i_{0}}\oplus\cdots\oplus E_{i}
.

(4)

Intertwining of foliations under extra assumption.
Fix
i_{0}\leq i<j\leq\ell
. Suppose that:

(a)

A
is irreducible.

(b)

H(\mathcal{W}^{f}_{i,j})=\mathcal{W}_{i,j}^{A}
, where
\mathcal{W}_{i,j}^{A}
denotes the linear foliation tangent to
E_{i}\oplus\cdots\oplus E_{j}
,

(c)

H(\mathcal{W}^{f}_{i})=\mathcal{W}^{A}_{i}
and
H
is a
C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
diffeomorphism along
\mathcal{W}^{f}_{i}
, where
\mathcal{W}_{i}^{A}
denotes the linear foliation tangent to
E_{i}
.

Then
H(\mathcal{W}^{f}_{i+1,j})=\mathcal{W}_{i+1,j}^{A}
.

(5)

Flags of
\alpha
-Hölder
Df
-invariant sub-bundles
. Suppose
\mathcal{S}
is a
Df
-invariant subbundle.
For every
f
-periodic point
p
denote by
\mu_{p}
the invariant measure
on its orbit. Set

\displaystyle\lambda_{+,\mathcal{S}}(Df,\mu_{p})

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\log\lVert Df^{n}|_{\mathcal{S}_{p}}\rVert}{n}\qquad\text{and}

\displaystyle\lambda_{-,\mathcal{S}}(Df,\mu_{p})

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\log\lVert Df^{-n}|_{\mathcal{S}_{p}}\rVert^{-1}}{n}.

Since
f
and
A
have the same
periodic data,
Df
has
constant periodic data
with exponent
\log\rho_{i}
along
\mathcal{E}_{i}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\lambda_{+,\mathcal{E}_{i}}(\rho_{i}^{-1}df,\mu_{p})=\lambda_{-,\mathcal{E}_{i}}(\rho_{i}^{-1}Df,\mu_{p})=0

for every periodic point
p
. Then it follows from Theorems 3.9 and 3.10 of
[
23
]
that the following hold:
there exists a flag of
\alpha
-
Hölder

Df
-invariant
subbundles

\{0\}=\mathcal{F}_{i,0}\subset\mathcal{F}_{i,1}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{F}_{i,j_{i}}=\mathcal{E}_{i}

and
\alpha
-
Hölder
Riemannian metrics on the quotient bundles

\mathcal{F}_{i,m}/\mathcal{F}_{i,m-1},\qquad m=1,\ldots,j_{i},

such that, for some positive
\alpha
-Hölder function

\phi_{i}:\mathbb{T}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
the quotient-cocycles induced by the cocycle

\phi_{i}Df
on

\mathcal{F}_{i,m}/\mathcal{F}_{i,m-1}
are isometries. Moreover, for any
x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}

\displaystyle\lVert Df^{n}|_{\mathcal{F}_{i,m}}\rVert\leq C\rho_{i}^{n}|n|^{m-1},\qquad\forall\,1\leq m\leq j_{i},\,\,\forall\,n\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}.

3.2.
Stable/unstable foliations and closing lemma

Since
A
is diagonalizable over
\mathbb{C}
and all eigenvalues of
A
on
E_{i}
have modulus
\rho_{i}
, we have

(3.4)

\displaystyle\lVert A^{n}|_{E_{i}}\rVert\leq C\rho_{i}^{n},\qquad\forall\,n\in\mathbb{Z}.

Define

E^{s,A}=\oplus_{\rho_{i}<1}E_{i},\qquad E^{u,A}=\oplus_{\rho_{i}>1}E_{i}.

Denote the corresponding linear foliations by
W^{s,A}
and
W^{u,A}
respectively, which are called stable and unstable foliations. Set

\displaystyle\nu_{0}=\max\{\rho_{i_{0}-1},\rho_{i_{0}}^{-1}\}^{\frac{1}{2}},\qquad\nu_{0}<\nu<1.

Then for
n\geq 0
we have

(3.5)

\displaystyle\lVert A^{n}|_{E^{s,A}}\rVert\leq C\nu_{0}^{n},\qquad\lVert A^{-n}|_{E^{u,A}}\rVert\leq C\nu_{0}^{n}.

Similarly, define

\mathcal{E}^{s,f}=\oplus_{\rho_{i}<1}\mathcal{E}_{i},\qquad\mathcal{E}^{u,f}=\oplus_{\rho_{i}>1}\mathcal{E}_{i}.

Denote the corresponding foliations by
\mathcal{W}^{s,f}
and
\mathcal{W}^{u,f}
respectively, which are called stable and unstable foliations. The foliations

\mathcal{W}^{s,f}
and
\mathcal{W}^{u,f}
have uniformly
C^{1+\alpha}
leaves.

By (
3.3
), for any
v\in\mathcal{E}^{s,f}(x)
and
n>0
, or any

v\in\mathcal{E}^{u,f}(x)
and
n<0
, we have

\displaystyle\lVert Df^{n}_{x}(v)\rVert\leq C\nu^{|n|}\lVert v\rVert.

We denote by
\mathcal{W}^{s,f}_{loc}(x)
(resp.
\mathcal{W}^{u,f}_{loc}(x)
) a sufficiently
small ball around
x
in the leaf
\mathcal{W}^{s,f}(x)
(resp.
\mathcal{W}^{u,f}(x)
).
For any
y\in\mathcal{W}^{s,f}_{loc}(x)
(resp.
y\in\mathcal{W}^{u,f}_{loc}(x)
) and any
n\geq 0
(resp.
n\leq 0
) we have

(3.6)

\displaystyle d(f^{n}x,\,f^{n}y)\leq C\nu^{n}d(x,y)\qquad\big(\text{resp. }d(f^{n}x,\,f^{n}y)\leq C\nu^{-n}d(x,y)\big).

We have a local product structure: there is
\delta>0
such that for any
x,\,y\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
with
d(x,y)\leq\delta
, there is
z\in\mathcal{W}^{s,f}_{loc}(x)\bigcap\mathcal{W}^{u,f}_{loc}(y)
satisfying

(3.7)

\displaystyle d(x,z)+d(y,z)<Cd(x,y).

The next result is a standard fact for Anosov diffeomorphisms
[
31
]
.

Theorem 3.1
.

(Closing Lemma) There exists constant
\delta>0
such that for any
x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
and

n\in\mathbb{N}
, if
d(x,f^{n}x)<\delta
, then there exists a periodic point
p\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
with
f^{n}p=p
such that

\displaystyle d(f^{i}x,f^{i}p)\leq Cd(x,f^{n}x)\nu^{\min\{i,n-i\}},\qquad\forall\,0\leq i\leq n.

3.3.
Invariant measure

Let
\mathfrak{m}
denote
Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{T}^{N}
. Let
\mu:=(H^{-1})_{*}\mathfrak{m}
.
As
H
is a topological conjugacy between
f
and
A
, the measure

\mu
is
f
-invariant.

In fact,
\mu
is the
Bowen-Margulis measure of maximal entropy for
f
, since
\mathfrak{m}
is the
Bowen-Margulis measure of maximal entropy for
A
. Indeed, denoting
topological entropy by
h_{\mathrm{top}}
and metric entropy with respect
to
\mu
by
h_{\mu}
, we have

h_{\mu}(f)=h_{\mathfrak{m}}(A)=h_{\mathrm{top}}(A)=h_{\mathrm{top}}(f).

In particular,
\mu
is ergodic, has full support, and has
local product structure.

(
3.3
) of Section
3.1
implies that the Lyapunov exponents

l_{i}^{f,\mu}
of
\mu
for

f
coincide with the Lyapunov exponents
l_{i}^{A}
of
A
.
Therefore, the sum of the positive Lyapunov exponents, counted with
multiplicity, is equal to the entropy:

h_{\mu}(f)=h_{\mathfrak{m}}(A)=\sum_{l_{i}^{A}>0}l_{i}^{A}=\sum_{l_{i}^{f,\mu}>0}l_{i}^{f,\mu}.

Thus equality holds in Ruelle’s inequality, i.e.
\mu
satisfies Pesin’s
entropy formula. It follows that

\mu
has absolutely continuous conditional measures on the
unstable foliation of
f

[
11
]
. Applying the same argument
to
f^{-1}
, we obtain absolutely continuous conditional measures on the stable
foliation of
f
. Since
\mu
has local product structure, we conclude that

\mu
itself is absolutely continuous with respect to
\mathfrak{m}
.

Moreover, the density
\kappa(x):=\frac{d\mu}{d\mathfrak{m}}(x)

is
C^{\alpha}
and positive. Indeed,
\kappa

satisfies the measurable coboundary equation

\frac{\kappa(fx)}{\kappa(x)}=|\det Df(x)|^{-1}.

By measurable Livšic regularity,
\kappa
has a
C^{\alpha}
positive
representative. Consequently,
\mu
is equivalent to
\mathfrak{m}
.

3.4.
Decomposed twisted cohomological equation

After changing coordinates, we may assume that
0
is a common fixed point of
both
f
and
A
. We choose the unique conjugacy
H
in the homotopy class
of the identity satisfying
H(0)=0
.

Lift
f
and
H
to maps on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, denoted by
\bar{f}
and

\bar{H}
, such that

\bar{H}=\operatorname{Id}+\bar{h},\qquad\bar{f}=A+\bar{R},

where
\bar{h},\bar{R}:\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
are

\mathbb{Z}^{N}
-periodic functions satisfying
\bar{h}(0)=0
and
\bar{R}(0)=0
.

The lifted conjugacy equation
A\circ\bar{H}=\bar{H}\circ\bar{f}
gives

A\bar{h}-\bar{h}\circ\bar{f}=\bar{R}.

This equation projects to the following equation on
\mathbb{T}^{N}
:

(3.8)

Ah-h\circ f=R,

where
h,R:\mathbb{T}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
satisfy
h(0)=0
and
R(0)=0
. Moreover,
R
is
C^{1+\alpha}
, while
h
is
C^{\beta}
continuous.

Let
p_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{N}\to E_{i}
denote the projection associated to the
splitting (
3.1
).
Set

h_{i}=p_{i}h,\qquad R_{i}=p_{i}R,\qquad A_{i}=A|_{E_{i}}.

Since the splitting is
A
-invariant, equation (
3.8
) decomposes into
the twisted cohomological equations

(3.9)

A_{i}h_{i}-h_{i}\circ f=R_{i},\qquad 1\leq i\leq\ell.

If
i\geq i_{0}
, we have

(3.10)

\displaystyle h_{i}=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}R_{i}\circ f^{m}.

4.
Rigidity for normalized cocycles

In this section, we prove an abstract cocycle rigidity criterion for a

Df
-invariant Hölder subbundle. We recall that
0
is a fixed point for
f
; see Section
3.4
.

Proposition 4.1
.

Suppose
\mathcal{L}
is a
Df
-invariant
\alpha
-Hölder subbundle over
\mathbb{T}^{N}
and
E
is a subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{N}
with
\dim E=\dim\mathcal{L}
. Let
\mathcal{A}:E\to E
be a linear automorphism. Suppose that there exist
\rho>0
and a
Riemannian metric
\|\cdot\|
on
\mathcal{L}
such that the following
conditions hold.

(1)

\mathcal{A}
is diagonalizable over
\mathbb{C}
and all eigenvalues of
\mathcal{A}
have modulus
\rho
;

(2)

At the fixed point
0
of
f
, we have

\displaystyle P_{E}Df_{0}|_{\mathcal{L}_{0}}=\mathcal{A}P_{E}|_{\mathcal{L}_{0}},

where
P_{E}
denotes the projection from
\mathbb{R}^{N}
to
E
. Moreover,
P_{E}:\mathcal{L}_{0}\to E
is an isomorphism.

(3)

For every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and every
x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
, we have

\bigl\|\rho^{-n}Df_{x}^{n}|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}\bigr\|\leq C.

(4)

There exists a sequence
k_{n}\to+\infty
such that:

(a)

\rho^{-k_{n}}\mathcal{A}^{k_{n}}\to I_{id}|_{E}
and
\rho^{k_{n}}\mathcal{A}^{-k_{n}}\to I_{id}|_{E}
as
n\to+\infty
;

(b)

for any
\epsilon>0
there is
l_{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{N}
such that: for any
k_{n}
with
n\geq l_{\epsilon}
and any
x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
with
f^{2k_{n}}x=x
we have

\displaystyle\Big\|\rho^{-k_{n}}Df^{k_{n}}_{f^{k_{n}}x}|_{\mathcal{L}_{f^{k_{n}}(x)}}-\rho^{k_{n}}Df^{-k_{n}}_{f^{k_{n}}x}|_{\mathcal{L}_{f^{k_{n}}(x)}}\Big\|\leq\epsilon.

Then the linear cocycle equation

(4.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(fx)\circ Df_{x}|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}=\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{K}(x),\qquad\forall\,x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}

has an
\alpha
-Hölder solution
\mathcal{K}
such that
\mathcal{K}(x):\mathcal{L}_{x}\to E
is an isomorphism for any
x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\lVert(\mathcal{K}(x))^{-1}u\rVert\leq C\lVert u\rVert,\qquad\forall\,x\in\mathbb{T}^{N},\,\,\forall\,u\in E.

In particular,
\mathcal{K}
is an
\alpha
-Hölder bundle isomorphism with

\alpha
-Hölder inverse.

4.1.
Role of Proposition
4.1

Proposition
4.1
will be used to prove Theorem
5.1
. More
precisely, Proposition
4.1
produces Hölder conjugacies for the
quotient cocycles. These quotient conjugacies are then assembled to obtain
the block upper-triangular reduction of
Df
over the Lyapunov block

\mathcal{E}_{i}
.

The hypotheses are formulated in terms of
uniform boundedness of the normalized cocycle and a periodic approximation
condition. In the proof of Theorem
5.1
, these hypotheses will be
verified for the quotient cocycles
\mathcal{F}_{i,j}/\mathcal{F}_{i,j-1}
,
which carry invariant conformal structures (see (
5
) of Section
3.1
).

4.2.
Proof strategy

We begin by summarizing the main steps in the proof. We first reduce the linear cocycle equation (
5.1
) to the
twisted cohomological equation (
4.8
). More precisely, we look for a
solution of (
5.1
) in the form

\mathcal{K}_{x}=P_{E}|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}+q(x),

which leads to the equation

\mathcal{A}\circ q(x)-q(fx)\circ Df_{x}|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}=\mathbf{r}(x).

At the fixed point
0
, condition (
2
) gives

\mathbf{r}(0)=0
. Hence the finite positive- and negative-time sums used to
define
q^{+}
and
q^{-}
vanish at
0
; see Remark
4.7
.

We then construct two one-sided solutions. The positive-time series
defines a solution
q^{+}
on the stable leaf
\mathcal{W}^{s,f}(0)
, while the
negative-time series defines a solution
q^{-}
on the unstable leaf

\mathcal{W}^{u,f}(0)
; see Section
4.4.3
. We also prove that
q^{+}
is
Hölder along
\mathcal{W}^{s,f}(0)
and that
q^{-}
is Hölder along

\mathcal{W}^{u,f}(0)
; see Section
4.4.4
.

The next step is to prove that the two one-sided solutions agree on the
homoclinic set

\mathcal{S}_{0}=\mathcal{W}^{s,f}(0)\cap\mathcal{W}^{u,f}(0).

This is where the periodic-data assumption is used, through the closing
argument and condition (
4
); see Section
4.4.5
. Once

q^{+}=q^{-}
on
\mathcal{S}_{0}
, we obtain a well-defined Hölder section

q
on
\mathcal{S}_{0}
; see Section
4.4.6
.

Since
\mathcal{S}_{0}
is dense in
\mathbb{T}^{N}
, the section
q
extends
uniquely to a Hölder section

\widetilde{q}\in C^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{N},\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{L},E)).

We then show that
\widetilde{q}
solves the twisted cohomological equation on
all of
\mathbb{T}^{N}
; see Section
4.4.7
.

Finally, setting

\mathcal{K}(x)=P_{E}|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}+\widetilde{q}(x),

we obtain a Hölder solution of the original linear cocycle equation. The
last step is to prove that
\mathcal{K}(x):\mathcal{L}_{x}\to E
is an
isomorphism for every
x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
, with uniformly bounded inverse; see Section
4.4.8
.

4.3.
Notation and basic facts

We list notation and basic facts that will be used in this section.

4.3.1.
Local charts for
\mathcal{L}

Since
\mathcal{L}
is an
\alpha
-Hölder subbundle of

T\mathbb{T}^{N}\cong\mathbb{T}^{N}\times\mathbb{R}^{N}
, we may choose a finite open cover

\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of
\mathbb{T}^{N}
and, on each
U_{i}
, an

\alpha
-Hölder local trivialization
\Theta_{i}(x):\mathbb{R}^{\text{rank}\mathcal{L}}\to\mathcal{L}_{x}
. For
x,y\in U_{i}
, define

\mathcal{I}_{x,y}:=\Theta_{i}(y)\circ\Theta_{i}(x)^{-1}:\mathcal{L}_{x}\to\mathcal{L}_{y}.

Then

(4.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{x,y}=\mathcal{I}_{y,x}^{-1},\quad\text{ and }\quad\lVert\mathcal{I}_{x,y}u-u\rVert\leq Cd(x,y)^{\alpha}\lVert u\rVert,\quad x,y\in U_{i},\quad u\in\mathcal{L}_{x}

where both fibers are viewed as subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{N}
.

After decreasing
\alpha>0
if necessary, we may assume that both

\mathcal{L}
and
Df
are
\alpha
-Hölder. Since
\mathcal{L}
is

Df
-invariant, the restricted cocycle
Df|_{\mathcal{L}}
is

\alpha
-Hölder with respect to the identifications
\mathcal{I}_{x,y}
. More precisely,
whenever
x,y
lie in a common chart and
fx,fy
lie in a common chart, we have

\left\|Df_{x}(u)-\mathcal{I}_{fy,fx}\circ Df_{y}\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,y}(u)\right\|\leq Cd(x,y)^{\alpha}\|u\|,\qquad u\in\mathcal{L}_{x}.

For simplicity, we write this as

\left\|Df_{x}|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}-Df_{y}|_{\mathcal{L}_{y}}\right\|\leq Cd(x,y)^{\alpha},

where the two maps are compared using the identifications

\mathcal{I}_{x,y}
and
\mathcal{I}_{fy,fx}
.

4.3.2.
Fiber bunched property of
\mathcal{L}

Let
\xi:\mathbb{T}^{N}\to GL(N,\mathbb{R})
be a map. The
GL(N,\mathbb{R})
-valued
cocycle over
f
generated by
\xi
is the map

\mathcal{B}:\mathbb{T}^{N}\times\mathbb{Z}\to GL(N,\mathbb{R})

defined by
\mathcal{B}(x,0)=\operatorname{Id}
and, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathcal{B}(x,n)=\xi(f^{n-1}x)\circ\cdots\circ\xi(x),\qquad\mathcal{B}(x,-n)=\mathcal{B}(f^{-n}x,n)^{-1}.

We say that a
\alpha
-Hölder cocycle
\mathcal{B}
over
f
is
fiber bunched
(see
[
38
]
) if
there exists
0<\theta<1
such that for all
x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\|\mathcal{B}^{n}_{x}\|\cdot\|(\mathcal{B}^{n}_{x})^{-1}\|\cdot\nu^{n\alpha}<C\theta^{n}\quad\text{and}\quad\|\mathcal{B}^{-n}_{x}\|\cdot\|(\mathcal{B}^{-n}_{x})^{-1}\|\cdot\nu^{n\alpha}<C\theta^{n},

where
\nu\in(0,1)
is the contraction constant fixed in Section
3.2
.

It follows from condition (
3
) of Proposition
4.1
that for any
n\geq 0

\displaystyle\lVert Df^{n}|_{\mathcal{L}}\rVert\cdot\lVert Df^{-n}|_{\mathcal{L}}\rVert\leq C.

Consequently, both
Df|_{\mathcal{L}}
and
Df^{-1}|_{\mathcal{L}}
are
fiber-bunched. This allows us to compare iterates of the cocycle along orbit
segments which remain exponentially close.

Proposition 4.2
.

[
23
, proposition 4.2]

There is
\delta>0
such that for any
0<\gamma<\delta
, any
m\in\mathbb{N}
and any
x,\,y\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
, if

\displaystyle d(f^{i}x,f^{i}y)\leq C\gamma\nu^{i},\qquad 0\leq i\leq m,

then we have

\displaystyle\big\|(Df^{m}_{y})^{-1}\circ\mathcal{I}_{f^{m}x,f^{m}y}\circ Df^{m}_{x}(u)-\mathcal{I}_{x,y}(u)\big\|\leq C\gamma^{\alpha}\lVert u\rVert

for any
u\in\mathcal{L}_{x}
.

Corollary 4.3
.

There is
\delta>0
such that for any
0<\gamma<\delta
, any
m\in\mathbb{N}
and any
x,\,y\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
, if

\displaystyle d(f^{j}x,f^{j}y)\leq C\gamma\nu^{m-j},\qquad\forall\,\,0\leq j\leq m,

then we have

\displaystyle\Big\|(Df^{-m}_{f^{m}(y)})^{-1}\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,y}\circ Df^{-m}_{f^{m}(x)}(u)-\mathcal{I}_{f^{m}(x),f^{m}(y)}(u)\Big\|\leq C\gamma^{\alpha}\lVert u\rVert

for any
u\in\mathcal{L}_{f^{m}(x)}
.

Proof.

Let
X=f^{m}x
and
Y=f^{m}y
. By assumption, for
0\leq j\leq m
,

\displaystyle d\big(f^{-j}X,f^{-j}Y\big)=d(f^{m-j}x,f^{m-j}y)\leq C\gamma\nu^{m-(m-j)}=C\gamma\nu^{j},\quad 0\leq j\leq m,

Thus the pair
X,Y
satisfies the hypothesis of Proposition
4.2
for
the inverse map
f^{-1}
. Applying Proposition
4.2
to the
fiber-bunched cocycle
Df^{-1}|_{\mathcal{L}}
, with
x,y
replaced by

X,Y
, gives the desired estimate.
∎

The following two lemmas are useful reformulations of
Proposition
4.2
and Corollary
4.3
for the normalized cocycles

\rho^{-m}Df^{m}|_{\mathcal{L}}
and
\rho^{m}Df^{-m}|_{\mathcal{L}}
.

Lemma 4.4
.

There is
\delta>0
such that for any
0<\gamma<\delta
, any
m\in\mathbb{N}
and any
x,\,y\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
, if

\displaystyle d(f^{i}x,f^{i}y)\leq C\gamma\nu^{i},\qquad 0\leq i\leq m,

then we have

(4.3)

\displaystyle\Big\|\mathcal{I}_{f^{m}(x),f^{m}(y)}\circ(\rho^{-m}Df^{m}_{x})(u)-(\rho^{-m}Df^{m}_{y})\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,y}(u)\Big\|\leq C\gamma^{\alpha}\lVert u\rVert

for any
u\in\mathcal{L}_{x}
; and

(4.4)

\displaystyle\Big\|(\rho^{m}Df^{-m}_{f^{m}(y)})\circ\mathcal{I}_{f^{m}(x),f^{m}(y)}(u)-\mathcal{I}_{x,y}\circ(\rho^{m}Df^{-m}_{f^{m}(x)})(u)\Big\|\leq C\gamma^{\alpha}\lVert u\rVert

for any
u\in\mathcal{L}_{f^{m}(x)}
.

Proof.

(
4.3
): For any
u\in\mathcal{L}_{x}
we have

\displaystyle\Big\|\mathcal{I}_{f^{m}(x),f^{m}(y)}\circ(\rho^{-m}Df^{m}_{x})(u)-(\rho^{-m}Df^{m}_{y})\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,y}(u)\Big\|

\displaystyle=\Big\|(\rho^{-m}Df^{m}_{y})\circ\big((Df^{m}_{y})^{-1}\circ\mathcal{I}_{f^{m}(x),f^{m}(y)}\circ Df^{m}_{x}(u)-\mathcal{I}_{x,y}(u)\big)\Big\|

\displaystyle\leq\big\|\rho^{-m}Df^{m}_{y}|_{\mathcal{L}_{y}}\big\|\cdot\Big\|(Df^{m}_{y})^{-1}\circ\mathcal{I}_{f^{m}(x),f^{m}(y)}\circ Df^{m}_{x}(u)-\mathcal{I}_{x,y}(u)\Big\|

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{\leq}C\cdot C\gamma^{\alpha}\lVert u\rVert=C_{1}\gamma^{\alpha}\lVert u\rVert.

Here in
(1)
we use condition (
3
) of Proposition
4.1
and Proposition
4.2
. Thus we get (
4.3
).

(
4.4
): Let

(4.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{I}_{f^{m}(x),f^{m}(y)}\circ(\rho^{-m}Df^{m}_{x})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}-(\rho^{-m}Df^{m}_{y})\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,y}.

It follows from (
4.3
) that

\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{Y}\rVert\leq C\gamma^{\alpha}.

Then we have

\displaystyle\Big\|(\rho^{m}Df^{-m}_{f^{m}(y)})\circ\mathcal{I}_{f^{m}(x),f^{m}(y)}(u)-\mathcal{I}_{x,y}\circ(\rho^{m}Df^{-m}_{f^{m}(x)})(u)\Big\|

\displaystyle=\Big\|(\rho^{m}Df^{-m}_{f^{m}(y)})\circ\mathcal{Y}\circ(\rho^{m}Df^{-m}_{f^{m}(x)})(u)\Big\|

\displaystyle\leq\big\|\rho^{m}Df^{-m}_{f^{m}(y)}|_{\mathcal{L}_{f^{m}(y)}}\big\|\cdot\big\|\mathcal{Y}\big\|\cdot\big\|(\rho^{m}Df^{-m}_{f^{m}(x)})(u)\big\|

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{\leq}C\cdot C\gamma^{\alpha}\cdot C\lVert u\rVert.

Here in
(1)
we use condition (
3
) of Proposition
4.1
and (
4.5
). Thus we get (
4.4
).

∎

Using Corollary
4.3
and condition (
3
) of Proposition
4.1
, the following lemma follows in the same way.

Lemma 4.5
.

There is
\delta>0
such that for any
0<\gamma<\delta
, any
m\in\mathbb{N}
and any
x,\,y\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
, if

\displaystyle d(f^{j}x,f^{j}y)\leq C\gamma\nu^{m-j},\qquad\forall\,\,0\leq j\leq m,

then we have

(4.6)

\displaystyle\Big\|\mathcal{I}_{x,y}\circ(\rho^{m}Df^{-m}_{f^{m}(x)})(u)-(\rho^{m}Df^{-m}_{f^{m}(y)})\circ\mathcal{I}_{f^{m}(x),f^{m}(y)}(u)\Big\|\leq C\gamma^{\alpha}\lVert u\rVert

for any
u\in\mathcal{L}_{f^{m}(x)}
; and

(4.7)

\displaystyle\Big\|(\rho^{-m}Df^{m}_{y})\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,y}(u)-\mathcal{I}_{f^{m}(x),f^{m}(y)}\circ(\rho^{-m}Df^{m}_{x})(u)\Big\|\leq C\gamma^{\alpha}\lVert u\rVert

for any
u\in\mathcal{L}_{x}
.

4.4.
Proof of Proposition
4.1

4.4.1.
Step 1: Reduction to a twisted cohomological equation

We look for a solution
\mathcal{K}_{x}:\mathcal{L}_{x}\to E
of
(
4.1
) in the form

\mathcal{K}_{x}=P_{E}|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}+q(x),

where
q(x)\in\text{Hom}(\mathcal{L}_{x},E)
. Let

\displaystyle\textbf{r}(x)=P_{E}\circ Df_{x}|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}-\mathcal{A}\circ P_{E}|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}},\qquad x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}.

Then the linear cocycle equation (
4.1
) is equivalent to the twisted cohomological equation:

(4.8)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}\circ q(x)-q(fx)\circ Df_{x}|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}=\textbf{r}(x),\qquad x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}.

Condition (
2
) of Proposition
4.1
gives that

(4.9)

\displaystyle\textbf{r}(0)=0.

Let

\displaystyle J(x)=-P_{E}|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}.

Since
\mathcal{K}(x)\equiv 0
is a trivial solution of equation (
4.1
),
J
solves

(4.10)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}\circ J(x)-J(fx)\circ Df|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}=\textbf{r}(x),\qquad x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}.

4.4.2.
Step 2: Preliminaries

In this step, we define series of linear maps and list some facts that will be frequently used later.
For any
m\geq 0
(resp.
n\leq-1
) define

(4.11)

\displaystyle q^{[m]}(x)=\sum_{j=0}^{m}\mathcal{A}^{-(j+1)}\circ\textbf{r}(f^{j}x)\circ Df^{j}|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}

(4.12)

\displaystyle\big(\text{resp. }

\displaystyle q^{[n]}(x)=-\sum_{j=n}^{-1}\mathcal{A}^{-(j+1)}\circ\textbf{r}(f^{j}x)\circ Df^{j}|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}\big)

Iterating (
4.10
), for any
m\geq 0
we have

(4.13)

\displaystyle q^{[m]}(x)=\mathcal{A}^{-(m+1)}\circ P_{E}\circ Df^{m+1}|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}+J(x),\quad\forall\,x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}.

Consequently, for any
m\geq 0
we have

\displaystyle\big\|q^{[m]}(x)\big\|

\displaystyle\leq\big\|\mathcal{A}^{-(m+1)}\circ P_{E}\circ Df^{m+1}|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}\big\|+\lVert J(x)\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\big\|(\rho^{m+1}\mathcal{A}^{-(m+1)})\big\|\cdot\big\|(\rho^{-(m+1)}Df^{m+1})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}\big\|+C

(4.14)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{\leq}C\cdot C+C

for any
x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
. Here in
(1)
we use conditions (
1
) and (
3
) of Proposition
4.1
.

Similarly, iterating (
4.10
) backwards for any
n\leq-1
we obtain

(4.15)

\displaystyle q^{[n]}(x)=\mathcal{A}^{-n}\circ P_{E}\circ Df^{n}|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}+J(x),\quad\forall\,x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}.

Similar to (
4.4.2
), for any
n\leq-1
we have

(4.16)

\displaystyle\big\|q^{[n]}(x)\big\|\leq C,\qquad\forall\,x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}.

From (
4.13
) and (
4.15
) for any
m\geq 0
and
n\leq-1
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{-(m+1)}\circ P_{E}\circ Df^{m+1}|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}-\mathcal{A}^{-n}\circ P_{E}\circ Df^{n}|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}

(4.17)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=n}^{m}\mathcal{A}^{-(j+1)}\circ\textbf{r}(f^{j}x)\circ Df^{j}|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}},

for any
x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
.

Since
0
is a fixed point of
f
and
\textbf{r}(0)=0
(see (
4.9
)), we have
q^{[k]}(0)=0
for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. From (
4.13
) and (
4.15
) we see that

(4.18)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{-k}\circ P_{E}\circ Df^{k}|_{\mathcal{L}_{0}}+J(0)=0,\qquad\forall\,k\in\mathbb{Z}.

Remark 4.6
.

Both definition (
4.11
) (resp. (
4.12
)) and the equivalent expression (
4.13
) (resp. (
4.15
)), will be used below.

4.4.3.
Step 3: Construction of the solutions
q^{+}
and
q^{-}

In this step, we show that, for every
x\in\mathcal{W}^{s,f}(0)
, the limit

(4.19)

q^{+}(x):=\lim_{n\to+\infty}q^{[n]}(x)\qquad\text{exists.}

Similarly, for every
x\in\mathcal{W}^{u,f}(0)
, the limit

(4.20)

q^{-}(x):=\lim_{n\to-\infty}q^{[n]}(x)\qquad\text{exists.}

Moreover,

(4.21)

\|q^{+}(x)\|\leq C,\qquad x\in\mathcal{W}^{s,f}(0),

and

(4.22)

\|q^{-}(x)\|\leq C,\qquad x\in\mathcal{W}^{u,f}(0).

Remark 4.7
.

Since
q^{[k]}(0)=0
for every
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, the limits defined in
(
4.19
) and (
4.20
) satisfy
q^{+}(0)=0
and
q^{-}(0)=0
.

Since
\textbf{r}(0)=0
, this value indeed satisfies the twisted equation
(
4.8
) at the fixed point
0
. The purpose of this step is to extend
this solution to the whole stable leaf
\mathcal{W}^{s,f}(0)
, respectively to
the whole unstable leaf
\mathcal{W}^{u,f}(0)
.

We first prove (
4.19
). Fix
x\in\mathcal{W}^{s,f}(0)
. First, we show that for any unit vector
v\in\mathcal{L}_{x}
and any
m_{1}>m_{2}\geq 1

(4.23)

\displaystyle\Big\|\sum_{j=m_{2}}^{m_{1}-1}\mathcal{A}^{-(j+1)}\circ\textbf{r}(f^{j}x)\circ Df^{j}_{x}(v)\Big\|\leq C\big\|q^{[m_{1}-m_{2}-1]}(f^{m_{2}}(x))\big\|.

To see this we rewrite

\displaystyle\sum_{j=m_{2}}^{m_{1}-1}\mathcal{A}^{-(j+1)}\circ\textbf{r}(f^{j}x)\circ Df^{j}_{x}(v)

\displaystyle=(\rho^{m_{2}}\mathcal{A}^{-m_{2}})\circ\big(\sum_{j=m_{2}}^{m_{1}-1}\mathcal{A}^{-(j-m_{2}+1)}\circ\textbf{r}(f^{j}x)\circ Df^{j-m_{2}}_{f^{m_{2}}(x)}\big)\circ\big(\rho^{-m_{2}}Df_{x}^{m_{2}}(v)\big)

(4.24)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{=}(\rho^{m_{2}}\mathcal{A}^{-m_{2}})\circ\big(q^{[m_{1}-m_{2}-1]}(f^{m_{2}}(x))\big)\circ\big(\rho^{-m_{2}}Df_{x}^{m_{2}}(v)\big).

Here in
(1)
we recall (
4.11
) and note that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=m_{2}}^{m_{1}-1}\mathcal{A}^{-(j-m_{2}+1)}\circ\textbf{r}(f^{j}x)\circ Df^{j-m_{2}}_{f^{m_{2}}(x)}|_{\mathcal{L}_{f^{m_{2}}(x)}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{m_{1}-m_{2}-1}\mathcal{A}^{-(j+1)}\circ\textbf{r}\big(f^{j}(f^{m_{2}}x)\big)\circ Df^{j}_{f^{m_{2}}x}|_{\mathcal{L}_{f^{m_{2}}(x)}}

\displaystyle=q^{[m_{1}-m_{2}-1]}(f^{m_{2}}(x)).

Then (
4.23
) follows from (
4.4.3
) and conditions (
1
) and (
3
) of Proposition
4.1
.

By (
4.11
), from (
4.23
) we see that to prove (
4.19
), it suffices to show that

\displaystyle\big\|q^{[m_{1}-m_{2}-1]}(f^{m_{2}}(x))\big\|

is arbitrarily small whenever
m_{2}
is sufficiently large, uniformly in

m_{1}>m_{2}
.

By (
4.13
) we have

\displaystyle\left\|q^{[m_{1}-m_{2}-1]}(f^{m_{2}}x)\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\mathcal{A}^{-(m_{1}-m_{2})}\circ P_{E}\circ Df_{f^{m_{2}}x}^{m_{1}-m_{2}}|_{\mathcal{L}_{f^{m_{2}}x}}+J(f^{m_{2}}x)\right\|

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{=}\left\|\left(\mathcal{A}^{-(m_{1}-m_{2})}\circ P_{E}\circ Df_{f^{m_{2}}x}^{m_{1}-m_{2}}|_{\mathcal{L}_{f^{m_{2}}x}}+J(f^{m_{2}}x)\right)\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\left.-\left(\mathcal{A}^{-(m_{1}-m_{2})}\circ P_{E}\circ Df_{0}^{m_{1}-m_{2}}|_{\mathcal{L}_{0}}+J(0)\right)\circ\mathcal{I}_{f^{m_{2}}x,0}\right\|

(4.25)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(2)}}{\leq}\|\mathcal{Y}\|+\left\|J(f^{m_{2}}x)-J(0)\circ\mathcal{I}_{f^{m_{2}}x,0}\right\|,

Here in
(1)
we use (
4.18
); in
(2)
we set

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}:={}

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{-(m_{1}-m_{2})}\circ P_{E}\circ Df_{f^{m_{2}}x}^{m_{1}-m_{2}}|_{\mathcal{L}_{f^{m_{2}}x}}-\mathcal{A}^{-(m_{1}-m_{2})}\circ P_{E}\circ Df_{0}^{m_{1}-m_{2}}\circ\mathcal{I}_{f^{m_{2}}x,0}.

We first estimate the second term in (
4.4.3
). We recall local identifications between fibers in Section
4.3.1
. Suppose
x\in\mathcal{W}^{s,f}(0)
. For any
\epsilon>0
there is
l=l(x,\epsilon)\in\mathbb{N}
such that

(4.26)

\displaystyle d(f^{p+n}(x),0)<\epsilon\nu^{n},\qquad p\geq l,\quad n\geq 0,

(see (
3.6
) of Section
3.2
). Suppose
m_{1}>m_{2}\geq l
. We have

(4.27)

\displaystyle\big\|J(f^{m_{2}}(x))-J(0)\circ(\mathcal{I}_{f^{m_{2}}(x),0})\big\|\overset{\text{(1)}}{\leq}Cd(f^{m_{2}}(x),0)^{\alpha}\overset{\text{(2)}}{<}C\epsilon^{\alpha}.

Here in
(1)
we use the fact that
\mathcal{L}
is
\alpha
-Hölder and thus
J
is
\alpha
-Hölder along
\mathcal{L}
, see Section
4.3.1
; in
(2)
we use (
4.26
) by letting
p=m_{2}
and
n=0
.

Next, we estimate
\big\|\mathcal{Y}\big\|
. Let
s=m_{1}-m_{2}
. We rewrite

\mathcal{Y}
as

\mathcal{Y}=(\rho^{s}\mathcal{A}^{-s})\circ P_{E}\circ(\mathcal{Y}_{1}+\mathcal{Y}_{2}),

where

\mathcal{Y}_{1}=(I_{id}-\mathcal{I}_{f^{m_{1}}x,0})\circ\left(\rho^{-s}Df_{f^{m_{2}}x}^{s}|_{\mathcal{L}_{f^{m_{2}}x}}\right)

and

\mathcal{Y}_{2}=\mathcal{I}_{f^{m_{1}}x,0}\circ\left(\rho^{-s}Df_{f^{m_{2}}x}^{s}|_{\mathcal{L}_{f^{m_{2}}x}}\right)-\left(\rho^{-s}Df_{0}^{s}\right)\circ\mathcal{I}_{f^{m_{2}}x,0}.

From (
4.26
) we have

\displaystyle d\big(f^{n}(f^{m_{2}}x),f^{n}(0)\big)=d(f^{m_{2}+n}(x),0)<\epsilon\nu^{n},\qquad\forall\,n\geq 0.

Next, we estimate
\|\mathcal{Y}_{2}\|
. Taking
m_{2}
sufficiently large, we may apply (
4.3
) of Lemma
4.4
with

f^{m_{2}}x
and
0
to estimate
\mathcal{Y}_{2}
. Thus we have:

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{Y}_{2}\|\leq C\epsilon^{\alpha}.

Now we estimate
\|\mathcal{Y}_{1}\|
.

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{Y}_{1}\|\leq\lVert I_{id}-\mathcal{I}_{f^{m_{1}}x,0}\rVert\cdot\|\rho^{-s}Df_{f^{m_{2}}x}^{s}\|\overset{\text{(1)}}{\leq}Cd(f^{m_{1}}x,0)^{\alpha}\cdot C\overset{\text{(2)}}{\leq}C_{1}\epsilon^{\alpha}.

Here in
(1)
we use (
4.2
) and condition (
3
) of Proposition
4.1
; in
(2)
we use (
4.26
) by letting
p=m_{1}
and
n=0
.

As a consequence, we have

(4.28)

\displaystyle\big\|\mathcal{Y}\big\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\rho^{s}\mathcal{A}^{-s}\|\|P_{E}\|(\|\mathcal{Y}_{1}\|+\|\mathcal{Y}_{2}\|)\overset{\text{(1)}}{\leq}C(\lVert\mathcal{Y}_{1}\rVert+\lVert\mathcal{Y}_{2}\rVert)\leq C_{1}\epsilon^{\alpha}.

Here in
(1)
we use condition (
1
) of Proposition
4.1
.

From (
4.4.3
), (
4.27
) and (
4.28
), we have

(4.29)

\displaystyle\big\|q^{[m_{1}-m_{2}-1]}(f^{m_{2}}(x))\big\|\leq C\epsilon^{\alpha}.

This together with (
4.23
) gives

\displaystyle\Big\|\sum_{j=m_{2}}^{m_{1}-1}\mathcal{A}^{-(j+1)}|_{E}\circ\textbf{r}(f^{j}x)\circ Df^{j}_{x}|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}\Big\|\leq C\epsilon^{\alpha}.

This implies (
4.19
).

The proof of (
4.20
) is similar. Finally, (
4.21
) follows
directly from (
4.19
) and (
4.4.2
), while (
4.22
) follows
from (
4.20
) and (
4.16
).

4.4.4.
Step 4: Hölder regularity of
q^{+}(x)
and
q^{-}(x)

In this step, we show that there exists
\delta_{0}>0
such that, for any

x,y\in\mathcal{W}^{s,f}(0)
with
d(x,y)<\delta_{0}
, we have

(4.30)

\displaystyle\|q^{+}(x)-q^{+}(y)\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,y}\|\leq Cd(x,y)^{\alpha}.

Similarly, for any
x,y\in\mathcal{W}^{u,f}(0)
with
d(x,y)<\delta_{0}
, we have

(4.31)

\displaystyle\|q^{-}(x)-q^{-}(y)\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,y}\|\leq Cd(x,y)^{\alpha}.

We first prove (
4.30
). Let
x,y\in\mathcal{W}^{s,f}(0)
with

d(x,y)
sufficiently small.
For any
m>0
we have

\displaystyle\big\|q^{[m-1]}(x)-q^{[m-1]}(y)\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,y}\big\|

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{=}\Big\|(\rho^{m}\mathcal{A}^{-m})\circ P_{E}\circ(\rho^{-m}Df^{m}_{x})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}+J(x)

\displaystyle-\big((\rho^{m}\mathcal{A}^{-m})\circ P_{E}\circ(\rho^{-m}Df^{m}_{y})\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,y}+J(y)\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,y}\big)\Big\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\rho^{m}\mathcal{A}^{-m}\|\cdot\big\|\mathcal{Y}\big\|+\big\|J(x)-J(y)\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,y}\big\|

(4.32)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(2)}}{\leq}C\big\|\mathcal{Y}\big\|+Cd(x,y)^{\alpha},

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}=P_{E}\circ(\rho^{-m}Df^{m}_{x})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}-P_{E}\circ(\rho^{-m}Df^{m}_{y})\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,y}.

Here in
(1)
we use (
4.13
); in
(2)
we use condition (
1
) of Proposition
4.1
and
the
\alpha
-
Hölder
of
J
along
\mathcal{L}
.

Next, we estimate
\big\|\mathcal{Y}\big\|
. We rewrite

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}

\displaystyle=P_{E}\circ(\rho^{-m}Df^{m}_{x})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}-P_{E}\circ\mathcal{I}_{f^{m}(x),f^{m}(y),\mathcal{L}}\circ(\rho^{-m}Df^{m}_{x})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}

\displaystyle+P_{E}\circ\mathcal{I}_{f^{m}(x),f^{m}(y),\mathcal{L}}\circ(\rho^{-m}Df^{m}_{x})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}-P_{E}\circ(\rho^{-m}Df^{m}_{y})\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,y}.

For the first part, we have

\displaystyle\big\|P_{E}\circ(\rho^{-m}Df^{m}_{x})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}-P_{E}\circ\mathcal{I}_{f^{m}(x),f^{m}(y)}\circ(\rho^{-m}Df^{m}_{x})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}\big\|

\displaystyle\leq C\big\|\mathcal{I}_{f^{m}(x),f^{m}(y)}-I_{id}\big\|\cdot\big\|(\rho^{-m}Df^{m})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}\big\|

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{\leq}Cd(f^{m}(x),f^{m}(y))^{\alpha}\cdot C

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(2)}}{\leq}C_{1}\nu^{\alpha m}d(x,y)^{\alpha}.

Here in
(1)
we use (
4.2
) and condition (
3
) of Proposition
4.1
; in
(2)
we use the contraction along stable leaves, see (
3.6
) of Section
3.2
.

Now we estimate the second part. By the contraction along stable leaves, for

d(x,y)
sufficiently small, apply (
4.3
) of Lemma
4.4
we have

\displaystyle\big\|\mathcal{I}_{f^{m}(x),f^{m}(y)}\circ(\rho^{-m}Df^{m}_{x})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}-(\rho^{-m}Df^{m}_{y})\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,y}\big\|\leq Cd(x,y)^{\alpha}.

Hence, it follows that

\displaystyle\big\|P_{E}\circ\mathcal{I}_{f^{m}(x),f^{m}(y)}\circ(\rho^{-m}Df^{m}_{x})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}-P_{E}\circ(\rho^{-m}Df^{m}_{y})\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,y}\big\|\leq Cd(x,y)^{\alpha}.

Hence, we have

(4.33)

\displaystyle\big\|\mathcal{Y}\big\|\leq C_{1}\nu^{\alpha m}d(x,y)^{\alpha}+Cd(x,y)^{\alpha}\leq C_{2}d(x,y)^{\alpha}.

(
4.33
) and (
4.4.4
) give

\displaystyle\big\|q^{[m-1]}(x)-q^{[m-1]}(y)\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,y}\big\|\leq Cd(x,y)^{\alpha}

for any
m>0
. Let
m\to\infty
. It follows from (
4.19
) that

\displaystyle\big\|q^{+}(x)-q^{+}(y)\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,y}\big\|\leq Cd(x,y)^{\alpha}.

Hence we get (
4.30
).

We now prove the unstable estimate (
4.31
). Let

x,y\in\mathcal{W}^{u,f}(0)
with
d(x,y)
sufficiently small. By using (
4.15
), similar to (
4.4.4
), for any
m>0
we have

\displaystyle\big\|q^{[-m]}(x)-q^{[-m]}(y)\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,y}\big\|

\displaystyle=\Big\|(\rho^{-m}\mathcal{A}^{m})\circ P_{E}\circ(\rho^{m}Df^{-m}_{x})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}+J(x)

\displaystyle-\big((\rho^{-m}\mathcal{A}^{m})\circ P_{E}\circ(\rho^{m}Df^{-m}_{y})\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,y}+J(y)\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,y}\big)\Big\|

(4.34)

\displaystyle\leq C\big\|\mathcal{Y}_{1}\big\|+Cd(x,y)^{\alpha},

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{1}=P_{E}\circ(\rho^{m}Df^{-m}_{x})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}-P_{E}\circ(\rho^{m}Df^{-m}_{y})\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,y}.

Since
x,y
lie on the same unstable leaf, backward iterates contract. Thus,
for
d(x,y)
sufficiently small, we have

\displaystyle d\big(f^{j}(f^{-m}x),

\displaystyle\,f^{j}(f^{-m}y)\big)=d(f^{j-m}x,\,f^{j-m}y)\leq C\nu^{m-j}d(x,y).

Applying (
4.6
) of Lemma
4.5
to the pair

f^{-m}x,f^{-m}y
, we have

(4.35)

\displaystyle\Big\|\mathcal{I}_{f^{-m}(x),f^{-m}(y)}\circ(\rho^{m}Df^{-m}_{x})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}-(\rho^{m}Df^{-m}_{y})\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,y}\Big\|\leq Cd(x,y)^{\alpha}.

By using (
4.35
), similar to (
4.33
), we can show that

\displaystyle\big\|\mathcal{Y}_{1}\big\|\leq Cd(x,y)^{\alpha}.

This, together with (
4.4.4
), gives

\displaystyle\big\|q^{[-m]}(x)-q^{[-m]}(y)\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,y}\big\|\leq Cd(x,y)^{\alpha}

for any
m>0
. Let
m\to\infty
. It follows from (
4.20
) that

\displaystyle\big\|q^{-}(x)-q^{-}(y)\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,y}\big\|\leq Cd(x,y)^{\alpha}.

Hence we get (
4.31
). Taking
\delta_{0}
to be the minimum of the local
constants required above completes the proof.

4.4.5.
Step 5: Coincidence of
q^{+}
and
q^{-}
on
\mathcal{W}^{s,f}(0)\bigcap\mathcal{W}^{u,f}(0)

Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{0}=\mathcal{W}^{s,f}(0)\bigcap\mathcal{W}^{u,f}(0).

In this step, we show that

(4.36)

\displaystyle q^{+}(x)=q^{-}(x),\qquad\forall\,x\in\mathcal{S}_{0}.

Recall the sequence
k_{n}\to+\infty
from condition (
4
) of Proposition
4.1
. We first prove
that, for every
x\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
,

(4.37)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\big(q^{[k_{n}-1]}(x)-q^{[-k_{n}]}(x)\big)=0.

Since
x\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
, the limits defining
q^{+}(x)
and
q^{-}(x)
both
exist. Hence (
4.36
) follows directly from (
4.37
).

Fix
x\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
. The key step in proving (
4.37
) is the following: for any
\epsilon>0
, there is
\tau_{x,\epsilon}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
k_{n}\geq\tau_{x,\epsilon}
:

(4.38)

\displaystyle\big\|P_{E}\circ(\rho^{-k_{n}}Df^{k_{n}})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}-P_{E}\circ(\rho^{k_{n}}Df^{-k_{n}})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}\big\|\leq C\epsilon^{\alpha}.

As a direct consequence of (
4.38
) we have

(4.39)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\big\|P_{E}\circ(\rho^{-k_{n}}Df^{k_{n}})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}-P_{E}\circ(\rho^{k_{n}}Df^{-k_{n}})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}\big\|=0.

Before we proceed to the proof of (
4.38
), we show how it implies (
4.37
).

For any
x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
, we have

\displaystyle q^{[k_{n}-1]}(x)-q^{[-k_{n}]}(x)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{=}(\rho^{k_{n}}\mathcal{A}^{-k_{n}})\circ P_{E}\circ(\rho^{-k_{n}}Df_{x}^{k_{n}})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}

\displaystyle\quad-(\rho^{-k_{n}}\mathcal{A}^{k_{n}})\circ P_{E}\circ(\rho^{k_{n}}Df_{x}^{-k_{n}})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}

(4.40)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(2)}}{=}\mathcal{Y}_{1}+\mathcal{Y}_{2}+\mathcal{Y}_{3},

Here in
(1)
we use (
4.13
) and (
4.15
); in
(2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{1}

\displaystyle=(\rho^{k_{n}}\mathcal{A}^{-k_{n}})\circ P_{E}\circ(\rho^{-k_{n}}Df^{k_{n}})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}-P_{E}\circ(\rho^{-k_{n}}Df^{k_{n}})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}},

\displaystyle=(\rho^{k_{n}}\mathcal{A}^{-k_{n}}-I_{id}|_{E})\circ P_{E}\circ(\rho^{-k_{n}}Df^{k_{n}})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{2}

\displaystyle=P_{E}\circ(\rho^{-k_{n}}Df^{k_{n}})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}-P_{E}\circ(\rho^{k_{n}}Df^{-k_{n}})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{3}

\displaystyle=P_{E}\circ(\rho^{k_{n}}Df^{-k_{n}})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}-(\rho^{-k_{n}}\mathcal{A}^{k_{n}})\circ P_{E}\circ(\rho^{k_{n}}Df^{-k_{n}})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}

\displaystyle=(I_{id}|_{E}-\rho^{-k_{n}}\mathcal{A}^{k_{n}})\circ P_{E}\circ(\rho^{k_{n}}Df^{-k_{n}})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}.

By using condition (
3
) of Proposition
4.1
we have

\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{Y}_{1}\rVert\leq C\big\|\rho^{k_{n}}\mathcal{A}^{-k_{n}}-I_{id}|_{E}\big\|,\quad\lVert\mathcal{Y}_{3}\rVert\leq C\big\|I_{id}|_{E}-\rho^{-k_{n}}\mathcal{A}^{k_{n}}\big\|.

By condition (
4a
) of Proposition
4.1
, these two quantities tend to zero. Together with (
4.39
), this proves (
4.37
).

It remains to prove (
4.38
). Since
x\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
, for any
\epsilon>0
there is
\tau_{x,\epsilon}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\displaystyle d(f^{m}(x),f^{-m}(x))\leq\epsilon,\qquad\forall\,m\geq\tau_{x,\epsilon}.

Assume
\epsilon>0
is small enough so that the Anosov Closing lemma (see Theorem
3.1
) applies.
For any
k_{n}\geq\tau_{x,\epsilon}
, since

\displaystyle d(f^{k_{n}}(x),f^{-k_{n}}(x))\leq\epsilon,

there is
y\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
satisfying
y=f^{2k_{n}}(y)
and

\displaystyle d(f^{-k_{n}+j}(x),\,f^{j}(y))

\displaystyle=d\big(f^{j}(f^{-k_{n}}x),f^{j}(y)\big)\leq C\nu^{\min\{j,2k_{n}-j\}}d(f^{k_{n}}(x),f^{-k_{n}}(x))

(4.41)

\displaystyle\leq C\nu^{\min\{j,2k_{n}-j\}}\epsilon,

for any
0\leq j\leq 2k_{n}
.

Increasing
\tau_{x,\epsilon}
if necessary, we may assume

k_{n}\geq l_{\epsilon}
(see condition (
4b
) of Proposition
4.1
). Applying (
4b
) to the periodic point
y
, we obtain

(4.42)

\displaystyle\Big\|(\rho^{-k_{n}}Df^{k_{n}})|_{\mathcal{L}_{f^{k_{n}}(y)}}-(\rho^{k_{n}}Df^{-k_{n}})|_{\mathcal{L}_{f^{k_{n}}(y)}}\Big\|\leq\epsilon.

Moreover, taking
j=0
and
j=2k_{n}
in (
4.4.5
), we get

(4.43)

\displaystyle d(f^{-k_{n}}(x),\,y)\leq C\epsilon\quad\text{and}\quad d(f^{k_{n}}(x),\,y)\leq C\epsilon.

We now estimate

\displaystyle P_{E}\circ(\rho^{-k_{n}}Df^{k_{n}})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}-P_{E}\circ(\rho^{k_{n}}Df^{-k_{n}})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}=\mathcal{X}_{1}+\mathcal{Y}+\mathcal{X}_{2},

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{1}=P_{E}\circ(I_{id}-\mathcal{I}_{f^{k_{n}}(x),y})\circ(\rho^{-k_{n}}Df^{k_{n}}_{x})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}=P_{E}\circ\mathcal{I}_{f^{k_{n}}(x),y}\circ(\rho^{-k_{n}}Df^{k_{n}}_{x})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}-P_{E}\circ\mathcal{I}_{f^{-k_{n}}(x),y}\circ(\rho^{k_{n}}Df^{-k_{n}}_{x})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{2}=P_{E}\circ(\mathcal{I}_{f^{-k_{n}}(x),y}-I_{id})\circ(\rho^{k_{n}}Df^{-k_{n}}_{x})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}.

By using condition (
3
) of Proposition, we have

\displaystyle\max\{\lVert\mathcal{X}_{1}\rVert,\,\lVert\mathcal{X}_{2}\rVert\}

\displaystyle\leq C\max\{\lVert I_{id}-\mathcal{I}_{f^{k_{n}}(x),y}\rVert,\,\lVert\mathcal{I}_{f^{-k_{n}}(x),y}-I_{id}\rVert\}

(4.44)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{\leq}C_{1}d(f^{k_{n}}(x),y)^{\alpha}+C_{1}d(f^{-k_{n}}(x),y)^{\alpha}\overset{\text{(2)}}{\leq}C_{2}\epsilon^{\alpha}.

Here in
(1)
we use (
4.2
); in
(2)
we use (
4.43
).

It remains to estimate
\mathcal{Y}
. Taking
j=k_{n}
in (
4.4.5
), we get

\displaystyle d(x,\,f^{k_{n}}(y))\leq C\nu^{k_{n}}\epsilon.

Thus, the closeness of
x
and
f^{k_{n}}(y)
allows us to rewrite
\mathcal{Y}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}=P_{E}\mathcal{Y}_{1}+P_{E}\mathcal{Y}_{2}+P_{E}\mathcal{Y}_{3},

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}_{f^{k_{n}}(x),y}\circ(\rho^{-k_{n}}Df^{k_{n}}_{x})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}-(\rho^{-k_{n}}Df^{k_{n}}_{f^{k_{n}}(y)})\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,f^{k_{n}}(y)},

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{2}

\displaystyle=(\rho^{-k_{n}}Df^{k_{n}}_{f^{k_{n}}(y)})\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,f^{k_{n}}(y)}-(\rho^{k_{n}}Df^{-k_{n}}_{f^{k_{n}}(y)})\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,f^{k_{n}}(y)},

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{3}

\displaystyle=(\rho^{k_{n}}Df^{-k_{n}}_{f^{k_{n}}(y)})\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,f^{k_{n}}(y)}-\mathcal{I}_{f^{-k_{n}}(x),y}\circ(\rho^{k_{n}}Df^{-k_{n}}_{x})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}.

First, by (
4.42
) and the uniform boundedness of the identifications:

\displaystyle\big\|\mathcal{Y}_{2}\big\|

\displaystyle\leq\Big\|(\rho^{-k_{n}})Df^{k_{n}}_{f^{k_{n}}(y)}|_{\mathcal{L}_{f^{k_{n}}(y)}}-(\rho^{k_{n}}Df^{-k_{n}}_{f^{k_{n}}(y)})|_{\mathcal{L}_{f^{k_{n}}(y)}}\Big\|\cdot\big\|\mathcal{I}_{x,f^{k_{n}}(y)}\big\|\leq C\epsilon.

Next, (
4.4.5
) shows that for any
0\leq j\leq k_{n}
, we have

\displaystyle d\big(f^{j}(x)

\displaystyle,f^{j}(f^{k_{n}}y)\big)=d\big(f^{-k_{n}+(j+k_{n})}(x),f^{j+k_{n}}(y)\big)

\displaystyle\leq C\nu^{\min\{j+k_{n},2k_{n}-(j+k_{n})\}}\epsilon=C\nu^{k_{n}-j}\epsilon.

Thus the pair
x,f^{k_{n}}y
satisfies the hypothesis of Lemma
4.5
.
Using (
4.7
), we obtain

\displaystyle\big\|\mathcal{Y}_{1}\big\|\leq C(C\epsilon)^{\alpha}.

Similarly, (
4.4.5
) shows that for any
0\leq j\leq k_{n}
, we have

\displaystyle d(f^{j}(f^{-k_{n}}(x)),f^{j}(y))=d(f^{-k_{n}+j}(x),f^{j}(y))

\displaystyle\leq C\nu^{j}\epsilon.

Thus the pair
f^{-k_{n}}x,y
satisfies the hypothesis of Lemma
4.4
.
Using (
4.4
), we obtain

\displaystyle\big\|\mathcal{Y}_{3}\big\|\leq C(C\epsilon)^{\alpha}.

Hence, for
\epsilon>0
sufficiently small,

\displaystyle\big\|\mathcal{Y}\big\|\leq C(C\epsilon)^{\alpha}+C\epsilon+C(C\epsilon)^{\alpha}\leq C_{1}\epsilon^{\alpha}.

This, together with (
4.4.5
), gives

\displaystyle\big\|P_{E}\circ(\rho^{-k_{n}}Df^{k_{n}})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}-P_{E}\circ(\rho^{k_{n}}Df^{-k_{n}})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}\big\|\leq C\epsilon^{\alpha}.

This implies (
4.38
).

4.4.6.
Step 6: Hölder regularity of
q(x)
on
\mathcal{S}_{0}

Let

(4.45)

\displaystyle q(x)=q^{+}(x)=q^{-}(x),\qquad\forall\,x\in\mathcal{S}_{0}.

In this step, we show that:

(1)

on
\mathcal{S}_{0}
we have
\lVert q(x)\rVert\leq C
;

(2)

there is
\delta_{0,1}>0
such that for any
x,\,y\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
, if
d(x,y)<\delta_{0,1}
, then

\displaystyle\big\|q(x)-q(y)\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,y}\big\|\leq Cd(x,y)^{\alpha}.

The bound in (
1
) follows directly from (
4.21
). We now
prove (
2
). Choose
\delta_{0,1}>0
sufficiently small so
that the following two conditions hold. First, the local product structure
applies at scale
\delta_{0,1}
(see (
3.7
) of Section
3.2
). Second, whenever

d(x,y)<\delta_{0,1}
and

\displaystyle z\in\mathcal{W}^{s,f}_{\mathrm{loc}}(x)\cap\mathcal{W}^{u,f}_{\mathrm{loc}}(y)

is the local product point, the three points
x,y,z
lie in a common
trivializing chart for
\mathcal{L}
. In particular,

(4.46)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{x,z}=\mathcal{I}_{y,z}\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,y}.

Let
x,y\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
with
d(x,y)<\delta_{0,1}
, and let

\displaystyle z\in\mathcal{W}^{s,f}_{\mathrm{loc}}(x)\cap\mathcal{W}^{u,f}_{\mathrm{loc}}(y)

be the local product point. Since
x\in\mathcal{W}^{s,f}(0)
and

z\in\mathcal{W}^{s,f}_{\mathrm{loc}}(x)
, we have

z\in\mathcal{W}^{s,f}(0)
. Similarly, since

y\in\mathcal{W}^{u,f}(0)
and

z\in\mathcal{W}^{u,f}_{\mathrm{loc}}(y)
, we have

z\in\mathcal{W}^{u,f}(0)
. Hence
z\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
.

By the local product estimates,

d(x,z)+d(y,z)\leq Cd(x,y).

Using the stable Hölder regularity of
q^{+}
and the unstable Hölder
regularity of
q^{-}
from Step 4, we obtain

\displaystyle\|q(x)

\displaystyle-q(y)\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,y}\|\leq\|q(x)-q(z)\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,z}\|+\|q(z)\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,z}-q(y)\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,y}\|

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(a)}}{=}\|q(x)-q(z)\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,z}\|+\|(q(z)\circ\mathcal{I}_{y,z}-q(y))\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,y}\|

\displaystyle\leq\|q(x)-q(z)\circ\mathcal{I}_{x,z}\|+C\|q(z)\circ\mathcal{I}_{y,z}-q(y)\|

\displaystyle\leq Cd(x,z)^{\alpha}+C_{1}d(z,y)^{\alpha}

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}d(x,y)^{\alpha}.

Here in
(a)
we use (
4.46
). This proves (
2
).

Remark 4.8
.

Since
A
is transitive,
f
is also transitive. Hence
\mathcal{S}_{0}
is dense in
\mathbb{T}^{N}
, see
[
4
]
. Then (
2
), together with the usual extension
argument in local trivializations implies that
q
extends uniquely to an

\alpha
-
Hölder
section

\widetilde{q}\in C^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{N},\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{L},E)).

Since
0\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
, Remark
4.7
gives
\tilde{q}(0)=0
.

4.4.7.
Step 7: Solvability of (
4.8
)

In this step, we show that
\tilde{q}
solves (
4.8
), i.e.,

(4.47)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}\circ\tilde{q}(x)-\tilde{q}(fx)\circ Df|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}=\textbf{r}(x),\qquad\forall\,x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}.

As
\tilde{q}
is Hölder on
\mathbb{T}^{N}
and
\mathcal{S}_{0}
is dense in
\mathbb{T}^{N}
, to show that (
4.47
) holds, it suffices to show that

\tilde{q}
solves (
4.8
) on
\mathcal{S}_{0}
, i.e.,

(4.48)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}\circ\tilde{q}(x)-\tilde{q}(fx)\circ Df|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}=\textbf{r}(x),\qquad\forall\,x\in\mathcal{S}_{0}.

Fix
x\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
. Since
\mathcal{S}_{0}
is
f
-invariant, we have

fx\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
. Hence

\widetilde{q}(x)=q(x),\qquad\widetilde{q}(fx)=q(fx).

Using the definition of
q
on
