\mathcal{S}_{0}
and the convergence

q^{[n]}\to q^{+}
on stable leaves, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}\circ q(x)-q(fx)\circ Df|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{=}\lim_{n}\big(\mathcal{A}\circ q^{[k_{n}-1]}(x)-q^{[k_{n}-1]}(fx)\circ Df|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}\big)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(2)}}{=}\lim_{n}\big(\sum_{j=0}^{k_{n}-1}\mathcal{A}^{-j}\circ\mathbf{r}(f^{j}x)\circ Df_{x}^{j}|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}-\sum_{j=0}^{k_{n}-1}\mathcal{A}^{-(j+1)}\circ\mathbf{r}(f^{j+1}x)\circ Df_{x}^{j+1}|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}\big)

(4.49)

\displaystyle=\textbf{r}(x)-\lim_{n}\mathcal{A}^{-k_{n}}\circ\textbf{r}(f^{k_{n}}x)\circ Df^{k_{n}}|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}.

Here in
(1)
we use (
4.19
); in
(2)
we recall definition (
4.11
) of
q^{[m]}
and the resulting telescoping identity.

Next, we show that

(4.50)

\displaystyle\lim_{n}\mathcal{A}^{-k_{n}}\circ\textbf{r}(f^{k_{n}}x)\circ Df^{k_{n}}|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}=0,\qquad\forall\,x\in\mathcal{S}_{0}.

In fact, we have

\displaystyle\big\|\mathcal{A}^{-k_{n}}\circ\textbf{r}(f^{k_{n}}x)\circ Df^{k_{n}}|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}\big\|

\displaystyle=\big\|(\rho^{k_{n}}\mathcal{A}^{-k_{n}})\circ\textbf{r}(f^{k_{n}}x)\circ(\rho^{-k_{n}}Df^{k_{n}})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}\big\|

\displaystyle\leq\big\|(\rho^{k_{n}}\mathcal{A}^{-k_{n}})\big\|\cdot\big\|\textbf{r}(f^{k_{n}}x)\big\|\cdot\big\|(\rho^{-k_{n}}Df^{k_{n}})|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}\big\|

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{\leq}C\cdot\big\|\textbf{r}(f^{k_{n}}x)\big\|\overset{\text{(2)}}{\rightarrow}C\cdot\lVert\textbf{r}(0)\rVert=0,\qquad\text{as }n\to\infty.

Here in
(1)
we use conditions (
1
) and (
3
) of Proposition
4.1
; in
(2)
we use
f^{k_{n}}x\to 0
as
n\to\infty
and
recall (
4.9
).

Hence we get (
4.50
). This, together with (
4.4.7
), implies (
4.48
).
Hence we proved (
4.47
).

4.4.8.
Step 8: Invertibility of
\mathcal{K}(x)

In this step, we show that

(4.51)

\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{K}(x)u\rVert\geq C^{-1}\lVert u\rVert,\qquad\forall\,x\in\mathbb{T}^{N},\,\,\forall\,u\in\mathcal{L}_{x}.

Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(x)=P_{E}|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}+\tilde{q}(x)\qquad\forall\,x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}.

From discussion in Section
4.4.1
, we see that
\mathcal{K}
solves (
4.1
). Since
\tilde{q}(0)=0
(see Remark
4.8
),

\mathcal{K}(0):\,\mathcal{L}_{0}\to E
is injective (see (
2
) of Proposition
4.1
). By continuity of
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{L}
, there is a neighbourhood
U
of
0
such that

(4.52)

\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{K}(x)u\rVert\geq C^{-1}\lVert u\rVert,\qquad\forall\,x\in U,\quad\forall\,u\in\mathcal{L}_{x}.

We now propagate this estimate along forward iterates. From (
4.1
), we have

\mathcal{K}_{f^{n}x}\circ Df_{x}^{n}|_{\mathcal{L}_{x}}=\mathcal{A}^{n}\circ\mathcal{K}_{x}.

Equivalently,

\mathcal{K}_{f^{n}x}=(\rho^{-n}\mathcal{A}^{n})\circ\mathcal{K}_{x}\circ(\rho^{n}Df_{f^{n}x}^{-n})|_{\mathcal{L}_{f^{n}x}}.

For any
x\in U
,
u\in\mathcal{L}_{f^{n}(x)}
and
n\geq 0
we have

\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{K}(f^{n}x)(u)\rVert

\displaystyle=\Big\|(\rho^{-n}\mathcal{A}^{n})\circ\mathcal{K}(x)\circ(\rho^{n}Df^{-n})|_{\mathcal{L}_{f^{n}(x)}}(u)\Big\|

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{\geq}C^{-1}\big\|\mathcal{K}(x)\circ(\rho^{n}Df^{-n})|_{\mathcal{L}_{f^{n}(x)}}(u)\big\|

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(2)}}{\geq}C^{-1}C^{-1}\lVert(\rho^{n}Df^{-n})|_{\mathcal{L}_{f^{n}(x)}}(u)\rVert

(4.53)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(3)}}{\geq}C^{-1}C^{-1}\cdot C^{-1}\lVert u\rVert.

Here in
(1)
we use the uniform invertibility of

\rho^{-n}\mathcal{A}^{n}
, which follows from condition (
1
) of Proposition
4.1
;
in
(2)
we use (
4.52
); in
(3)
we use that

(\rho^{n}Df_{f^{n}x}^{-n})^{-1}=\rho^{-n}Df_{x}^{n}
and condition (
3
) of Proposition
4.1
.

(
4.4.8
) shows that (
4.51
) holds on
\bigcup_{n\geq 0}f^{n}(U)
, which is a dense set by transitivity of
f
. By continuity of
\mathcal{K}
,
(
4.51
) holds on
\mathbb{T}^{N}
.

Since
\dim\mathcal{L}_{x}=\dim E
, estimate (
4.51
) implies that

\mathcal{K}(x):\mathcal{L}_{x}\to E
is an isomorphism for every
x
. Moreover,
(
4.51
) gives the uniform inverse bound. Since

\widetilde{q}
and
\mathcal{L}
are
\alpha
-Hölder, the bundle map

\mathcal{K}
is
\alpha
-Hölder; the uniform inverse bound then implies that

\mathcal{K}^{-1}
is also
\alpha
-Hölder. This completes the proof of
Proposition
4.1
.

5.
Reduction of the derivative cocycle

5.1.
Notation

We recall the following notations.

(1)

The numbers
\rho_{i}
, the Lyapunov blocks
\mathcal{E}_{i}
, and the
linear subspaces
E_{i}
are defined in Section
3.1
.

(2)

The maps
A_{i}
and
p_{i}
are defined in Section
3.4
.

(3)

The subbundles
\mathcal{F}_{i,j}
are defined in item
5

of Section
3.1
.

In this section, we prove a cocycle-reduction result over the Lyapunov blocks

\mathcal{E}_{i}
. This reduction will play a crucial role in the proofs of
Theorems
1.2
and
1.3
.

Since
A
is irreducible,
A
is diagonalizable over
\mathbb{C}
. Thus, for each

i
, after a fixed real linear change of coordinates on
E_{i}
, the map

A_{i}
has a real block-diagonal form.

Theorem 5.1
.

For every
1\leq i\leq\ell
, there exists a flag of
A_{i}
-invariant
subspaces

(5.1)

\displaystyle\{0\}=V_{i,0}\subset V_{i,1}\subset\cdots\subset V_{i,j_{i}}=E_{i}

and an
\alpha
-Hölder bundle isomorphism
\mathcal{C}_{i}:\mathcal{E}_{i}\to E_{i}
, whose inverse is also
\alpha
-Hölder, such that, for every
x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
, the map

\mathcal{C}_{i}(x):\mathcal{E}_{i}(x)\to E_{i}

is a linear isomorphism satisfying the following properties.

(1)

For every
0\leq j\leq j_{i}-1
, there is an
A_{i}
-invariant subspace

W_{i,j+1}\subset E_{i}
such that

\displaystyle V_{i,j+1}=V_{i,j}\oplus W_{i,j+1}.

(2)

For every
0\leq j\leq j_{i}
,

\mathcal{C}_{i}(x)(\mathcal{F}_{i,j}(x))=V_{i,j}.

We denote the restriction
\mathcal{C}_{i,j}(x):=\mathcal{C}_{i}(x)|_{\mathcal{F}_{i,j}(x)}
.

(3)

There exists an
\alpha
-Hölder map

\widetilde{A}_{i}:\mathbb{T}^{N}\to\operatorname{End}(E_{i}),

such that, for every
x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
,

\mathcal{C}_{i}(fx)\circ Df_{x}|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}(x)}=\widetilde{A}_{i}(x)\circ\mathcal{C}_{i}(x).

With respect to the decomposition

E_{i}=W_{i,1}\oplus\cdots\oplus W_{i,j_{i}},

the linear map
\widetilde{A}_{i}(x)
is block upper triangular for every

x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
, and its diagonal blocks coincide with the corresponding
diagonal blocks of
A_{i}
.

In particular, for the first subbundle in the flag, we have

(5.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(fx)\circ Df_{x}|_{\mathcal{F}_{i,1}(x)}=A_{i}|_{V_{i,1}}\circ\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(x),\qquad x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}.

Remark 5.2
.

From (
5.2
), it is natural to think of
\mathcal{C}_{i,1}
as

DH
along
\mathcal{F}_{i,1}
. However,
\mathcal{F}_{i,1}
is constructed
abstractly and is not necessarily integrable. Even if
\mathcal{F}_{i,1}
is
integrable, the differentiability of
H
along
\mathcal{F}_{i,1}
is not
straightforward.

5.2.
Role of Theorem
5.1

Theorem
5.1
will be used twice later, in the proofs of
Theorems
1.2
and
1.3
.

The first use is in the proof of Proposition
6.1
, where we obtain
differentiability of
H
along curves tangent to
\mathcal{F}_{i,1}
. This
eventually leads to the integrability of
\mathcal{F}_{i,1}
and to the
differentiability of
H
along
\mathcal{F}_{i,1}
; see
Theorem
7.1
. In this step, the conjugacy relation
(
5.2
) plays a crucial role.

The second use is in the proof of Theorem
9.1
, where we obtain
differentiability of
H
along the whole block
\mathcal{E}_{i}
. In that step,
the full block upper-triangular reduction in (
3
) plays a
crucial role.

5.3.
Proof strategy

Fix
1\leq i\leq\ell
. The flag

\displaystyle\{0\}=\mathcal{F}_{i,0}\subset\mathcal{F}_{i,1}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{F}_{i,j_{i}}=\mathcal{E}_{i}

reduces the problem to the quotient cocycles on
\mathcal{B}_{i,j}=\mathcal{F}_{i,j}/\mathcal{F}_{i,j-1}
.
Each quotient cocycle carries an invariant conformal structure, and the
periodic-data estimates verified below allow us to apply
Proposition
4.1
. This gives a Hölder conjugacy on each quotient
bundle. We then assemble these quotient conjugacies, using a Hölder
splitting subordinate to the flag, to obtain the block upper-triangular
reduction of
Df
over
\mathcal{E}_{i}
.

5.4.
Notations and basic facts

We list notations and basic facts that will be used in this section. Recall notations in (
5
) of Section
3.1
.

5.4.1.
Periodic data

Lemma 5.3
.

Fix
1\leq i\leq\ell
. For every
1\leq j\leq j_{i}
, every periodic point
p
of
f
with period
n
, there exist an
A_{i}^{n}
-invariant subspace

s_{i,j,p}\subset E_{i}
and a linear isomorphism

\mathcal{T}_{i,j,p}:(\mathcal{F}_{i,j})_{p}/(\mathcal{F}_{i,j-1})_{p}\to s_{i,j,p}

such that

\mathcal{T}_{i,j,p}\circ\overline{Df_{p}^{n}}=A_{i}^{n}|_{s_{i,j,p}}\circ\mathcal{T}_{i,j,p},

where
\overline{Df_{p}^{n}}
denotes the map induced by
Df_{p}^{n}
on the quotient

(\mathcal{F}_{i,j})_{p}/(\mathcal{F}_{i,j-1})_{p}
. Moreover,

\max\left\{\|\mathcal{T}_{i,j,p}\|,\|\mathcal{T}_{i,j,p}^{-1}\|\right\}\leq C.

Proof.

By the construction of the flag
\mathcal{F}_{i,j}
, the quotient cocycle
induced by
\phi_{i}Df
on
\mathcal{F}_{i,j}/\mathcal{F}_{i,j-1}

is an isometry with respect to a Hölder Riemannian metric (see (
5
) of Section
3.1
). Hence, for every
periodic point
p
of period
n
, the map

\phi_{i}(f^{n-1}p)\cdots\phi_{i}(p)\,\overline{Df_{p}^{n}}

is an isometry on
(\mathcal{F}_{i,j})_{p}/(\mathcal{F}_{i,j-1})_{p}
.

Since all eigenvalues of
\overline{Df_{p}^{n}}
have modulus
\rho_{i}^{n}
, we have

(5.3)

\displaystyle\phi_{i}(f^{n-1}p)\cdots\phi_{i}(p)=\rho_{i}^{-n}.

Thus
\rho_{i}^{-n}\overline{Df_{p}^{n}}
is an isometry on
(\mathcal{F}_{i,j})_{p}/(\mathcal{F}_{i,j-1})_{p}
.

On the other hand, since
A_{i}
is diagonalizable over
\mathbb{C}
and all
of its eigenvalues have modulus
\rho_{i}
, we may choose an inner product on

E_{i}
with respect to which
\rho_{i}^{-1}A_{i}
is an isometry.

By the periodic-data assumption,
Df_{p}^{n}
is conjugate to
A^{n}
. Since

\mathcal{F}_{i,j}(p)
and
\mathcal{F}_{i,j-1}(p)
are
Df_{p}^{n}
-invariant, the
eigenvalues of the quotient map
\overline{Df_{p}^{n}}
are among the eigenvalues
of
Df_{p}^{n}|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}(p)}
, counted with multiplicity. Hence they agree
with a subcollection of the eigenvalues of
A_{i}^{n}
, counted with
multiplicity. Let
s_{i,j,p}\subset E_{i}
be the real
A_{i}^{n}
-invariant
subspace corresponding to this subcollection. Then

A_{i}^{n}|_{s_{i,j,p}}
and
\overline{Df_{p}^{n}}
have the same eigenvalues,
counted with multiplicity.

Therefore the normalized maps

\rho_{i}^{-n}\overline{Df_{p}^{n}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\rho_{i}^{-n}A_{i}^{n}|_{s_{i,j,p}}

are isometries with the same complex eigenvalues, counted with multiplicity.
Real isometries are orthogonally conjugate precisely when they have the same
complex eigenvalues with multiplicity. Hence there exists an isometry

\mathcal{T}_{i,j,p}:(\mathcal{F}_{i,j})_{p}/(\mathcal{F}_{i,j-1})_{p}\to s_{i,j,p}

such that

\mathcal{T}_{i,j,p}\circ\rho_{i}^{-n}\overline{Df_{p}^{n}}=\rho_{i}^{-n}A_{i}^{n}|_{s_{i,j,p}}\circ\mathcal{T}_{i,j,p}.

Equivalently,

\mathcal{T}_{i,j,p}\circ\overline{Df_{p}^{n}}=A_{i}^{n}|_{s_{i,j,p}}\circ\mathcal{T}_{i,j,p}.

Finally, the Hölder metrics on the quotient bundles are uniformly equivalent
to the background metric, and the metric on
E_{i}
is fixed. Hence the
isometries
\mathcal{T}_{i,j,p}
and their inverses have uniformly bounded
operator norms. Therefore

\max\left\{\|\mathcal{T}_{i,j,p}\|,\|\mathcal{T}_{i,j,p}^{-1}\|\right\}\leq C.

∎

Corollary 5.4
.

Fix
1\leq i\leq\ell
and
1\leq j\leq j_{i}
. For every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

\displaystyle\Big\|\rho_{i}^{-n}\overline{Df_{x}^{n}}\Big\|\leq C,\qquad x\in\mathbb{T}^{N},

where
\overline{Df_{x}^{n}}
denotes the cocycle induced by
Df_{x}^{n}
on the
quotient bundle
\mathcal{F}_{i,j}/\mathcal{F}_{i,j-1}
.

Proof.

Let

\Phi_{i}(x):=\ln(\rho_{i}\phi_{i}(x)).

(
5.3
) in Lemma
5.3
shows that, for every periodic point
p
of
period
m
, we have

\Pi_{k=0}^{m-1}\phi_{i}(f^{k}p)=\rho_{i}^{-m}.

Hence

\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\Phi_{i}(f^{k}p)=\ln\left(\Pi_{k=0}^{m-1}\rho_{i}\phi_{i}(f^{k}p)\right)=0.

By the Livšic theorem, there exists a Hölder function

\lambda:\mathbb{T}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

\lambda(fx)-\lambda(x)=\Phi_{i}(x).

Therefore, for every
n\geq 1
,

\Pi_{k=0}^{n-1}\phi_{i}(f^{k}x)=\rho_{i}^{-n}e^{\lambda(f^{n}x)-\lambda(x)}.

Since the quotient cocycle induced by
\phi_{i}Df
on
\mathcal{F}_{i,j}/\mathcal{F}_{i,j-1}

is an isometry with respect to a Hölder Riemannian metric, and since this metric is uniformly equivalent to
the background metric, we have

\displaystyle\Big\|\left(\Pi_{k=0}^{n-1}\phi_{i}(f^{k}x)\right)\overline{Df_{x}^{n}}\Big\|\leq C

Then we have

\left\|\rho_{i}^{-n}\overline{Df_{x}^{n}}\right\|=e^{\lambda(x)-\lambda(f^{n}x)}\Big\|\left(\Pi_{k=0}^{n-1}\phi_{i}(f^{k}x)\right)\overline{Df_{x}^{n}}\Big\|\leq Ce^{\lambda(x)-\lambda(f^{n}x)}\leq C_{1}.

The estimate for
n<0
follows by applying the same argument to the inverse
quotient cocycle. The case
n=0
is immediate. This completes the proof.

∎

5.4.2.
Asymptotic behavior

Lemma 5.5
.

Fix
1\leq i\leq\ell
and
1\leq j\leq j_{i}
. Denote by
\overline{Df_{x}}

induced cocycle by
Df
on the quotient bundle
\mathcal{B}:=\mathcal{F}_{i,j}/\mathcal{F}_{i,j-1}
.
Then there exists a sequence
k_{n}\to+\infty
such that:

(1)

we have

\displaystyle\rho_{i}^{-k_{n}}A_{i}^{k_{n}}\to I_{id}|_{E_{i}}\quad\text{and}\quad\rho_{i}^{k_{n}}A_{i}^{-k_{n}}\to I_{id}|_{E_{i}}\quad n\to+\infty;

(2)

for any
\epsilon>0
there is
l_{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{N}
such that: for any
k_{n}
with
n\geq l_{\epsilon}
and any
x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
with
f^{2k_{n}}x=x
we have

\displaystyle\Big\|\rho_{i}^{-k_{n}}\overline{Df^{k_{n}}_{f^{k_{n}}(x)}}-\rho_{i}^{k_{n}}\overline{Df^{-k_{n}}_{f^{k_{n}}(x)}}\Big\|\leq\epsilon.

Proof.

Since
A_{i}
is diagonalizable over
\mathbb{C}
and all of its eigenvalues have
modulus
\rho_{i}
, we may write its eigenvalues as

\rho_{i}e^{\sqrt{-1}a_{1}},\ldots,\rho_{i}e^{\sqrt{-1}a_{m}}.

Choose a sequence
\ell_{n}\to+\infty
with
\ell_{n+1}-\ell_{n}\to+\infty
such
that

\displaystyle e^{\sqrt{-1}a_{r}\ell_{n}}\qquad\text{converges for every }1\leq r\leq m.

Let
k_{n}=\ell_{n+1}-\ell_{n}
. This proves (
1
).

(
2
): Suppose that
f^{2k_{n}}x=x
. Let
y=f^{k_{n}}x
. Then
f^{2k_{n}}y=y
. By Lemma
5.3
, there
exist an
A_{i}^{2k_{n}}
-invariant subspace
s_{i,j,y}\subset E_{i}
and a linear isomorphism

T_{i,j,y}:\mathcal{B}_{y}\to s_{i,j,y}

such that

T_{i,j,y}\circ\overline{Df^{2k_{n}}_{y}}=A^{2k_{n}}|_{s_{i,j,y}}\circ T_{i,j,y}.

Hence

(5.4)

\displaystyle\left(\rho_{i}^{-2k_{n}}\overline{Df_{y}^{2k_{n}}}-I_{id}|_{\mathcal{B}_{y}}\right)=T_{i,j,y}^{-1}\left(\rho_{i}^{-2k_{n}}A^{2k_{n}}|_{s_{i,j,y}}-I_{id}\right)T_{i,j,y}.

By (
1
), for every
\epsilon>0
there exists
l_{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{N}
such
that

\displaystyle\big\|\rho_{i}^{-2k_{n}}A_{i}^{2k_{n}}-I_{id}|_{E_{i}}\big\|<\epsilon^{2},\qquad n\geq l_{\epsilon}.

By using the uniform bounds on
T_{i,j,y}
and
T_{i,j,y}^{-1}
from
Lemma
5.3
, (
5.4
) gives

\displaystyle\big\|\rho_{i}^{-2k_{n}}\overline{Df_{y}^{2k_{n}}}-I_{id}|_{\mathcal{B}_{y}}\big\|

\displaystyle\leq C\epsilon^{2},\qquad n\geq l_{\epsilon}.

We note that

\rho_{i}^{-k_{n}}\overline{Df_{y}^{k_{n}}}-\rho_{i}^{k_{n}}\overline{Df_{y}^{-k_{n}}}=\rho_{i}^{k_{n}}\overline{Df_{y}^{-k_{n}}}\left(\rho_{i}^{-2k_{n}}\overline{Df_{y}^{2k_{n}}}-Id|_{\mathcal{B}_{y}}\right).

This, together with Corollary
5.4
, gives

\displaystyle\left\|\rho_{i}^{-k_{n}}\overline{Df_{y}^{k_{n}}}-\rho_{i}^{k_{n}}\overline{Df_{y}^{-k_{n}}}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\rho_{i}^{k_{n}}\overline{Df_{y}^{-k_{n}}}\right\|\left\|\rho_{i}^{-2k_{n}}\overline{Df_{y}^{2k_{n}}}-\operatorname{Id}_{\mathcal{B}_{y}}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot C\epsilon^{2}\leq\epsilon.

Since
y=f^{k_{n}}x
, this is exactly (
2
). The proof is complete.

∎

5.5.
Proof of Theorem
5.1

Fix
1\leq i\leq\ell
. Recall that
\mathcal{E}_{i}=\mathcal{F}_{i,j_{i}}
and that we have the flag

\{0\}=\mathcal{F}_{i,0}\subset\mathcal{F}_{i,1}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{F}_{i,j_{i}}=\mathcal{E}_{i},

see (
5
) of Section
3.1
. For each
1\leq j\leq j_{i}
, set

\mathcal{B}_{i,j}:=\mathcal{F}_{i,j}/\mathcal{F}_{i,j-1},

and denote by

\overline{Df_{x}}:(\mathcal{B}_{i,j})_{x}\to(\mathcal{B}_{i,j})_{fx}

the quotient cocycle induced by
Df_{x}
.

Since
Df_{0}
is conjugate to
A
, and since
Df_{0}
preserves the flag at

0
, the quotient map

\overline{Df_{0}}:(\mathcal{B}_{i,j})_{0}\to(\mathcal{B}_{i,j})_{0}

is diagonalizable over
\mathbb{C}
, and all of its eigenvalues have modulus

\rho_{i}
. After reordering and grouping the real diagonal blocks of

A_{i}:=A|_{E_{i}}
if necessary, we may choose an
A_{i}
-invariant block

W_{i,j}\subset E_{i}
and a linear isomorphism

P_{i,j}:(\mathcal{B}_{i,j})_{0}\to W_{i,j}

such that, writing
A_{i,j}:=A_{i}|_{W_{i,j}}
, we have

(5.5)

\displaystyle P_{i,j}\circ\overline{Df_{0}}=A_{i,j}\circ P_{i,j}.

We now apply Proposition
4.1
to the quotient cocycle

\overline{Df_{x}}
on
\mathcal{B}_{i,j}
, with target space
(\mathcal{B}_{i,j})_{0}
and fixed
linear map
\overline{Df_{0}}
. More precisely, we use Proposition
4.1
in its vector-bundle form, with

\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{B}_{i,j},\qquad E=(\mathcal{B}_{i,j})_{0},\qquad\mathcal{A}=\overline{Df_{0}}.

We verify the hypotheses of Proposition
4.1
.

(1)

By (
5.5
), the map
\overline{Df_{0}}|_{E}
is conjugate to

A_{i,j}
. Hence
\overline{Df_{0}}
is diagonalizable over
\mathbb{C}
, and
all of its eigenvalues have modulus
\rho_{i}
.

(2)

At the fixed point
0
, the fixed-point compatibility condition is
tautological:

\overline{Df_{0}}=\mathcal{A}\qquad\text{on }(\mathcal{B}_{i,j})_{0}.

(3)

By Corollary
5.4
, for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

\bigl\|\rho_{i}^{-n}\overline{Df_{x}^{n}}\bigr\|\leq C,\qquad x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}.

(4)

Let the sequence
k_{n}
be as in Lemma
5.5
. By (
5.5
) and (
1
) of Lemma
5.5
, we have

\displaystyle\rho_{i}^{-k_{n}}(\overline{Df_{0}})^{k_{n}}\to I_{id}|_{(\mathcal{B}_{i,j})_{0}},\qquad\rho_{i}^{k_{n}}(\overline{Df_{0}})^{-k_{n}}\to I_{id}|_{(\mathcal{B}_{i,j})_{0}}.

Moreover, by (
2
) of Lemma
5.5
, for any
\epsilon>0
there is
l_{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{N}
such that: for any
k_{n}
with
n\geq l_{\epsilon}
and any
x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
with
f^{2k_{n}}x=x
we have

\displaystyle\Big\|(\rho_{i}^{-k_{n}}\overline{Df^{k_{n}})}|_{\mathcal{B}_{f^{k_{n}}(x)}}-(\rho_{i}^{k_{n}}\overline{Df^{-k_{n}})}|_{\mathcal{B}_{f^{k_{n}}(x)}}\Big\|\leq\epsilon.

Hence all hypotheses of Proposition
4.1
are satisfied. Then for each
1\leq j\leq j_{i}
, there exists an

\alpha
-
Hölder
bundle map

\mathcal{K}_{i,j}(x):(\mathcal{B}_{i,j})_{x}\to(\mathcal{B}_{i,j})_{0}

such that each
\mathcal{K}_{i,j}(x)
is a linear isomorphism and

\mathcal{K}_{i,j}(fx)\circ\overline{Df_{x}}=\overline{Df_{0}}\circ\mathcal{K}_{i,j}(x),\qquad x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}.

Moreover,

\|(\mathcal{K}_{i,j}(x))^{-1}u\|\leq C\|u\|,\qquad x\in\mathbb{T}^{N},\quad u\in(\mathcal{B}_{i,j})_{0}.

Define

\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{i,j}(x):=P_{i,j}\circ\mathcal{K}_{i,j}(x).

Then

\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{i,j}(x):(\mathcal{B}_{i,j})_{x}\to W_{i,j}

is an
\alpha
-
Hölder
bundle isomorphism satisfying

(5.6)

\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{i,j}(fx)\circ\overline{Df_{x}}=A_{i,j}\circ\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{i,j}(x),\qquad x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}.

We now assemble these quotient conjugacies. Choose an

\alpha
-
Hölder
splitting subordinate to the flag:

\mathcal{E}_{i}=\mathcal{G}_{i,1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{G}_{i,j_{i}},

where

\mathcal{F}_{i,j}=\mathcal{G}_{i,1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{G}_{i,j}.

Let

\pi_{i,j}:\mathcal{F}_{i,j}\to\mathcal{F}_{i,j}/\mathcal{F}_{i,j-1}=\mathcal{B}_{i,j}

be the quotient projection. Define

V_{i,j}:=W_{i,1}\oplus\cdots\oplus W_{i,j},\qquad 0\leq j\leq j_{i},

with
V_{i,0}=\{0\}
. Then

\{0\}=V_{i,0}\subset V_{i,1}\subset\cdots\subset V_{i,j_{i}}=E_{i}

is a flag of
A_{i}
-invariant, and hence
A
-invariant, subspaces.

For

v=v_{1}+\cdots+v_{j_{i}},\qquad v_{j}\in\mathcal{G}_{i,j}(x),

define

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{i}(x)v:=\sum_{j=1}^{j_{i}}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{i,j}(x)\bigl(\pi_{i,j}(v_{j})\bigr).

Then

\mathcal{C}_{i}(x):\mathcal{E}_{i}(x)\to E_{i}

is a linear isomorphism for every
x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
. Moreover, for every

0\leq j\leq j_{i}
,

\mathcal{C}_{i}(x)(\mathcal{F}_{i,j}(x))=V_{i,j}.

Thus, if we denote

\mathcal{C}_{i,j}(x):=\mathcal{C}_{i}(x)|_{\mathcal{F}_{i,j}(x)},

then

\mathcal{C}_{i,j}(x):\mathcal{F}_{i,j}(x)\to V_{i,j}

is a bundle isomorphism.

Since
Df
preserves the flag

\mathcal{F}_{i,0}\subset\mathcal{F}_{i,1}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{F}_{i,j_{i}},

the map

\mathcal{C}_{i}(fx)\circ Df_{x}|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}(x)}\circ\mathcal{C}_{i}(x)^{-1}

is block upper triangular with respect to the decomposition

E_{i}=W_{i,1}\oplus\cdots\oplus W_{i,j_{i}}.

Its
j
-th diagonal block is
A_{i,j}
, by
(
5.6
). Therefore, defining

\widetilde{A}_{i}(x):=\mathcal{C}_{i}(fx)\circ Df_{x}|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}(x)}\circ\mathcal{C}_{i}(x)^{-1},

we obtain a block upper-triangular linear map

\widetilde{A}_{i}(x):E_{i}\to E_{i}

whose diagonal blocks coincide with the corresponding diagonal blocks of

A_{i}
, and

\mathcal{C}_{i}(fx)\circ Df_{x}|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}(x)}=\widetilde{A}_{i}(x)\circ\mathcal{C}_{i}(x),\qquad x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}.

It remains to check the Hölder regularity. Since each

\mathcal{K}_{i,j}
is
\alpha
-Hölder and its inverse is
uniformly bounded, the identity

\mathcal{K}_{i,j}(x)^{-1}-\mathcal{K}_{i,j}(y)^{-1}=\mathcal{K}_{i,j}(x)^{-1}\bigl(\mathcal{K}_{i,j}(y)-\mathcal{K}_{i,j}(x)\bigr)\mathcal{K}_{i,j}(y)^{-1}

shows that
\mathcal{K}_{i,j}^{-1}
is also
\alpha
-Hölder.
Thus each
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{i,j}
is a bi-
\alpha
-Hölder
bundle isomorphism. Since the splitting

\mathcal{E}_{i}=\mathcal{G}_{i,1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{G}_{i,j_{i}}

and the quotient projections
\pi_{i,j}
are
\alpha
-Hölder,
the assembled bundle map
\mathcal{C}_{i}
is bi-
\alpha
-Hölder.

Finally, for
j=1
, we have

\mathcal{B}_{i,1}=\mathcal{F}_{i,1}/\mathcal{F}_{i,0}=\mathcal{F}_{i,1}.

Hence

\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(fx)\circ Df_{x}|_{\mathcal{F}_{i,1}(x)}=A_{i}|_{V_{i,1}}\circ\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(x),\qquad x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}.

This proves the stated assertion for the first subbundle in the flag and
completes the proof of Theorem
5.1
.

6.
Curve differentiability of
H

6.1.
Notation

We recall the following notation.

(1)

\mathfrak{m}
and
\mu
are defined in Section
3.1
.

(2)

The numbers
\rho_{i}
, the Lyapunov blocks
\mathcal{E}_{i}
, and the
linear subspaces
E_{i}
are defined in Section
3.1
.

(3)

The maps
A_{i}
and
p_{i}
are defined in Section
3.4
.

(4)

The foliations
\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f}
and
\mathcal{W}_{i}^{A}
are defined in
(
2
) of Section
3.1
.

(5)

The subbundle
\mathcal{F}_{i,1}
is defined in (
5
) of
Section
3.1
.

(6)

The map
\mathcal{C}_{i,1}
and the subspace
V_{i,1}
are defined in
Theorem
5.1
.

(7)

Set
H_{i}=p_{i}\circ H
. After choosing a lift to the universal cover,
we write

H_{i}=p_{i}+h_{i}.

Any two choices of lift differ by a constant vector. Hence this choice does
not affect the differentiability of
H_{i}
or
H
in this section.

We fix
i_{0}\leq i\leq\ell
. Throughout this section, we write
\mathcal{F}:=\mathcal{F}_{i,1}
for simplicity.

6.2.
Curve differentiability along
\mathcal{F}

We introduce the notion of curve differentiability of
H
along

\mathcal{F}
. Since
H
is torus-valued, derivatives and integrals of

H\circ\gamma
will always be understood after choosing a local lift to

\mathbb{R}^{N}
. This convention is independent of the choice of lift.

(1)

A curve
\gamma:(-1,1)\to\mathbb{T}^{N}
is called a
regular curve along

\mathcal{F}
if
\gamma
is a
C^{1+\alpha}
curve and

\gamma^{\prime}(t)\in\mathcal{F}_{\gamma(t)}
for any
t\in(-1,1)
. We say that
\gamma
passes through
x
if

\gamma(0)=x
.

(2)

x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
. Two regular curves
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}
along
\mathcal{F}

passing through
x
are called
equivalent
if
\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(0)=\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(0)
.
We denote the equivalence class of
\gamma
by
[\gamma]_{x}
.

(3)

Let
x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
and
u\in\mathcal{F}_{x}
. We say that
H
is

curve differentiable along
u
if there exists a regular curve

\gamma
along
\mathcal{F}
such that

\gamma(0)=x,\qquad\gamma^{\prime}(0)=u,

and the derivative

\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}H\circ\gamma(t)

exists and is independent of the choice of such
\gamma
. In this case we define

dH_{x}^{\mathfrak{c}}(u):=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}H\circ\gamma(t).

6.3.
Main result

The role of Proposition
6.1
is to provide the first step in the proof of Theorem
7.1
. More precisely, Proposition
6.1
proves curve differentiability of
H
along
\mathcal{F}
, which will later be used to establish the integrability of
\mathcal{F}

and the differentiability of
H
along its integral leaves.

Proposition 6.1
.

Suppose that
H(\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f})=\mathcal{W}_{i}^{A}
for some

i_{0}\leq i\leq\ell
. Then, for every
x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
and every

u\in\mathcal{F}_{x}
, we have

dH_{x}^{\mathfrak{c}}(u)=\mathcal{D}_{i}(x)(u),

where

\mathcal{D}_{i}(x):=B_{i}\circ\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(x):\mathcal{F}_{x}\to E_{i}.

Here
B_{i}:V_{i,1}\to E_{i}
is a fixed linear map.

Remark 6.2
.

From the conjugacy relation (
5.2
) in Theorem
5.1
, it is
natural to think that
\mathcal{C}_{i,1}
represents

DH_{i}|_{\mathcal{F}}
. However, the solution in (
5.2
) is not
canonically normalized. For instance, one may post-compose

\mathcal{C}_{i,1}
with a constant linear map commuting with
A_{i}
and
obtain another solution of the same conjugacy relation. Proposition
6.1

identifies the actual curve derivative of
H_{i}
along
\mathcal{F}
: up to
the fixed linear map
B_{i}
, it is precisely
\mathcal{C}_{i,1}
.

A key step in the proof of Proposition
6.1
is the following result:

Lemma 6.3
.

Suppose
H(\mathcal{W}^{f}_{i})=\mathcal{W}^{A}_{i}
for some
i_{0}\leq i\leq\ell
. Then for any regular curve
\gamma
along
\mathcal{F}
, there is
\delta>0
such that for any
C^{1}
function
\omega
compactly supported on
(-\delta,\delta)
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}H\circ\gamma(t)\omega^{\prime}(t)dt=-\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\mathcal{D}_{i}(\gamma(t))(\gamma^{\prime}(t))\omega(t)dt.

The integrals in Lemma
6.3
are understood componentwise. More precisely,
if
g
is vector-valued and
\varphi
is scalar-valued, then
g\varphi

denotes their componentwise product, and the integral of
g\varphi
is taken
componentwise.

Remark 6.4
.

Under the assumption

H(\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f})=\mathcal{W}_{i}^{A}
, the components
H_{j}
,
j\neq i
, are
constant along the leaves of
\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f}
. Hence, for
j\neq i
,

\displaystyle\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}H_{j}\circ\gamma(t)\,\omega^{\prime}(t)\,dt=H_{j}(\gamma(0))\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\omega^{\prime}(t)\,dt=0,

because
\omega\in C_{c}^{1}(-\delta,\delta)
. Hence,

\displaystyle\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}H\circ\gamma(t)\omega^{\prime}(t)dt=\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}H_{i}\circ\gamma(t)\omega^{\prime}(t)dt.

6.4.
Proof of Proposition
6.1

In this subsection, we prove Proposition
6.1
assuming
Lemma
6.3
. If
u=0
, the conclusion is immediate. Hence assume that

u\neq 0
.

Let
\gamma
be any regular curve along
\mathcal{F}
such that

\gamma(0)=x,\qquad\gamma^{\prime}(0)=u.

By Lemma
6.3
, after shrinking the interval if necessary, for every

\omega\in C_{c}^{1}((-\delta,\delta))
we have

\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}(H\circ\gamma)(t)\omega^{\prime}(t)\,dt=-\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\mathcal{D}_{i}(\gamma(t))(\gamma^{\prime}(t))\omega(t)\,dt.

The integrals are understood after choosing a local lift of
H\circ\gamma

to
\mathbb{R}^{N}
. Thus the lifted curve
H\circ\gamma
has weak derivative

t\mapsto\mathcal{D}_{i}(\gamma(t))(\gamma^{\prime}(t)).

Since
\mathcal{D}_{i}
is Hölder and
\gamma
is
C^{1+\alpha}
, this
weak derivative is continuous. Therefore
H\circ\gamma
is
C^{1}
, and

\frac{d}{dt}(H\circ\gamma)(t)=\mathcal{D}_{i}(\gamma(t))(\gamma^{\prime}(t)).

Evaluating at
t=0
, we obtain

\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}H\circ\gamma(t)=\mathcal{D}_{i}(x)(u).

The right-hand side depends only on
x
and
u
, not on the chosen regular
curve
\gamma
. Hence
H
is curve differentiable along
u
, and

dH_{x}^{\mathfrak{c}}(u)=\mathcal{D}_{i}(x)(u).

This proves Proposition
6.1
.

It remains to prove Lemma
6.3
; this will occupy the rest of the section.

6.5.
Proof strategy for Lemma
6.3

We explain the strategy of the proof of Lemma
6.3
. Since the
integrability of
\mathcal{F}
is not known a priori, we cannot begin with
leafwise differentiability. Instead, we prove differentiability along curves.
Fix a regular curve
\gamma:(-\delta,\delta)\to\mathbb{T}^{N}
tangent to

\mathcal{F}
. The goal is to prove that, for every

\omega\in C_{c}^{1}((-\delta,\delta))
,

\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}(H_{i}\circ\gamma)(t)\omega^{\prime}(t)\,dt=-\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\mathcal{D}_{i}(\gamma(t))(\gamma^{\prime}(t))\omega(t)\,dt.

Formally, using the expansion of
H_{i}
from (
3.10
), one would like to
write

\displaystyle\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}(H_{i}\circ\gamma)(t)\omega^{\prime}(t)\,dt

\displaystyle=\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}p_{i}(\gamma(t))\omega^{\prime}(t)\,dt+\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}(R_{i}\circ f^{m})(\gamma(t))\omega^{\prime}(t)\,dt

\displaystyle=-\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}p_{i}(\gamma^{\prime}(t))\omega(t)\,dt-\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{\gamma(t)}(\gamma^{\prime}(t))\omega(t)\,dt.

Since
\gamma^{\prime}(t)\in\mathcal{F}_{\gamma(t)}
, it is natural to use the
conjugacy relation (
5.2
) from Theorem
5.1
to replace the
growth of
Df^{m}|_{\mathcal{F}}
by the linear growth of
A_{i}^{m}
. However, the
resulting series is difficult to control directly on the one-dimensional curve.
The main idea is therefore to approximate the curve distribution by normalized
averages over small plaques in a foliation box. This converts the curve-level
problem into an averaged problem on
\mathbb{T}^{N}
, where the estimates developed
earlier can be applied.

More precisely, we choose a foliation chart
\Phi_{k}
adapted to
\gamma
, so
that

\Phi_{k}(t,0,0)=\gamma(t).

Here
t
is the distinguished direction along the curve, while the remaining
leaf variables and transverse variables are denoted by
w
and
y
. Let

\mathcal{V}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}:=D\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)(\partial_{t}).

We then define a normalized averaged expression

\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{V},\epsilon}
(see (
6.12
)) over a tube
O_{\epsilon}
around
\gamma
.
Here
\epsilon
measures the size of the tube in the
w
- and
y
-directions,
and
c_{\epsilon}
is the inverse of the corresponding
normalizing volume. The factor

\phi_{1}^{\epsilon}(w)\phi_{2}^{\epsilon}(y)
in the definition of

\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{V},\epsilon}
localizes the average near the central
curve. As
\epsilon\to 0
, the normalized tube averages converge to the
corresponding integral along
\gamma
. In particular, one obtains

\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}H_{i}(\gamma(t))\omega^{\prime}(t)\,dt=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{V},\epsilon}-\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}p_{i}(\gamma^{\prime}(t))\omega(t)\,dt,\qquad\text{see Section }\ref{sec:30}.

However, although
\mathcal{V}
agrees with the curve direction on the central
curve

\displaystyle\Phi_{k}(t,0,0)=\gamma(t),

away from the curve it need not take values
in
\mathcal{F}
. Hence (
5.2
) from Theorem
5.1
cannot be
applied directly. We therefore introduce a vector field
\mathcal{U}
which
takes values in
\mathcal{F}
and agrees with
\mathcal{V}
on the central
curve. The difference between
\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{U}
on the

\epsilon
-neighborhood is
O(\epsilon^{\alpha})
. Combining this with the
averaging estimate of Lemma
6.6
, we obtain

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{V},\epsilon}=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{U},\epsilon}.

Hence we may replace
\mathcal{V}
by the more convenient field

\mathcal{U}
; see Section
6.7.3
.

Finally, we compute the limit involving
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{U},\epsilon}
.
After applying the reduction and the approximate-identity argument, the finite
sums split into two terms; see (
6.7.4
). The first term converges to

\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}p_{i}(\gamma^{\prime}(t))\omega(t)\,dt,\qquad\text{see }\eqref{for:143}.

The second term is the only place where the mixing property of
A
enters.
More precisely, after rewriting the foliation-box integral with respect to
Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{T}^{N}
, we conjugate by
H
. The iterates

f^{k_{m}}
then become the linear iterates
A^{k_{m}}
. The mixing of
A

allows us to replace the oscillating factor by its space average; see
(
6.7.4
). This produces the averaged linear map
B_{i}
, and hence the
operator

\mathcal{D}_{i}(x)=B_{i}\circ\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(x).

Consequently,

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{U},\epsilon}=\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\mathcal{T}_{i}(\gamma(t))(\gamma^{\prime}(t))\omega(t)\,dt,

where

\mathcal{T}_{i}(x)=p_{i}|_{\mathcal{F}_{x}}-\mathcal{D}_{i}(x).

Combining this with

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{V},\epsilon}=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{U},\epsilon},

we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}H_{i}(\gamma(t))\omega^{\prime}(t)\,dt

\displaystyle=\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\mathcal{T}_{i}(\gamma(t))(\gamma^{\prime}(t))\omega(t)\,dt-\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}p_{i}(\gamma^{\prime}(t))\omega(t)\,dt

\displaystyle=-\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\mathcal{D}_{i}(\gamma(t))(\gamma^{\prime}(t))\omega(t)\,dt.

By Remark
6.4
, the same identity holds with
H
in place of
H_{i}
.
This proves Lemma
6.3
.

6.6.
Notation and basic facts

We collect the notation and basic facts that will be used in the proof.

6.6.1.
Absolute continuity of
\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f}

We explain why the conditional measures of Lebesgue measure along

\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f}
are absolutely continuous, under the assumption
H(\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f})=\mathcal{W}_{i}^{A}
. The argument is similar to those in
[
24
]
and
[
43
]
.

Let
\xi
be a measurable partition subordinate to
\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f}
, and let
\xi^{A}:=H\xi

be the corresponding measurable partition subordinate to the linear foliation

\mathcal{W}_{i}^{A}
. Recall that
\mu=(H^{-1})_{*}\mathfrak{m}
is
f
-invariant.
We have

H_{\mu}(f^{-1}\xi\mid\xi)=H_{\mathfrak{m}}(A^{-1}\xi^{A}\mid\xi^{A}).

For the linear foliation
\mathcal{W}_{i}^{A}
, the conditional measures of

\mathfrak{m}
are Lebesgue measures on the affine leaves. Hence Ledrappier’s
entropy formula gives

H_{\mathfrak{m}}(A^{-1}\xi^{A}\mid\xi^{A})=\log|\det(A|_{E_{i}})|.

On the other hand, since the Lyapunov exponents of
f
along

\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f}
agree with the corresponding Lyapunov exponents of
A
, we
have

\log|\det(A|_{E_{i}})|=\int\log\operatorname{Jac}(f|_{\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f}})(x)\,d\mu(x).

Therefore,

H_{\mu}(f^{-1}\xi\mid\xi)=\int\log\operatorname{Jac}(f|_{\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f}})(x)\,d\mu(x).

By Ledrappier’s criterion
[
11
]
, the conditional measures of
\mu
along the leaves
of
\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f}
are absolutely continuous with respect to leafwise
Lebesgue measure. Since
\mu
is equivalent to Lebesgue measure

\mathfrak{m}
(see Section
3.3
), the same absolute-continuity statement holds for the
conditional measures of
\mathfrak{m}
along
\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f}
.

6.6.2.
Foliation charts for
\mathcal{W}^{f}_{i}

Since
\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f}
is a
Hölder
foliation with uniformly

C^{1+\alpha}
leaves, we may choose a finite cover
\{U_{k}\}_{k\in I}
of

\mathbb{T}^{N}
by foliation charts

\Gamma_{k}:O_{k}\subset\mathbb{R}^{\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}}\times\mathbb{R}^{N-\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}}\to U_{k}.

We write points in
O_{k}
as
(x,y)
, where
x\in\mathbb{R}^{\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}}
and
y\in\mathbb{R}^{N-\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}}
.
For each fixed
y
, the plaque

\mathfrak{r}_{y}:=\bigl(\mathbb{R}^{\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}}\times\{y\}\bigr)\cap O_{k}

is mapped by
\Gamma_{k}
into a local leaf of
\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f}
. Moreover,

\Gamma_{k}
is
C^{1+\alpha}
in the leaf variable
x
, uniformly in the
foliation chart.

Since
\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f}
is absolutely continuous (see Section
6.6.1
), the restriction of

\mathfrak{m}
to each foliation box admits a disintegration along the local
plaques. Thus, for every continuous function
g
compactly supported in
O_{k}

and every bounded measurable function
\omega
on
U_{k}
, we have

(6.1)

\displaystyle\int_{U_{k}}\omega(z)g(\Gamma_{k}^{-1}z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(z)=\int\left(\int\omega(\Gamma_{k}(x,y))g(x,y)J_{k}(x,y)\,dx\right)d\nu_{k}(y),

where
\nu_{k}
is a transverse measure and
J_{k}(x,y)>0
is the leafwise
density of the conditional measure of
\mathfrak{m}
on the plaque

\Gamma_{k}(\mathfrak{r}_{y})
.

Moreover, the leafwise densities may be chosen to be Hölder in the leaf
variable
x
, uniformly in the transverse parameter
y
. Indeed,
Ledrappier’s criterion gives conditional densities for
\mu
along

\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f}
. As in
[
24
]
, these densities are given, up to
normalization, by ratios of leafwise Jacobians. Since

\mu=\kappa\,\mathfrak{m}
, where
\kappa
is positive and
C^{\alpha}
by
Section
3.3
, the corresponding conditional densities for

\mathfrak{m}
are obtained, up to plaque-dependent normalization, by
dividing by
\kappa
along the plaques. The normalization constants depend
only on the plaque and therefore do not affect Hölder regularity in the leaf
variable. Hence the densities
J_{k}(x,y)
may be chosen Hölder in the leaf
variable
x
, uniformly in the transverse parameter
y
.

We say that a function
\omega
on
\mathbb{T}^{N}
is
C^{1+\alpha}
along

\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f}
if, in every foliation chart
\Gamma_{k}
, the function
x\mapsto\omega(\Gamma_{k}(x,y))

is
C^{1+\alpha}
for each fixed
y
, with uniform
C^{1+\alpha}
bounds in
the leaf variable.

6.6.3.
Rearranging coordinates along a curve

Let
\gamma:(-1,1)\to\mathbb{T}^{N}
be a
C^{1+\alpha}
curve tangent to

\mathcal{E}_{i}
, that is,
\gamma^{\prime}(t)\in(\mathcal{E}_{i})_{\gamma(t)}
. Assume that
\gamma^{\prime}(0)\neq 0
. Choose a foliation chart

(U_{k},\Gamma_{k})
such that
\gamma(0)\in U_{k}
. After shrinking the interval of
definition of
\gamma
, we may assume that
\gamma(t)\in U_{k}
for all relevant

t
.

Since
\gamma
is tangent to
\mathcal{E}_{i}=T\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f}
, the curve

\gamma
is contained in one local leaf of
\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f}
. Hence, in the
foliation chart, we may write

\Gamma_{k}^{-1}\gamma(t)=(a(t),y_{0}),\qquad a(t)\in\mathbb{R}^{\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}}.

After a linear change of the leaf coordinate
x\in\mathbb{R}^{\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}}
, we may assume that the
first component of
a^{\prime}(0)
is nonzero. Shrinking the interval again if
necessary, we may assume that the first component of
a^{\prime}(t)
is nonzero for
all relevant
t
. Write

w=(x_{2},\ldots,x_{\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}})\in\mathbb{R}^{\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}-1}.

Define a new chart by

\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)=\Gamma_{k}\bigl(a(t)+(0,w),\,y_{0}+y\bigr),

on a sufficiently small domain. Then

\Phi_{k}(t,0,0)=\gamma(t).

Moreover, the map
(t,w)\mapsto a(t)+(0,w)
is a local
C^{1+\alpha}
diffeomorphism in the leaf variables. Therefore,
after possibly shrinking the domain,
\Phi_{k}
is again a foliation chart for

\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f}
, with the same regularity properties as
\Gamma_{k}
. In
particular,
\Phi_{k}
is
C^{1+\alpha}
in the leaf variables and its
leafwise derivative is
\alpha
-Hölder.

The disintegration formula remains valid in the new chart. Namely, for every
continuous function
g
compactly supported in the domain of
\Phi_{k}
and
every bounded measurable function
\omega
on
U_{k}
, we have

(6.2)

\displaystyle\int_{U_{k}}\omega(z)g(\Phi_{k}^{-1}z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(z)=\int\left(\int\omega(\Phi_{k}(t,w,y))g(t,w,y)J_{k}(t,w,y)\,dt\,dw\right)d\nu_{k}(y),

where, after renaming the density,
J_{k}(t,w,y)>0
is

\alpha
-
Hölder
in the leaf variable
(t,w)
, uniformly in
y
.

6.6.4.
Smooth approximations

We will use standard smooth approximations by convolution. Let

\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
be a nonnegative bump function supported
in the unit ball and satisfying
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\phi=1
. For

0<\varepsilon<1
, set

\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)=\varepsilon^{-N}\phi(x/\varepsilon),\qquad\omega_{\varepsilon}=\omega*\phi_{\varepsilon}.

We note that
\phi_{\varepsilon}

is supported on the ball of radius
\varepsilon
centered
at
0
and
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\phi_{\varepsilon}=1
. Moreover,

(6.3)

\displaystyle\lVert(I-\Delta)^{m}\phi_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{L^{1}}\leq c_{m}\varepsilon^{-2m},

where
\Delta
is the Laplacian on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, and
c_{m}
a constant dependent only on
m
. If
\omega\in C^{a}
,
a>0
, then
\omega_{\varepsilon}
is
C^{\infty}
with estimates

(6.4)

\displaystyle\lVert\omega_{\varepsilon}-\omega\rVert_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{a}\varepsilon^{a}\lVert\omega\rVert_{C^{a}},

(6.5)

\displaystyle\lVert\omega_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{C^{r}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{r}\varepsilon^{-r}\lVert\omega\rVert_{C^{0}},\quad\text{for all }r\geq 0.

6.7.
Proof of Lemma
6.3

6.7.1.
Preparatory step

Let
\gamma:(-1,1)\to\mathbb{T}^{N}
be a regular curve along

\mathcal{F}
. Choose a
foliation chart
(U_{k},\Phi_{k})
as in Section
6.6.3
. We denote by
O_{k}

the domain of the rearranged chart
\Phi_{k}
associated with
\gamma
. After
shrinking the interval if necessary, there exists
\delta>0
such that

\displaystyle\gamma((-\delta,\delta))\subset U_{k}\quad\text{and}\quad\Phi_{k}(t,0,0)=\gamma(t),\qquad t\in(-\delta,\delta).

We write the coordinates of
\Phi_{k}
as

(t,w,y),\qquad w\in\mathbb{R}^{\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}-1},\qquad y\in\mathbb{R}^{N-\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}}.

Let
\phi_{1}(w)\geq 0
and
\phi_{2}(y)\geq 0
be two
C^{\infty}
nonnegative bump functions such that

\phi_{1}\equiv 1\quad\text{on }B(0,1/2),\qquad\operatorname{supp}\phi_{1}\subset B(0,1),

and

\phi_{2}\equiv 1\quad\text{on }B(0,1/2),\qquad\operatorname{supp}\phi_{2}\subset B(0,1).

For
\epsilon>0
, set

\displaystyle\phi_{1}^{\epsilon}(w):=\phi_{1}\left(\frac{w}{\epsilon}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\phi_{2}^{\epsilon}(y)=\phi_{2}\left(\frac{y}{\epsilon}\right).

Choose
\epsilon_{0}>0
sufficiently small so that

O_{\epsilon_{0}}:=\{(t,w,y):|t|\leq\delta,\ \|w\|\leq\epsilon_{0},\ \|y\|\leq\epsilon_{0}\}\subset O_{k}.

Define the product measure in these coordinates by

(6.6)

\displaystyle d\widehat{\mu}(t,w,y):=dt\,dw\,d\nu_{k}(y).

Remark 6.5
.

In the computations below, we use the product measure

d\widehat{\mu}(t,w,y)
rather than the full foliation-box measure

J_{k}(t,w,y)\,dt\,dw\,d\nu_{k}(y).

The reason is that we need to differentiate in the leaf direction and integrate
by parts. If the factor
J_{k}(t,w,y)
were included in the measure, then
integration by parts would produce additional terms involving derivatives of

J_{k}
.

This causes no loss. Whenever we need to compare the product measure

d\widehat{\mu}
with Lebesgue measure
\mathfrak{m}
, we absorb the Jacobian
factor
J_{k}(t,w,y)
into the test function. Since
J_{k}
is regular along the
leaves, with estimates uniform in the transverse variable
y
, multiplying by

J_{k}
preserves the Hölder class and the relevant estimates. Thus the use of

d\widehat{\mu}
is only a technical convenience and does not affect the
averaging argument.

For
0<\epsilon\leq\epsilon_{0}
, define the normalizing constant

c_{\epsilon}:=\Big(\int\phi_{1}^{\epsilon}(w)\phi_{2}^{\epsilon}(y)dw\,d\nu_{k}(y)\Big)^{-1}.

Equivalently,

c_{\epsilon}=\epsilon^{-(\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}-1)}\left(\int\phi_{1}(w)\,dw\right)^{-1}\left(\int\phi_{2}\left(\frac{y}{\epsilon}\right)d\nu_{k}(y)\right)^{-1}.

Recall the local identifications of nearby fibers from Section
4.3.1
. Applying them to the bundle
\mathcal{F}
, and after possibly shrinking the
domain once more, we assume that, for every

(t,w,y)\in O_{k}
, the two points

\Phi_{k}(t,0,0)\quad\text{and}\quad\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)

lie in a common trivializing neighborhood for
\mathcal{F}
. Thus the local
fiber identification

\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{F}}_{\Phi_{k}(t,0,0),\,\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}

is well defined throughout the chart.

Next, we introduce two
\alpha
-Hölder vector
fields on
\Phi_{k}(O_{k})
. First, let

\mathcal{V}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}:=D\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)(\partial_{t}).

Since
t
is a leaf coordinate, we have

\mathcal{V}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}\in(\mathcal{E}_{i})_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}.

However, except on the curve
\Phi_{k}(t,0,0)=\gamma(t)
, the vector field

\mathcal{V}
need not belong to
\mathcal{F}
. This is why we introduce a second
vector field.

Define a vector
field
\mathcal{U}
on
\Phi_{k}(O_{k})
by

\mathcal{U}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}:=\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{F}}_{\Phi_{k}(t,0,0),\,\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}\bigl(\gamma^{\prime}(t)\bigr).

Then
\mathcal{U}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}\in\mathcal{F}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}
for all
(t,w,y)\in O_{k}
. Moreover, since

\displaystyle\Phi_{k}(t,0,0)=\gamma(t)\quad\text{and}\quad D\Phi_{k}(t,0,0)(\partial_{t})=\gamma^{\prime}(t),

we have

(6.7)

\mathcal{U}_{\Phi_{k}(t,0,0)}=\mathcal{V}_{\Phi_{k}(t,0,0)},\qquad-\delta\leq t\leq\delta.

Equivalently,

(6.8)

\mathcal{U}_{\gamma(t)}=\mathcal{V}_{\gamma(t)},\qquad-\delta\leq t\leq\delta.

Both vector fields are
\alpha
-Hölder on
\Phi_{k}(O_{k})
. For

\mathcal{V}
, this follows from the
\alpha
-Hölder regularity of the
leafwise derivative
D_{(t,w)}\Phi_{k}
. For
\mathcal{U}
, it follows from the

C^{1+\alpha}
regularity of
\gamma
, the
\alpha
-Hölder regularity of the
bundle
\mathcal{F}
, and the
\alpha
-Hölder dependence of the local fiber
identifications
\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{F}}_{x,y}
.

At the end of this section, we prove the following decay estimate, which will be used in the subsequent parts. We use the product measure
d\widehat{\mu}
defined in (
6.6
).

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be an
\alpha
-Hölder vector field on

\Phi_{k}(O_{k})
taking values in
\mathcal{E}_{i}
. Then, for every

\omega\in C_{c}^{1}(-\delta,\delta)
, for every
m\geq 0

and every sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
, we have

\displaystyle\Big\|c_{\epsilon}\int_{O_{\epsilon}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}(\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)})\omega(t)\phi_{1}^{\epsilon}(w)\phi_{2}^{\epsilon}(y)d\widehat{\mu}\Big\|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\phi_{1}}\epsilon^{-1}\rho_{i}^{-\frac{\alpha(m+1)}{2}}(|m|+1)^{\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}-1}\lVert R_{i}\rVert_{C^{1}}\lVert\omega\rVert_{C^{1}}\lVert\mathcal{Q}\rVert_{C^{\alpha}}.

Proof.

It follows from (
5
) of Section
3.1
and the fact that

A_{i}
is diagonalizable with all eigenvalues of modulus
\rho_{i}
(see Section
3.4
) that, for
every
m\geq 0
,

(6.9)

\displaystyle\lVert A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}}\rVert\leq C(|m|+1)^{\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}-1}\lVert R_{i}\rVert_{C^{1}}.

For
(t,w,y)\in O_{k}
, define the leafwise coordinate vector fields

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{0}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}:=D\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)(\partial_{t}),

and, for
1\leq j\leq\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}-1
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{j}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}:=D\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)(\partial_{w_{j}}).

Since
\mathcal{Q}
takes values in
\mathcal{E}_{i}
, we can write

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}=\sum_{j=0}^{\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}-1}a_{j}(t,w,y)\mathcal{W}^{j}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}.

The coefficients
a_{j}
are
\alpha
-Hölder in the leaf variables

(t,w)
, uniformly in
y
, and satisfy

\|a_{j}\|_{C^{0}}\leq C\|\mathcal{Q}\|_{C^{0}},\qquad\|a_{j}\|_{C^{\alpha}_{t,w}}\leq C\|\mathcal{Q}\|_{C^{\alpha}}.

We smooth
a_{j}
only in the leaf variables
(t,w)
, treating
y
as a
parameter. Let
a_{j,\eta}
be the
corresponding
C^{\infty}
approximation as in Section
6.6.4
. Then the corresponding estimates hold uniformly in

y
:

(6.10)

\displaystyle\lVert a_{j,\eta}-a_{j}\rVert_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq C\eta^{\alpha}\lVert a_{j}\rVert_{C^{\alpha}},

(6.11)

\displaystyle\lVert a_{j,\eta}\rVert_{C^{1}_{t,w}}

\displaystyle\leq C\eta^{-1}\lVert a_{j}\rVert_{C^{0}}.

For each
j
, set

b_{j}(t,w,y):=a_{j}(t,w,y)\omega(t)\phi_{1}^{\epsilon}(w)\phi_{2}^{\epsilon}(y),

and

b_{j,\eta}(t,w,y):=a_{j,\eta}(t,w,y)\omega(t)\phi_{1}^{\epsilon}(w)\phi_{2}^{\epsilon}(y).

Then we have

\displaystyle\int_{O_{\epsilon}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}(\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)})\omega(t)\phi_{1}^{\epsilon}(w)\phi_{2}^{\epsilon}(y)d\widehat{\mu}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}-1}\int_{O_{\epsilon}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}(\mathcal{W}^{j}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)})b_{j}(t,w,y)d\widehat{\mu}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}-1}\mathcal{X}_{j}+\sum_{j=0}^{\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}-1}\mathcal{Z}_{j},

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{j}

\displaystyle=\int_{O_{\epsilon}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}(\mathcal{W}^{j}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)})b_{j,\eta}(t,w,y)d\widehat{\mu}

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{j}

\displaystyle=\int_{O_{\epsilon}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}(\mathcal{W}^{j}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)})(b_{j}-b_{j,\eta})(t,w,y)d\widehat{\mu}.

We first estimate
\|c_{\epsilon}\mathcal{X}_{j}\|
. Write
z_{0}=t
and
z_{j}=w_{j}
for

1\leq j\leq\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}-1
. We note that

D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}\bigl(\mathcal{W}^{j}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}\bigr)=\partial_{z_{j}}(R_{i}\circ f^{m}\circ\Phi_{k})(t,w,y).

Then we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{j}=-\int_{O_{\epsilon}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}(R_{i}\circ f^{m}\circ\Phi_{k})(t,w,y)\big(\partial_{z_{j}}b_{j,\eta}(t,w,y)\big)d\widehat{\mu}.

Since
\omega
is compactly supported in
t
and

\phi_{1}^{\epsilon}
is compactly supported in
w
, no boundary terms appear
when integrating by parts in the leaf variables.

Hence,

\displaystyle\lVert c_{\epsilon}\mathcal{X}_{j}\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\Big\|A_{i}^{-(m+1)}(R_{i}\circ f^{m}\circ\Phi_{k})(t,w,y)\big(\partial_{z_{j}}b_{j,\eta}(t,w,y)\Big)\big\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq C\lVert A_{i}^{-(m+1)}\rVert\lVert R_{i}\rVert_{C^{0}}\cdot\big(\epsilon^{-1}\lVert a_{j,\eta}\rVert_{C^{1}_{t,w}}\lVert\omega\rVert_{C^{1}}\lVert\phi_{1}\rVert_{C^{1}}\big)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{\leq}C\epsilon^{-1}\eta^{-1}\rho_{i}^{-(m+1)}\lVert R_{i}\rVert_{C^{0}}\|\mathcal{Q}\|_{C^{0}}\lVert\omega\rVert_{C^{1}}\lVert\phi_{1}\rVert_{C^{1}}.

Here in
(1)
we use (
6.11
).

We next estimate each
\lVert c_{\epsilon}\mathcal{Z}_{j}\rVert
. It follows from (
6.9
) and (
6.10
) that

\displaystyle\lVert c_{\epsilon}\mathcal{Z}_{j}\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\Big\|A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}(\mathcal{W}^{j}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)})(b_{j}-b_{j,\eta})(t,w,y)\Big\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq C\big\|A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}}\big\|\cdot\lVert a_{j,\eta}-a_{j}\rVert_{C^{0}}\lVert\omega\rVert_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\eta^{\alpha}(|m|+1)^{\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}-1}\lVert R_{i}\rVert_{C^{1}}\lVert\omega\rVert_{C^{0}}\lVert\mathcal{Q}\rVert_{C^{\alpha}}.

Combining the estimates for
\mathcal{X}_{j}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{j}
, and summing
over
0\leq j\leq\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}-1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Big\|c_{\epsilon}\int_{O_{\epsilon}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}(\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)})\omega(t)\phi_{1}^{\epsilon}(w)\phi_{2}^{\epsilon}(y)d\widehat{\mu}\Big\|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\phi_{1}}\big(\epsilon^{-1}\eta^{-1}\rho_{i}^{-(m+1)}+\eta^{\alpha}(|m|+1)^{\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}-1}\big)\lVert R_{i}\rVert_{C^{1}}\lVert\omega\rVert_{C^{1}}\lVert\mathcal{Q}\rVert_{C^{\alpha}}.

Let
\eta=\rho_{i}^{-\frac{m+1}{2}}
. Then

\displaystyle\rho_{i}^{-(m+1)}\eta^{-1}=\rho_{i}^{-\frac{m+1}{2}},\quad\eta^{\alpha}=\rho_{i}^{-\frac{\alpha(m+1)}{2}},\quad\text{and}\quad\rho_{i}^{-\frac{m+1}{2}}\leq\rho_{i}^{-\frac{\alpha(m+1)}{2}}.

Also, since
\epsilon>0
is small, we have

\displaystyle\left\|c_{\epsilon}\int_{O_{\epsilon}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}\bigl(\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}\bigr)\omega(t)\phi_{1}^{\epsilon}(w)\phi_{2}^{\epsilon}(y)\,d\widehat{\mu}\right\|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C_{\phi_{1}}\epsilon^{-1}\rho_{i}^{-\frac{\alpha(m+1)}{2}}(m+1)^{\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}-1}\|R_{i}\|_{C^{1}}\|\omega\|_{C^{1}}\|\mathcal{Q}\|_{C^{\alpha}}.

This proves the lemma.
∎

Lemma
6.6
shows that for any
\alpha
-Hölder vector field
\mathcal{Q}
on
\Phi_{k}(O_{k})
taking values in
\mathcal{E}_{i}
, the
following series converges absolutely for every fixed sufficiently small

\epsilon>0
:

(6.12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{Q},\epsilon}

\displaystyle=-c_{\epsilon}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\int_{O_{\epsilon}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}(\mathcal{Q}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)})\omega(t)\phi_{1}^{\epsilon}(w)\phi_{2}^{\epsilon}(y)d\widehat{\mu}.

6.7.2.
Step 1: Transfer to vector field

Suppose
\omega(t)
is compacted supported on
(-\delta,\delta)
. In this step, we show that

\displaystyle\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}H_{i}(\gamma(t))\omega^{\prime}(t)dt=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{V},\epsilon}-\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}p_{i}(\gamma^{\prime}(t))\omega(t)dt.

Recall that
\mathcal{V}
is defined in Section
6.7.1
.

Here, as in (
7
) of Section
6.1
, we write

H_{i}:=p_{i}\circ H=p_{i}+h_{i}.

First, we show that

(6.13)

\displaystyle\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}H_{i}(\gamma(t))\omega^{\prime}(t)dt=\mathcal{Y}_{1}+\mathcal{Y}_{2},

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{1}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}c_{\epsilon}\int_{O_{\epsilon}}(h_{i}\circ\Phi_{k})(t,w,y)\omega^{\prime}(t)\phi_{1}^{\epsilon}(w)\phi_{2}^{\epsilon}(y)d\widehat{\mu}.

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{2}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}c_{\epsilon}\int_{O_{\epsilon}}(p_{i}\circ\Phi_{k})(t,w,y)\omega^{\prime}(t)\phi_{1}^{\epsilon}(w)\phi_{2}^{\epsilon}(y)d\widehat{\mu}.

Indeed, the normalized measures

c_{\epsilon}\phi_{1}^{\epsilon}(w)\phi_{2}^{\epsilon}(y)\,dw\,d\nu_{k}(y)

converge weakly to the Dirac mass at
(w,y)=(0,0)
. Since

\Phi_{k}(t,0,0)=\gamma(t)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}H_{i}(\gamma(t))\omega^{\prime}(t)\,dt

\displaystyle=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}c_{\epsilon}\int_{O_{\epsilon}}(H_{i}\circ\Phi_{k})(t,w,y)\omega^{\prime}(t)\phi_{1}^{\epsilon}(w)\phi_{2}^{\epsilon}(y)\,d\widehat{\mu}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Y}_{1}+\mathcal{Y}_{2}.

This proves (
6.13
).

We now compute
\mathcal{Y}_{1}
. Using (
3.10
) of Section
3.4
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{1}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}c_{\epsilon}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\int_{O_{\epsilon}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}(R_{i}\circ f^{m}\circ\Phi_{k})(t,w,y)\omega^{\prime}(t)\phi_{1}^{\epsilon}(w)\phi_{2}^{\epsilon}(y)d\widehat{\mu}

\displaystyle=-\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}c_{\epsilon}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\int_{O_{\epsilon}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}(\mathcal{V}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)})\omega(t)\phi_{1}^{\epsilon}(w)\phi_{2}^{\epsilon}(y)d\widehat{\mu}

(6.14)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{V},\epsilon}.

Finally, we compute
\mathcal{Y}_{2}
. Since

\Phi_{k}(t,0,0)=\gamma(t)
, the same averaging argument gives

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{2}=\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}p_{i}(\gamma(t))\omega^{\prime}(t)\,dt=-\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}p_{i}(\gamma^{\prime}(t))\omega(t)dt.

Combining this identity with (
6.13
) and (
6.7.2
) proves the result.

6.7.3.
Step 2: Replacing
\mathcal{V}
by
\mathcal{U}

In this step, we show that

(6.15)

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{V},\epsilon}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{U},\epsilon}.

Recall that
\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{U}
are defined in Section
6.7.1
. Since

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{V},\epsilon}-\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{U},\epsilon}=\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{V}-\mathcal{U},\epsilon},

to prove (
6.15
), it suffices to show that: for any
\epsilon>0

(6.16)

\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{N}_{(\mathcal{V}-\mathcal{U}),\epsilon}\rVert\leq C_{\phi_{1}}\epsilon^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}\lVert R_{i}\rVert_{C^{1}}\lVert\omega\rVert_{C^{1}}\max\{\lVert\mathcal{U}\rVert_{C^{\alpha}},\,\lVert\mathcal{V}\rVert_{C^{\alpha}}\}.

Let
n_{\epsilon}=\lfloor\epsilon^{-\frac{\alpha}{2\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}}}\rfloor+1
. Then

(6.17)

\displaystyle(n_{\epsilon}+1)^{\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}}\epsilon^{\alpha}\leq C\epsilon^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}.

Moreover, since
n_{\epsilon}+2\geq\epsilon^{-\frac{\alpha}{2\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}}}
, we have

(6.18)

\displaystyle\epsilon^{-1}\rho_{i}^{-\frac{\alpha(n_{\epsilon}+2)}{4}}\leq\epsilon^{-1}e^{-c\epsilon^{-\frac{\alpha}{2\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}}}}\leq C\epsilon^{\frac{\alpha}{2}},

where
c=\frac{\alpha\log\rho_{i}}{4}>0
, for all sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
.

We split

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{(\mathcal{V}-\mathcal{U}),\epsilon}=\mathcal{M}_{1}+\mathcal{M}_{2}+\mathcal{M}_{3},

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{1}

\displaystyle:=c_{\epsilon}\sum_{m=0}^{n_{\epsilon}}\int_{O_{\epsilon}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}-\mathcal{V}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 128.0374pt\cdot\omega(t)\phi_{1}^{\epsilon}(w)\phi_{2}^{\epsilon}(y)\,d\widehat{\mu},

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{2}

\displaystyle:=c_{\epsilon}\sum_{m=n_{\epsilon}+1}^{\infty}\int_{O_{\epsilon}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 128.0374pt\cdot\omega(t)\phi_{1}^{\epsilon}(w)\phi_{2}^{\epsilon}(y)\,d\widehat{\mu},

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{3}

\displaystyle:=-c_{\epsilon}\sum_{m=n_{\epsilon}+1}^{\infty}\int_{O_{\epsilon}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}\bigl(\mathcal{V}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\cdot\omega(t)\phi_{1}^{\epsilon}(w)\phi_{2}^{\epsilon}(y)\,d\widehat{\mu}.

The sign of
\mathcal{M}_{3}
will play no role in the estimates.

We first estimate
\mathcal{M}_{1}
. On
\Phi_{k}(O_{\epsilon})
we have

\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{U}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}

\displaystyle-\mathcal{V}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}\rVert\overset{\text{(1)}}{=}\|\mathcal{U}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}-\mathcal{U}_{\Phi_{k}(t,0,0)}+\mathcal{V}_{\Phi_{k}(t,0,0)}-\mathcal{V}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathcal{U}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}-\mathcal{U}_{\Phi_{k}(t,0,0)}\|+\|\mathcal{V}_{\Phi_{k}(t,0,0)}-\mathcal{V}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}\|

(6.19)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(2)}}{\leq}C\epsilon^{\alpha}\max\{\lVert\mathcal{U}\rVert_{C^{\alpha}},\,\lVert\mathcal{V}\rVert_{C^{\alpha}}\}.

Here in
(1)
we use (
6.7
); in
(2)
we note that the points
\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)
and
\Phi_{k}(t,0,0)
are at distance

O(\epsilon)
in the chart, and both vector fields are
\alpha
-Hölder.

Then it follows that

\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{M}_{1}\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m=0}^{n_{\epsilon}}\big\|A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}}\big\|\cdot\lVert\mathcal{U}-\mathcal{V}\rVert_{C^{0}}\lVert\omega\rVert_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{\leq}\sum_{m=0}^{n_{\epsilon}}C(|m|+1)^{\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}-1}\lVert R_{i}\rVert_{C^{1}}\cdot C\epsilon^{\alpha}\max\{\lVert\mathcal{U}\rVert_{C^{\alpha}},\,\lVert\mathcal{V}\rVert_{C^{\alpha}}\}\cdot\lVert\omega\rVert_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}(n_{\epsilon}+1)^{\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}}\epsilon^{\alpha}\lVert R_{i}\rVert_{C^{1}}\lVert\omega\rVert_{C^{0}}\max\{\lVert\mathcal{U}\rVert_{C^{\alpha}},\,\lVert\mathcal{V}\rVert_{C^{\alpha}}\}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(2)}}{\leq}C_{2}\epsilon^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}\lVert R_{i}\rVert_{C^{1}}\lVert\omega\rVert_{C^{0}}\max\{\lVert\mathcal{U}\rVert_{C^{\alpha}},\,\lVert\mathcal{V}\rVert_{C^{\alpha}}\}.

Here in
(1)
we use (
6.9
) and (
6.7.3
); in
(2)
we use (
6.17
).

Next, it follows from Lemma
6.6
that

\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{M}_{2}\rVert+\lVert\mathcal{M}_{3}\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m=n_{\epsilon}+1}^{\infty}C_{\phi_{1}}\epsilon^{-1}\rho_{i}^{-\frac{\alpha(m+1)}{2}}(|m|+1)^{\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}-1}\lVert R_{i}\rVert_{C^{1}}\lVert\omega\rVert_{C^{1}}\max\{\lVert\mathcal{U}\rVert_{C^{\alpha}},\,\lVert\mathcal{V}\rVert_{C^{\alpha}}\}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\phi_{1},1}\epsilon^{-1}\rho_{i}^{-\frac{\alpha(n_{\epsilon}+2)}{4}}\lVert R_{i}\rVert_{C^{1}}\lVert\omega\rVert_{C^{1}}\max\{\lVert\mathcal{U}\rVert_{C^{\alpha}},\,\lVert\mathcal{V}\rVert_{C^{\alpha}}\}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{\leq}C_{\phi_{1},2}\epsilon^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}\lVert R_{i}\rVert_{C^{1}}\lVert\omega\rVert_{C^{1}}\max\{\lVert\mathcal{U}\rVert_{C^{\alpha}},\,\lVert\mathcal{V}\rVert_{C^{\alpha}}\}.

Here in
(1)
we use (
6.18
).

Combining the estimates for
\mathcal{M}_{1},\mathcal{M}_{2},\mathcal{M}_{3}
, we get
(
6.16
). Hence

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\bigl(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{V},\epsilon}-\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{U},\epsilon}\bigr)=0,

which proves (
6.15
).

6.7.4.
Step 3: Computation of
\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{U},\epsilon}

We first list two facts that will be used in the proof.

(1)

From (
1
) of Lemma
5.5
, there is a sequence
k_{n}\to+\infty
such that

\displaystyle\rho_{i}^{-k_{m}}A_{i}^{k_{m}}\to I_{id}|_{E_{i}}\quad\text{and}\quad\rho_{i}^{k_{m}}A_{i}^{-k_{m}}\to I_{id}|_{E_{i}}

as
k_{m}\to+\infty
.

(2)

It follows from Theorem
5.1
that
there exist an
A
-invariant subspace
V_{i,1}\subset E_{i}
and a bi-
\alpha
-
Hölder
subbundle map

\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(x):\mathcal{F}_{x}\to V_{i,1}

(see (
5
) of Section
3.1
) such that each
\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(x)
is a linear isomorphism and

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(fx)\circ Df_{x}|_{\mathcal{F}_{x}}=A_{i}|_{V_{i,1}}\circ\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(x),\qquad x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}.

Define

(6.20)

\displaystyle P(x):=p_{i}\circ\bigl(\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(x)\bigr)^{-1}:V_{i,1}\to E_{i}\quad\text{and}\quad B_{i}:=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}P(H^{-1}z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(z).

Thus
B_{i}:V_{i,1}\to E_{i}
is a fixed linear map. Define

(6.21)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{i}(x):=p_{i}|_{\mathcal{F}_{x}}-B_{i}\circ\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(x)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{D}_{i}(x):=B_{i}\circ\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(x).

Since
B_{i}
is a fixed linear map and
\mathcal{C}_{i,1}
is

\alpha
-Hölder, the maps
\mathcal{T}_{i}
and
\mathcal{D}_{i}
are both

\alpha
-Hölder bundle maps on
\mathcal{F}
.

In this step, we prove that

(6.22)

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{U},\epsilon}=\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\mathcal{T}_{i}(\gamma(t))(\gamma^{\prime}(t))\omega(t)\,dt.

Let

\displaystyle J(x)=-p_{i}|_{\mathcal{F}_{x}}.

Then
J
satisfies the twisted coboundary equation

(6.23)

\displaystyle A_{i}\circ J(x)-J(fx)\circ Df|_{\mathcal{F}_{x}}=DR_{i}|_{\mathcal{F}_{x}},\qquad x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}.

Iterating (
6.23
), for any
m\geq 1
we have

\displaystyle J(x)

\displaystyle+A_{i}^{-m}p_{i}\circ Df^{m}|_{\mathcal{F}_{x}}=\sum_{n=0}^{m-1}A_{i}^{-(n+1)}DR_{i}(f^{n}x)\circ Df^{n}(x)|_{\mathcal{F}_{x}}

(6.24)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{m-1}A_{i}^{-(n+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{n})|_{\mathcal{F}_{x}}

for any
x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
. Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{l}(t,w,y)=\sum_{m=0}^{l}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}(\mathcal{U}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}).

Taking
m=k_{n}
in (
6.7.4
), we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{k_{m}-1}(t,w,y)

\displaystyle=-p_{i}\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}\bigr)+A_{i}^{-k_{m}}p_{i}\circ Df_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}^{k_{m}}\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}\bigr).

By the absolute convergence given by Lemma
6.6
, we may pass to the
subsequence of partial sums
k_{m}-1
. Hence

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{U},\epsilon}

\displaystyle=-c_{\epsilon}\lim_{m\to+\infty}\int_{O_{\epsilon}}\mathcal{S}_{k_{m}-1}(t,w,y)\omega(t)\phi_{1}^{\epsilon}(w)\phi_{2}^{\epsilon}(y)d\widehat{\mu}

(6.25)

\displaystyle=c_{\epsilon}\int_{O_{\epsilon}}p_{i}(\mathcal{U}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)})\omega(t)\phi_{1}^{\epsilon}(w)\phi_{2}^{\epsilon}(y)d\widehat{\mu}-\mathcal{Z}_{\epsilon}

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\epsilon}=c_{\epsilon}\lim_{m\to+\infty}\int_{O_{\epsilon}}\Big(A_{i}^{-k_{m}}p_{i}\circ Df^{k_{m}}|_{\mathcal{F}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}}(\mathcal{U}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)})\Big)\omega(t)\phi_{1}^{\epsilon}(w)\phi_{2}^{\epsilon}(y)d\widehat{\mu}.

Moreover, by the approximate-identity argument, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}c_{\epsilon}\int_{O_{\epsilon}}p_{i}(\mathcal{U}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)})\omega(t)\phi_{1}^{\epsilon}(w)\phi_{2}^{\epsilon}(y)d\widehat{\mu}=\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}p_{i}(\mathcal{U}_{\Phi_{k}(t,0,0)})\omega(t)dt

(6.26)

\displaystyle=\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}p_{i}(\gamma^{\prime}(t))\omega(t)dt.

It remains to compute
\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathcal{Z}_{\epsilon}
.

Then, by (
2
), for
\tau=(t,w,y)
, we have

\displaystyle A_{i}^{-k_{m}}p_{i}\circ Df^{k_{m}}|_{\mathcal{F}_{\Phi_{k}(\tau)}}(\mathcal{U}_{\Phi_{k}(\tau)})

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{=}A_{i}^{-k_{m}}p_{i}\circ\big(\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(f^{k_{m}}\Phi_{k}(\tau))\big)^{-1}\circ A_{i}^{k_{m}}\circ\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(\Phi_{k}(\tau))(\mathcal{U}_{\Phi_{k}(\tau)})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{M}_{1,m}(\tau)+\mathcal{M}_{2,m}(\tau)+\mathcal{M}_{3,m}(\tau),

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{1,m}(\tau)

\displaystyle:=\bigl(\rho_{i}^{k_{m}}A_{i}^{-k_{m}}-I_{id}|_{E_{i}}\bigr)\circ P(f^{k_{m}}\Phi_{k}(\tau))\circ\bigl(\rho_{i}^{-k_{m}}A_{i}^{k_{m}}|_{V_{i,1}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\circ\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(\Phi_{k}(\tau))\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{\Phi_{k}(\tau)}\bigr),

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{2,m}(\tau)

\displaystyle:=P(f^{k_{m}}\Phi_{k}(\tau))\circ\bigl(\rho_{i}^{-k_{m}}A_{i}^{k_{m}}|_{V_{i,1}}-I_{id}|_{V_{i,1}}\bigr)\circ\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(\Phi_{k}(\tau))\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{\Phi_{k}(\tau)}\bigr),

and

\mathcal{M}_{3,m}(\tau):=P(f^{k_{m}}\Phi_{k}(\tau))\circ\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(\Phi_{k}(\tau))\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{\Phi_{k}(\tau)}\bigr).

For
\mathcal{M}_{1,m}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2,m}
, we have

\displaystyle\max\{\|\mathcal{M}_{1,m}\|_{C^{0}},\|\mathcal{M}_{2,m}\|_{C^{0}}\}

\displaystyle\leq C\max\left\{\|\rho_{i}^{k_{m}}A_{i}^{-k_{m}}-I_{id}|_{E_{i}}\|,\|\rho_{i}^{-k_{m}}A_{i}^{k_{m}}-I_{id}|_{E_{i}}\|\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\|P\|_{C^{0}}\|\mathcal{C}_{i,1}\|_{C^{0}}\|\mathcal{U}\|_{C^{0}}.

This, together with the convergence in (
1
), implies that for every fixed

\epsilon>0
,

(6.27)

\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}\int_{O_{\epsilon}}\mathcal{M}_{j,m}(t,w,y)\omega(t)\phi_{1}^{\epsilon}(w)\phi_{2}^{\epsilon}(y)\,d\widehat{\mu}=0,\qquad j=1,2.

We now compute the contribution of
\mathcal{M}_{3,n}
. This is where the
mixing property of
A
enters the argument. Let

\Psi_{\epsilon}(t,w,y):=\frac{\omega(t)\phi_{1}^{\epsilon}(w)\phi_{2}^{\epsilon}(y)}{J_{k}(t,w,y)},

where
J_{k}(t,w,y)
is the density in the local disintegration formula (see (
6.2
)). We extend

\Psi_{\epsilon}\circ\Phi_{k}^{-1}
by zero outside
U_{k}
. Using
(
6.2
), we get

\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}\int_{O_{\epsilon}}\mathcal{M}_{3,m}(t,w,y)\omega(t)\phi_{1}^{\epsilon}(w)\phi_{2}^{\epsilon}(y)\,d\widehat{\mu}

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}P(f^{k_{m}}z)\circ\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(z)(\mathcal{U}_{z})\,\Psi_{\epsilon}(\Phi_{k}^{-1}z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(z)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{=}\lim_{m\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}P(H^{-1}A^{k_{m}}q)\circ\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(H^{-1}q)\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{H^{-1}q}\bigr)

(6.28)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\cdot\Psi_{\epsilon}\bigl(\Phi_{k}^{-1}(H^{-1}q)\bigr)\frac{1}{\kappa(H^{-1}q)}\,d\mathfrak{m}(q).

Here in
(1)
we used:

(a)

the conjugacy relation
H\circ f=A\circ H
, so that

f^{k_{m}}z=H^{-1}A^{k_{m}}H(z);

(b)

the change of variables
q=H(z)
;

(c)

the identity

H_{*}\mathfrak{m}=(\kappa\circ H^{-1})^{-1}\mathfrak{m},

which follows from
(H^{-1})_{*}\mathfrak{m}=\kappa\,\mathfrak{m}
.

Set

G(q):=P(H^{-1}q)

and

F_{\epsilon}(q):=\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(H^{-1}q)\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{H^{-1}q}\bigr)\Psi_{\epsilon}\bigl(\Phi_{k}^{-1}(H^{-1}q)\bigr)\frac{1}{\kappa(H^{-1}q)}.

For each fixed
\epsilon>0
, the function
F_{\epsilon}
belongs to

L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{N},\,V_{i,1})
. Moreover,
G
is Hölder, since
P
and

H^{-1}
are Hölder.

Then the preceding expression is

\lim_{m\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}G(A^{k_{m}}q)F_{\epsilon}(q)\,d\mathfrak{m}(q).

By definition

B_{i}:=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}G(q)\,d\mathfrak{m}(q)=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}P(H^{-1}q)\,d\mathfrak{m}(q).

Then
G-B_{i}
has zero average. By the mixing property of
A
, applied componentwise, we have

\lim_{m\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}G(A^{k_{m}}q)F_{\epsilon}(q)\,d\mathfrak{m}(q)=B_{i}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}F_{\epsilon}(q)\,d\mathfrak{m}(q)\Big).

Finally, using again
(H^{-1})_{*}\mathfrak{m}=\kappa\,\mathfrak{m}
, we get

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}F_{\epsilon}(q)\,d\mathfrak{m}(q)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(z)(\mathcal{U}_{z})\Psi_{\epsilon}(\Phi_{k}^{-1}z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(z)

\displaystyle=\int_{O_{\epsilon}}\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(\Phi_{k}(t,w,y))\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}\bigr)\omega(t)\phi_{1}^{\epsilon}(w)\phi_{2}^{\epsilon}(y)\,d\widehat{\mu}.

Therefore,

(6.29)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\epsilon}=c_{\epsilon}B_{i}\int_{O_{\epsilon}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(\Phi_{k}(t,w,y))\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{\Phi_{k}(t,w,y)}\bigr)\omega(t)\phi_{1}^{\epsilon}(w)\phi_{2}^{\epsilon}(y)\,d\widehat{\mu}.

Passing to the limit
\epsilon\to 0
, we obtain

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathcal{Z}_{\epsilon}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}B_{i}\circ\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(\Phi_{k}(t,0,0))\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{\Phi_{k}(t,0,0)}\bigr)\omega(t)\,dt

(6.30)

\displaystyle=\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}B_{i}\circ\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(\gamma(t))\bigl(\gamma^{\prime}(t)\bigr)\omega(t)\,dt.

Combining (
6.7.4
), (
6.7.4
), and (
6.7.4
), we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{U},\epsilon}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}p_{i}(\gamma^{\prime}(t))\omega(t)\,dt-\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}B_{i}\circ\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(\gamma(t))\bigl(\gamma^{\prime}(t)\bigr)\omega(t)\,dt

\displaystyle=\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\mathcal{T}_{i}(\gamma(t))\bigl(\gamma^{\prime}(t)\bigr)\omega(t)\,dt

This proves (
6.22
).

6.7.5.
Step 4: Conclusion

It follows from (
6.13
), (
6.7.2
) and (
6.15
) that
we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}H_{i}(\gamma(t))\omega^{\prime}(t)\,dt

\displaystyle=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{V},\epsilon}-\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}p_{i}(\gamma^{\prime}(t))\omega(t)\,dt

\displaystyle=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{U},\epsilon}-\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}p_{i}(\gamma^{\prime}(t))\omega(t)\,dt

\displaystyle=\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\mathcal{T}_{i}(\gamma(t))(\gamma^{\prime}(t))\omega(t)\,dt-\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}p_{i}(\gamma^{\prime}(t))\omega(t)\,dt

\displaystyle=-\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}B_{i}\circ\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(\gamma(t))\bigl(\gamma^{\prime}(t)\bigr)\omega(t)\,dt,

\displaystyle=-\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\mathcal{D}_{i}(\gamma(t))(\gamma^{\prime}(t))\omega(t)\,dt.

By Remark
6.4
, the left-hand side agrees with

\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}H(\gamma(t))\omega^{\prime}(t)\,dt.

Therefore Lemma
6.3
follows.

7.
Partial
C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
regularity of
H
along
\mathcal{W}^{f}_{i}

We recall the following notation:

(1)

The foliations
\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f}
and
\mathcal{W}_{i}^{A}
are defined in
(
2
) of Section
3.1
.

(2)

The subbundle
\mathcal{F}_{i,1}
is defined in (
5
) of
Section
3.1
.

(3)

The maps
A_{i}
and
p_{i}
are defined in Section
3.4
.

(4)

\mathcal{C}_{i,1}
is the bundle map given by
Theorem
5.1
.

(5)

B_{i}:V_{i,1}\to E_{i}
is the linear map
defined in Proposition
6.1
.

(6)

H_{i}
is defined in (
7
) of Section
6.1
.

7.1.
Main result

Theorem 7.1
.

Suppose
H(\mathcal{W}^{f}_{i})=\mathcal{W}^{A}_{i}
for some
i_{0}\leq i\leq\ell
. Then:

(1)

\mathcal{F}_{i,1}
is uniquely integrable to a foliation with
uniformly
C^{1+\alpha}
leaves, denoted by

\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{F}_{i,1}}
.

(2)

H
is a
C^{1+\alpha}
diffeomorphism along

\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{F}_{i,1}}
. Moreover, for every

x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
and
u\in\mathcal{F}_{i,1}(x)
,

D(H_{i})_{x}(u)=B_{i}\circ\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(x)(u).

In addition,
B_{i}
is injective.

(3)

The subspace
W:=B_{i}(V_{i,1})

is
A_{i}
-invariant, and
H(\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{F}_{i,1}})=W^{L}
, where
W^{L}
denotes the linear foliation tangent to

W
.

Remark 7.2
.

Since
H(\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f})=\mathcal{W}_{i}^{A}
, the components
p_{j}\circ H
,

j\neq i
, are constant along the leaves of
\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f}
. In
particular, they are constant along curves tangent to
\mathcal{F}_{i,1}
.
Therefore, for every
u\in\mathcal{F}_{i,1}(x)
,

p_{j}(DH_{x}(u))=0\quad(j\neq i),\qquad p_{i}(DH_{x}(u))=D(H_{i})_{x}(u).

Equivalently, after identifying
E_{i}\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
, we have

DH_{x}(u)=D(H_{i})_{x}(u),\qquad u\in\mathcal{F}_{i,1}(x).

7.2.
Role of Theorem
7.1

The partial differentiability obtained in Theorem
7.1
, together with
the
A_{i}
-invariant subspace
W=B_{i}(V_{i,1})
, plays a crucial role in the
proof of Theorem
8.1
. In particular, the Diophantine property of
W

is used to control the negative-time distributional series

h_{i}^{-}:=-\sum_{j=-1}^{-\infty}A_{i}^{-(j+1)}R_{i}\circ f^{j}.

More precisely, the partial
C^{1+\alpha}
regularity of
H^{-1}
along the
linear foliation tangent to
W
, together with the Diophantine property of

W
, allows us to prove that
h_{i}^{-}
is a well-defined distribution and that

h_{i}^{-}=h_{i}\qquad\text{as distributions}.

This identity is the key step in proving the equality of the positive- and
negative-time distributions in Theorem
8.1
.

7.3.
Proof of Theorem
7.1

Throughout the proof, we write
\mathcal{F}:=\mathcal{F}_{i,1}
for simplicity. By Proposition
6.1
,
H
is curve differentiable along
\mathcal{F}
,
and

dH_{x}^{\mathfrak{c}}=\mathcal{D}_{i}(x)=B_{i}\circ\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(x),\qquad x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}.

Applying the curve derivative to the conjugacy equation

H\circ f=A\circ H

along
\mathcal{F}
, we have

(7.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{i}(fx)\circ Df|_{\mathcal{F}_{x}}=A_{i}\circ\mathcal{D}_{i}(x),\qquad\forall\,x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}.

Since

\ker\mathcal{D}_{i}(x)=\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(x)^{-1}(\ker B_{i}),

it is enough to prove that
\ker B_{i}=\{0\}
. Suppose, by contradiction, that
there exists
0\neq v\in\ker B_{i}
. Define a local vector
field
X
tangent to
\mathcal{F}
by

X_{x}:=\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(x)^{-1}(v).

By
Peano’s existence theorem, there exists a local integral curve
\gamma
of

X
. Moreover, since
X
is Hölder and nonvanishing,
\gamma
is a nonconstant
regular curve along
\mathcal{F}
. Along this curve,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}(H\circ\gamma)(t)=\mathcal{D}_{i}(\gamma(t))(\gamma^{\prime}(t))=B_{i}\circ\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(\gamma(t))\bigl(\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(\gamma(t))^{-1}(v)\bigr)=B_{i}v=0.

Hence
H\circ\gamma
is constant, contradicting the fact that
H
is a
homeomorphism and
\gamma
is nonconstant. Therefore
\ker B_{i}=\{0\}
, and consequently

\mathcal{D}_{i}(x)
is injective on
\mathcal{F}_{x}
for every
x
.

Since
\mathcal{D}_{i}(x)
is continuous and
\mathcal{F}
is a continuous
bundle over the compact space
\mathbb{T}^{N}
, the injectivity is uniform. Then

(7.2)

\|\mathcal{D}_{i}(x)(u)\|\geq C^{-1}\|u\|,\qquad x\in\mathbb{T}^{N},\quad u\in\mathcal{F}_{x}.

Define

W:=B_{i}(V_{i,1})\subset E_{i}.

Then, for every
x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
,

\mathcal{D}_{i}(x)(\mathcal{F}_{x})=W.

Using (
7.1
), we see that

\displaystyle A_{i}W=A_{i}\mathcal{D}_{i}(x)(\mathcal{F}_{x})=\mathcal{D}_{i}(fx)(Df_{x}\mathcal{F}_{x})=\mathcal{D}_{i}(fx)(\mathcal{F}_{fx})=W.

Hence
W
is
A_{i}
-invariant.

Let
W^{L}
be the linear foliation tangent to
W
. We define

\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{F}}(x):=H^{-1}\bigl(W^{L}(H(x))\bigr).

Since
H
is
a homeomorphism, the sets
\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{F}}(x)
form a topological
foliation. We now show that this foliation is tangent to
\mathcal{F}
and has
uniformly
C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
leaves.

For each
x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
, the map
\mathcal{D}_{i}:\mathcal{F}_{x}\to W
is a linear isomorphism. By (
7.2
), its inverse is uniformly bounded.
Since
\mathcal{D}_{i}(x)
is
\alpha
-Hölder, the family of
inverses

\bigl(\mathcal{D}_{i}(x)\bigr)^{-1}:W\to\mathcal{F}_{x}

is also
\alpha
-
Hölder
in
x
. Fix
v\in W
. Define a vector field
X_{v}
on
\mathbb{T}^{N}
by

X_{v}(x):=\bigl(\mathcal{D}_{i}(x)\bigr)^{-1}(v).

Then
X_{v}
is
\alpha
-Hölder and tangent to
\mathcal{F}
. Let

\gamma
be a local integral curve of
X_{v}
, with
\gamma(0)=x
. Then

\frac{d}{dt}(H\circ\gamma)(t)=\mathcal{D}_{i}(\gamma(t))(\gamma^{\prime}(t))=\mathcal{D}_{i}(\gamma(t))(X_{v}(\gamma(t)))=v.

Therefore, for
t
sufficiently small,

H(\gamma(t))=H(x)+tv.

Equivalently,

H^{-1}(H(x)+tv)=\gamma(t).

Thus
H^{-1}
is differentiable along the affine
W
-leaves, and

D(H^{-1})_{H(x)}|_{W}=\bigl(\mathcal{D}_{i}(x)\bigr)^{-1}.

Since the right-hand side is
\alpha
-Hölder in
x
, it follows
that
H^{-1}
is
C^{1+\alpha}
along affine
W
-leaves.
Consequently, the leaves of

\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{F}}(x)=H^{-1}(H(x)+W)

are uniformly
C^{1+\alpha}
and tangent to
\mathcal{F}
. Since
H

maps each leaf of
\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{F}}
diffeomorphically onto an
affine
W
-leaf,
H
is
C^{1+\alpha}
along

\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{F}}
and

(7.3)

\displaystyle DH_{x}|_{\mathcal{F}_{x}}=\mathcal{D}_{i}(x).

It remains to prove unique integrability. Let
\theta
be any
C^{1}
curve tangent to
\mathcal{F}
. By (
7.3
),
H\circ\theta
is differentiable and

\frac{d}{dt}(H\circ\theta)(t)=\mathcal{D}_{i}(\theta(t))(\theta^{\prime}(t))\in W.

Hence
H\circ\theta
is contained in the affine subspace
H(\theta(0))+W
. Therefore

\theta(t)\in H^{-1}(H(\theta(0))+W)=\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{F}}(\theta(0))

for all
t
. This proves unique integrability of
\mathcal{F}
.

Taking
\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{F}_{i,1}}:=\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{F}}
,
we obtain the desired foliation. Moreover,
H
maps each leaf of

\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{F}_{i,1}}
onto an affine leaf parallel to the fixed

A_{i}
-invariant subspace
W
, and
H
is

C^{1+\alpha}
along
\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{F}_{i,1}}
. This
completes the proof.

8.
Invariant distributions along
\mathcal{E}_{i}

We recall the following notation:

(1)

H
is a bi-
\eta
-Hölder conjugacy; see Section
3
.

(2)

\mathfrak{m}
and
\mu
are defined in Section
3.1
.

(3)

The numbers
\rho_{i}
, the Lyapunov blocks
\mathcal{E}_{i}
, and the
linear subspaces
E_{i}
are defined in Section
3.1
.

(4)

The maps
A_{i}
and
p_{i}
are defined in Section
3.4
.

(5)

The foliations
\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f}
and
\mathcal{W}_{i}^{A}
are defined in
(
2
) of Section
3.1
.

(6)

The subbundle
\mathcal{F}_{i,j}
is defined in (
5
) of
Section
3.1
.

(7)

H_{i}
is defined in (
7
) of Section
6.1
.

Theorem 8.1
.

Suppose that
H(\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f})=\mathcal{W}_{i}^{A}
for some

i_{0}\leq i\leq\ell
. Let

0<\beta<1
. If
\mathcal{V}
is an
\alpha
-Hölder vector
field on
\mathbb{T}^{N}
taking values in
\mathcal{E}_{i}
, then the following
formulas define
E_{i}
-valued bounded linear functionals on

C^{\beta}(\mathbb{T}^{N})
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{V}}^{+}(\omega)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{H^{-1}z}\bigl(\mathcal{V}_{H^{-1}z}\bigr)\,\omega(z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(z),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{V}}^{-}(\omega)

\displaystyle:=-\sum_{m=-1}^{-\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{H^{-1}z}\bigl(\mathcal{V}_{H^{-1}z}\bigr)\,\omega(z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(z),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{+}(\omega)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{z}\bigl(\mathcal{V}_{z}\bigr)\,\omega(z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(z),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{-}(\omega)

\displaystyle:=-\sum_{m=-1}^{-\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{z}\bigl(\mathcal{V}_{z}\bigr)\,\omega(z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(z).

More precisely, for every
\omega\in C^{\beta}(\mathbb{T}^{N})
,

\max\{\|\mathfrak{D}^{+}_{\mathcal{V}}(\omega)\|,\|\mathfrak{D}^{-}_{\mathcal{V}}(\omega)\|,\|\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{+}(\omega)\|,\|\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{-}(\omega)\|\}\leq C_{\beta}\|\mathcal{V}\|_{C^{\alpha}}\|\omega\|_{C^{\beta}}.

Moreover,

\mathfrak{D}^{+}_{\mathcal{V}}=\mathfrak{D}^{-}_{\mathcal{V}},\quad\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{+}=\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{-}

as
E_{i}
-valued distributions on
C^{\beta}(\mathbb{T}^{N})
.

We understand the integrals in the theorem componentwise. More precisely, if

g
is vector-valued and
\varphi
is scalar-valued, then
g\varphi

denotes their componentwise product, and the integral of
g\varphi
is taken
componentwise.

Remark 8.2
.

We will see that

D_{\mathcal{V}}h_{i}=\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{+},\qquad D_{\mathcal{V}}h_{i}^{-}=\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{-}

as distributions, where
h_{i}^{-}
is defined in (
8.1
).

The distributions
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{V}}^{\pm}
are the conjugated versions
of
