\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{\pm}
under the Franks–Manning
conjugacy
H
. More precisely, let
\kappa
denote the density determined by

H^{-1}_{*}\mathfrak{m}=\kappa\,\mathfrak{m}.

Equivalently,

H_{*}\mathfrak{m}=(\kappa\circ H^{-1})^{-1}\mathfrak{m}.

Then, for every test function
\omega\in C^{\beta}(\mathbb{T}^{N})
,

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{V}}^{\pm}(\omega)=\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{\pm}\bigl((\omega\circ H)\kappa\bigr).

Indeed, this follows from the change of variables
z=H(x)
:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{H^{-1}z}\bigl(\mathcal{V}_{H^{-1}z}\bigr)\omega(z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(z)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{x}\bigl(\mathcal{V}_{x}\bigr)(\omega\circ H)(x)\kappa(x)\,d\mathfrak{m}(x).

Thus
\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{\pm}
are the distributions in the
original
f
-coordinates, and they are the ones directly related to the
distributional derivative of
H_{i}
. The distributions

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{V}}^{\pm}
are introduced because, after conjugating by

H
, the stable and unstable foliations become the linear foliations of
A
.
This allows us to use translations on the linear torus and to apply the
distribution-to-Hölder criterion to the candidate derivative in the proof of
Theorem
9.1
.

8.1.
Role of Theorem
8.1

Theorem
8.1
is the main distributional input in the proof of
Theorem
9.1
. It identifies the positive- and negative-time
distributional expressions for the candidate derivative of
H_{i}
. Together
with the triangular reduction of
Df|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}}
(see Theorem
5.1
), this equality allows
us to obtain stable-direction translation estimates from

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{V}}^{+}
and unstable-direction translation estimates
from
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{V}}^{-}
on the linear side. Since

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{V}}^{+}=\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{V}}^{-},

these one-sided estimates combine to give the two-sided estimates required by
the distribution-to-Hölder criterion. This is what ultimately shows that the distribution

D_{\mathcal{V}}h_{i}
is in fact a Hölder map.

8.2.
Proof strategy for Theorem
8.1

The positive-time series are controlled directly by an averaging estimate along
the foliation
\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f}
. More precisely, Lemma
8.5
gives
summability for local test functions in foliation boxes, and
Corollary
8.6
converts this local estimate into a global estimate.
This proves that the positive-time distributions
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{V}}^{+}
and
\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{+}

are bounded
E_{i}
-valued linear maps on
C^{\beta}(\mathbb{T}^{N})
.

The negative-time series are more delicate. We first define formally

(8.1)

\displaystyle h_{i}^{-}=-\sum_{j=-1}^{-\infty}A_{i}^{-(j+1)}R_{i}\circ f^{j}.

The key point is to prove that this negative-time expression represents the
same distribution as
h_{i}
. To do this, we conjugate by
H
and show that

h_{i}^{-}\circ H^{-1}=h_{i}\circ H^{-1}

as distributions. The proof uses a two-sided telescoping identity coming from
the cohomological equation

A_{i}h_{i}-h_{i}\circ f=R_{i}.

The positive endpoint of the telescoping sum converges to zero by expansion of

A_{i}
. The negative endpoint is handled using the partial

C^{1+\alpha}
regularity of
H^{-1}
along the linear foliation tangent to the
subspace
W\subset E_{i}
obtained in Theorem
7.1
. The Diophantine
property of
W
then allows us to integrate by parts against Fourier modes and
show that the negative endpoint also tends to zero. This gives

h_{i}^{-}\circ H^{-1}=h_{i}\circ H^{-1}.

Changing variables back through
H
, we obtain
h_{i}^{-}=h_{i}
as distributions. This is one place where irreducibility of
A
is used in an essential way: it guarantees the Diophantine property for the invariant subspaces that arise in the proof. A new feature of the proof is that the exponential mixing required to realize the formal negative-time
expression as a distribution is
not obtained from standard decay of correlations. Instead, partial

C^{1+\alpha}
regularity along a Diophantine linear foliation is combined
with a Fourier integration-by-parts argument. The Diophantine property controls
the small divisors with only polynomial loss, while the contraction of the
linear dynamics along the same foliation provides the exponential gain needed
to control the twisted negative-time series; see Remark
8.8
.

We then take distributional derivatives along an
\alpha
-Hölder vector field

\mathcal{V}
. By the positive-time expansion of
h_{i}
, we have

D_{\mathcal{V}}h_{i}=\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{+},

while by the negative-time expansion of
h_{i}^{-}
,

D_{\mathcal{V}}h_{i}^{-}=\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{-}.

Since
h_{i}^{-}=h_{i}
, these derivatives coincide. Hence
\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{+}=\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{-}
. This also gives boundedness of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{-}
. Finally, the conjugated distributions satisfy

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{V}}^{\pm}(\omega)=\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{\pm}\bigl((\omega\circ H)\kappa\bigr),

where
H^{-1}_{*}\mathfrak{m}=\kappa\,\mathfrak{m}
. Since
(\omega\circ H)\kappa\in C^{\beta\eta}(\mathbb{T}^{N})
,
the equality for
\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{\pm}
, applied with
the exponent
\beta\eta
, gives
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{V}}^{+}=\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{V}}^{-}
.
The same change-of-variables relation and the boundedness of the

\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{\pm}
give the estimates for

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{V}}^{\pm}
. This proves the boundedness and equality
claims in Theorem
8.1
.

8.3.
Notations and basic facts

8.3.1.
Diophantine subspace

Suppose
L
is a subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{N}
. We say that
L
has Diophantine property, i.e., for any
0\neq m\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}
and any basis
\{v_{1},\cdots,v_{\dim L}\}
of
L
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\dim L}|m\cdot v_{i}|\geq C_{v_{1},\cdots,v_{\dim L}}\lVert m\rVert^{-N}.

We will use the following arithmetic fact later in the proof.

Lemma 8.3

(Katznelson’s Lemma)
.

Let
P
be a
N\times N
integer matrix.
Assume that
\mathbb{R}^{N}
splits as
\mathbb{R}^{N}=V_{1}\oplus V_{2}
, where
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
are invariant under
P
,
and
P|_{V_{1}}
and
P|_{V_{2}}
have no common eigenvalues. If
V_{1}\cap\mathbb{Z}^{N}=\{0\}
, then
there exists a constant
K
such that

\displaystyle dist(n,V_{1})\geq K\lVert n\rVert^{-N}\quad\text{for all }\,0\neq n\in\mathbb{Z}^{N},

where
\lVert n\rVert
denotes
Euclidean norm and
dist
is Euclidean distance.

Lemma 8.4
.

If
L
is an
A
-invariant subspace, then
L
has the Diophantine property.

Proof.

Since
L
is
A
-invariant,
L^{\perp}
is invariant under
A^{\tau}
, the transpose of
A
. We claim that

L^{\perp}\cap\mathbb{Z}^{N}=\{0\}.

Indeed, if
0\neq z\in L^{\perp}\cap\mathbb{Z}^{N}
, then the rational span of the

A^{\tau}
-orbit of
z
gives a nonzero proper rational
A^{\tau}
-invariant
subspace. This contradicts the irreducibility of
A^{\tau}
, which is equivalent
to the irreducibility of
A
.

We have a generalize eigenspace decomposition of
A^{\tau}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{N}=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Delta}V_{\lambda},

where

\Delta
denotes the set of eigenvalues of
A
. Since
A^{\tau}
is irreducible, each
V_{\lambda}
is minimal, i.e.,
V_{\lambda}
has no nontrivial
A^{\tau}
-invariant subspace. In fact,
each
V_{\lambda}
is one or two dimensional and
A^{\tau}|_{V_{\lambda}}
is conformal. Let
\Delta_{L}
denote the set of eigenvalues of
A^{\tau}|_{L^{\bot}}
. Let
L^{\prime}
be the subspace spanned by generalized eigenvectors corresponding to eigenvalues inside
\Delta\backslash\Delta_{L^{\bot}}
. It is clear that
L^{\prime}
is also an invariant subspace of
A^{\tau}
and

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{N}=L^{\bot}\bigoplus L^{\prime}.

Moreover, the restrictions of
A^{\tau}
to
L^{\perp}
and
L^{\prime}
have no common
eigenvalues.

Applying Lemma
8.3
to
P=A^{\tau}
,
V_{1}=L^{\perp}
, and

V_{2}=L^{\prime}
, we obtain

(8.2)

\displaystyle d(m,L^{\bot})\geq C_{L}\lVert m\rVert^{-N},\qquad\forall\,\,0\neq m\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}.

Let
p_{L}
denote the projection to
L
. Then for any
0\neq m\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\dim L}|m\cdot v_{i}|

\displaystyle\geq C_{v_{1},\cdots,v_{\dim L}}\lVert p_{L}(m)\rVert=C_{v_{1},\cdots,v_{\dim L}}d(m,L^{\bot})

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{\geq}C_{v_{1},\cdots,v_{\dim L},1}\lVert m\rVert^{-N}.

Here in
(1)
we use (
8.2
). Hence, we finish the proof.
∎

8.3.2.
Decay estimate

We recall notations in Section
6.6.2
. In this subsection, we prove two decay estimates that will be used later.
The proof is similar to that of Lemma
6.6
, but we include the details
for completeness. As in Remark
6.5
, we use the product measure
dx\,d\nu_{k}(y)
in the foliation box, rather than the full foliation-box measure

\displaystyle J_{k}(x,y)\,dx\,d\nu_{k}(y).

The Jacobian factor
J_{k}(x,y)
can be absorbed into the test function when one compares the product measure with
\mathfrak{m}
.

Lemma 8.5
.

Fix a foliation chart
(\Gamma_{k},\,O_{k})
for
\mathcal{W}^{f}_{i}
. Let
\mathcal{Q}
be an
\alpha
-Hölder vector field on

\Gamma_{k}(O_{k})
taking values in
\mathcal{E}_{i}
. Let
\varphi
be a function compactly
supported on
O_{k}
, which is
\beta
-Hölder in the
leaf variable
x
, uniformly in
y
. Then, for every
m\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\Big\|\int_{O_{k}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{\Gamma_{k}(x,y)}(\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma_{k}(x,y)})\varphi(x,y)dxd\nu_{k}(y)\Big\|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\beta}\rho_{i}^{-\frac{\varrho(m+1)}{2}}(|m|+1)^{\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}-1}\lVert R_{i}\rVert_{C^{1}}\lVert\mathcal{Q}\rVert_{C^{\alpha}}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{C^{\beta}_{x}},

where
\varrho=\min\{\alpha,\,\beta\}
.

Proof.

It follows from (
5
) of Section
3.1
and the fact that

A_{i}
is diagonalizable with all eigenvalues of modulus
\rho_{i}
(see Section
3.4
) that, for
every
m\geq 0
,

(8.3)

\displaystyle\lVert A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}}\rVert\leq C(|m|+1)^{\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}-1}\lVert R_{i}\rVert_{C^{1}}.

Set

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{j}_{\Gamma_{k}(x,y)}:=D\Gamma_{k}(x,y)(\partial_{x_{j}}),\qquad 1\leq j\leq\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}.

Since
\mathcal{Q}
takes values in
\mathcal{E}_{i}
, we can write

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma_{k}(x,y)}=\sum_{j=1}^{\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}}a_{j}(x,y)\mathcal{W}^{j}_{\Gamma_{k}(x,y)}.

The coefficients
a_{j}
are
\alpha
-
Hölder
in the leaf variables

x
, uniformly in
y
, and satisfy

\|a_{j}\|_{C^{0}}\leq C\|\mathcal{Q}\|_{C^{0}},\qquad\|a_{j}\|_{C^{\alpha}_{x}}\leq C\|\mathcal{Q}\|_{C^{\alpha}}.

For each
j
, define

b_{j}(x,y):=a_{j}(x,y)\varphi(x,y).

Then
b_{j}
is
\varrho
-Hölder in the leaf variable
x
, uniformly in
y
,
and

\|b_{j}\|_{C^{\varrho}_{x}}\leq C_{\beta}\|\mathcal{Q}\|_{C^{\alpha}}\|\varphi\|_{C^{\beta}_{x}}.

We smooth
b_{j}
only in the leaf variables
x
, treating
y
as a parameter.
Let
b_{j,\eta}
be the corresponding
C^{\infty}
approximation as in Section
6.6.4
. Since

\varphi
is compactly supported in
O_{k}
, after extending
b_{j}
by zero in
the leaf variables and taking
\eta
sufficiently small, there are no boundary
terms in the integration by parts below. The standard smoothing estimates give

(8.4)

\displaystyle\|b_{j,\eta}-b_{j}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\beta}\eta^{\varrho}\|\mathcal{Q}\|_{C^{\alpha}}\|\varphi\|_{C^{\beta}_{x}},

(8.5)

\displaystyle\|b_{j,\eta}\|_{C^{1}_{x}}

\displaystyle\leq C\eta^{-1}\|\mathcal{Q}\|_{C^{0}}\|\varphi\|_{C^{0}}.

We split

\displaystyle\int_{O_{k}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{\Gamma_{k}(x,y)}(\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma_{k}(x,y)})\varphi(x,y)dxd\nu_{k}(y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}}\int_{O_{k}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{\Gamma_{k}(x,y)}(\mathcal{W}^{j}_{\Gamma_{k}(x,y)})b_{j}(x,y)dxd\nu_{k}(y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}}\mathcal{X}_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}}\mathcal{Z}_{j},

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{j}

\displaystyle=\int_{O_{k}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{\Gamma_{k}(x,y)}(\mathcal{W}^{j}_{\Gamma_{k}(x,y)})b_{j,\eta}(x,y)dxd\nu_{k}(y)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{j}

\displaystyle=\int_{O_{k}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{\Gamma_{k}(x,y)}(\mathcal{W}^{j}_{\Gamma_{k}(x,y)})(b_{j}-b_{j,\eta})(x,y)dxd\nu_{k}(y).

We first estimate
\|\mathcal{X}_{j}\|
. We note that

D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{\Gamma_{k}(x,y)}\bigl(\mathcal{W}^{j}_{\Gamma_{k}(x,y)}\bigr)=\partial_{x_{j}}(R_{i}\circ f^{m}\circ\Gamma_{k})(x,y).

Then we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{j}=-\int_{O_{k}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}(R_{i}\circ f^{m}\circ\Gamma_{k})(x,y)\big(\partial_{x_{j}}b_{j,\eta}(x,y)\big)dxd\nu_{k}(y).

Hence,

\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{X}_{j}\rVert

\displaystyle\leq C\lVert A_{i}^{-(m+1)}\rVert\lVert R_{i}\rVert_{C^{0}}\lVert b_{j,\eta}\rVert_{C^{1}_{x}}\overset{\text{(1)}}{\leq}C\eta^{-1}\rho_{i}^{-(m+1)}\lVert R_{i}\rVert_{C^{0}}\|\mathcal{Q}\|_{C^{0}}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{C^{0}}.

Here in
(1)
we use (
8.5
).

We next estimate each
\lVert\mathcal{Z}_{j}\rVert
. It follows from (
8.3
) and (
8.4
) that

\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{Z}_{j}\rVert

\displaystyle\leq C\big\|A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}}\big\|\cdot\lVert b_{j,\eta}-b_{j}\rVert_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\beta}\eta^{\varrho}(|m|+1)^{\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}-1}\lVert R_{i}\rVert_{C^{1}}\lVert\mathcal{Q}\rVert_{C^{\alpha}}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{C^{\beta}_{x}}.

Combining the estimates for
\mathcal{X}_{j}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{j}
, and summing
over
1\leq j\leq\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Big\|\int_{O_{k}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{\Gamma_{k}(x,y)}(\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma_{k}(x,y)})\varphi(x,y)dxd\nu_{k}(y)\Big\|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\beta}\big(\eta^{-1}\rho_{i}^{-(m+1)}+\eta^{\varrho}(|m|+1)^{\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}-1}\big)\lVert R_{i}\rVert_{C^{1}}\lVert\mathcal{Q}\rVert_{C^{\alpha}}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{C^{\beta}_{x}}.

Let
\eta=\rho_{i}^{-\frac{m+1}{2}}
. Then

\displaystyle\rho_{i}^{-(m+1)}\eta^{-1}=\rho_{i}^{-\frac{m+1}{2}},\quad\eta^{\varrho}=\rho_{i}^{-\frac{\varrho(m+1)}{2}},\quad\text{and}\quad\rho_{i}^{-\frac{m+1}{2}}\leq\rho_{i}^{-\frac{\varrho(m+1)}{2}}.

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{O_{k}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{\Gamma_{k}(x,y)}\bigl(\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma_{k}(x,y)}\bigr)\varphi(x,y)dxd\nu_{k}(y)\right\|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C_{\beta}\rho_{i}^{-\frac{\varrho(m+1)}{2}}(m+1)^{\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}-1}\|R_{i}\|_{C^{1}}\|\mathcal{Q}\|_{C^{\alpha}}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{C^{\beta}_{x}}.

This proves the lemma.
∎

The following is a direct consequence of Lemma
8.5
.

Corollary 8.6
.

Fix a foliation chart
(\Gamma_{k},\,O_{k})
for
\mathcal{W}^{f}_{i}
. Let
\mathcal{Q}
be an
\alpha
-Hölder vector field on

\Gamma_{k}(O_{k})
taking values in
\mathcal{E}_{i}
. Let
\varphi
be a function compactly
supported on
\Gamma_{k}(O_{k})
, such that
\varphi\circ\Gamma_{k}
is
\beta
-Hölder in the
leaf variable
x
, uniformly in
y
. Then, for every
m\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\Big\|\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{z}(\mathcal{Q}_{z})\varphi(z)d\mathfrak{m}\Big\|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\beta,k}\rho_{i}^{-\frac{\varrho(m+1)}{2}}(|m|+1)^{\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}-1}\lVert R_{i}\rVert_{C^{1}}\lVert\mathcal{Q}\rVert_{C^{\alpha}}\lVert\varphi\circ\Gamma_{k}\rVert_{C^{\beta}_{x}},

where
\varrho=\min\{\alpha,\,\beta\}
.

Proof.

By using the measure-change formula (
6.1
) in Section
6.6.2
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{z}(\mathcal{Q}_{z})\varphi(z)d\mathfrak{m}

\displaystyle=\int_{O_{k}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{\Gamma_{k}(x,y)}(\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma_{k}(x,y)})\varphi(\Gamma_{k}(x,y))J_{k}(x,y)dxd\nu_{k}(y).

The function

(\varphi\circ\Gamma_{k})(x,y)J_{k}(x,y)

is
\varrho
-Hölder in the leaf variable
x
, uniformly in
y
. Moreover,
since
J_{k}
is uniformly
\alpha
-Hölder in the leaf variable,

\displaystyle\lVert(\varphi\circ\Gamma_{k})J_{k}\rVert_{C^{\varrho}_{x}}\leq C\lVert\varphi\circ\Gamma_{k}\rVert_{C^{\beta}_{x}}\lVert J_{k}\rVert_{C^{\alpha}_{x}}.

The desired estimate now follows from Lemma
8.5
, with the part
\lVert J_{k}(x,y)\rVert_{C^{\alpha}_{x}}

absorbed into
C_{\beta,k}
.
∎

8.3.3.
A decay estimate from the Diophantine property

Recall the
A_{i}
-invariant subspace
W
defined in Theorem
7.1
. Let
C^{1+\alpha,W}(\mathbb{T}^{N})
denote the space of continuous functions

\psi
such that

\displaystyle\|\psi\|_{C^{1+\alpha,W}}

\displaystyle:=\|\psi\|_{C^{0}}+\sup_{w\in W,\ \|w\|=1}\|\partial_{w}\psi\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle+\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathbb{T}^{N},\ 0\neq v\in W\\
w\in W,\ \|w\|=1\end{subarray}}\frac{|\partial_{w}\psi(x+v)-\partial_{w}\psi(x)|}{\|v\|^{\alpha}}<\infty.

Lemma 8.7
.

Suppose
\xi:\mathbb{T}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
is
C^{1+\alpha}
along
W
. Then for any
l>0
and any
0\neq n\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\Big\|\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}\xi(A^{-l}z)e_{n}(z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(z)\Big\|\leq C\lVert n\rVert^{(1+\alpha)N}\rho_{i}^{-(\alpha+1)l}\|\xi\|_{C^{1+\alpha,W}}.

Proof.

Fix a basis
\{w_{1},\cdots,w_{\dim W}\}
of
W
.
It follows from Lemma
8.4
that
W
has Diophantine property. Hence, for each
0\neq n\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}
, there exists
j_{0}=j_{0}(n)
such that

(8.6)

\displaystyle|n\cdot w_{j_{0}}|\geq C_{W}\lVert n\rVert^{-N}.

Let

I_{l,n}:=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}\xi(A^{-l}z)e_{n}(z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(z).

Since

\partial_{w_{j_{0}}}e_{n}(z)=2\pi\mathbf{i}(n\cdot w_{j_{0}})e_{n}(z),

we have

\displaystyle I_{l,n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}(n\cdot w_{j_{0}})}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}\xi(A^{-l}z)\partial_{w_{j_{0}}}e_{n}(z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(z)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}(n\cdot w_{j_{0}})}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}D\xi_{A^{-l}z}(A^{-l}w_{j_{0}})e_{n}(z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(z).

Set

\displaystyle a_{l}:=\|A^{-l}w_{j_{0}}\|,\qquad\widetilde{w}_{l}:=\frac{A^{-l}w_{j_{0}}}{\|A^{-l}w_{j_{0}}\|},\qquad g_{l}:=\partial_{\widetilde{w}_{l}}\xi.

Then

(8.7)

\displaystyle I_{l,n}=-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}(n\cdot w_{j_{0}})}a_{l_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}g_{l_{2}}(A^{-l}z)e_{n}(z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(z).

Since
\xi
is
C^{1+\alpha}
along
W
,

\sup_{l\geq 1}\|g_{l}\|_{C^{\alpha,W}}<\infty.

Let
t=\frac{1}{2(n\cdot w_{j_{0}})}
. Then

e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\,t(n\cdot w_{j_{0}})}-1=-2.

Then

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}g_{l}(A^{-l}z)e_{n}(z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(z)

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{1}{e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\,t(n\cdot w_{j_{0}})}-1}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}\Big[g_{l}(A^{-l}(z-tw_{j_{0}}))-g_{l_{2}}(A^{-l}z)\Big]e_{n}(z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(z)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}\Big[g_{l}(A^{-l}(z-tw_{j_{0}}))-g_{l}(A^{-l}z)\Big]e_{n}(z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(z).

Therefore,

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}g_{l_{2}}(A^{-l}z)e_{n}(z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(z)\right\|

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{\leq}C\|g_{l}\|_{C^{\alpha,W}}\|tA^{-l}w_{j_{0}}\|^{\alpha}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}|n\cdot w_{j_{0}}|^{-\alpha}\|\xi\|_{C^{1+\alpha,W}}\|A^{-l}w_{j_{0}}\|^{\alpha}.

Here in
(1)
we note that
A^{-l}w_{j_{0}}\in W
since
W\subset E_{i}
is
A_{i}
-invariant.

This, together with (
8.7
) gives

\displaystyle\|I_{l,n}\|

\displaystyle\leq C|n\cdot w_{j_{0}}|^{-(1+\alpha)}\|\xi\|_{C^{1+\alpha,W}}\|A^{-l}w_{j_{0}}\|^{1+\alpha}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{\leq}C_{1}|n\cdot w_{j_{0}}|^{-(1+\alpha)}\rho_{i}^{-l(\alpha+1)}\|\xi\|_{C^{1+\alpha,W}}

(8.8)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(2)}}{\leq}C_{2}\lVert n\rVert^{(1+\alpha)N}\rho_{i}^{-l(\alpha+1)}\|\xi\|_{C^{1+\alpha,W}}.

Here in
(1)
we use (
3.4
) of Section
3.2
and the fact that
W\subset E_{i}
is
A_{i}
-invariant; in
(2)
we use (
8.6
).
∎

Remark 8.8
.

The Diophantine property of
W
is crucial in this argument. It allows us,
for each nonzero Fourier mode
n
, to choose a direction
w_{j_{0}}\in W

such that

|n\cdot w_{j_{0}}|\geq C\|n\|^{-N}.

Thus the small divisor
n\cdot w_{j_{0}}
has only polynomial loss in the
Fourier frequency.

Equivalently, this property allows us to solve the elementary equation

e_{n}(z)=\partial_{w_{j_{0}}}\bigl(\varphi(z+tw_{j_{0}})-\varphi(z)\bigr)

with controlled loss. Indeed, for
t=\frac{1}{2(n\cdot w_{j_{0}})}
, we have

e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\,t(n\cdot w_{j_{0}})}-1=-2,

and the function

\varphi(z)=\frac{e_{n}(z)}{2\pi\mathbf{i}(n\cdot w_{j_{0}})}\frac{1}{e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\,t(n\cdot w_{j_{0}})}-1}=-\frac{1}{2}\frac{e_{n}(z)}{2\pi\mathbf{i}(n\cdot w_{j_{0}})}

satisfies the desired identity.

Applying this identity and integrating by parts gives

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}\xi(A^{-l}z)e_{n}(z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(z)

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}\partial_{w_{j_{0}}}\bigl(\xi(A^{-l}z)\bigr)\bigl(\varphi(z+tw_{j_{0}})-\varphi(z)\bigr)\,d\mathfrak{m}(z).

The derivative
\partial_{w_{j_{0}}}\bigl(\xi(A^{-l}z)\bigr)
produces the
factor
\|A^{-l}w_{j_{0}}\|
, which is of order
\rho_{i}^{-l}
, because

W\subset E_{i}
is
A_{i}
-invariant. The finite-difference step then uses
the
C^{\alpha}
regularity of the leafwise derivative of
\xi
and produces
an additional factor of order
\rho_{i}^{-\alpha l}
. Together these two
effects give the decay rate
\rho_{i}^{-(1+\alpha)l}
.
After multiplication by the twist, whose norm grows at most like

\rho_{i}^{l}
, one still obtains the decay rate
\rho_{i}^{-\alpha l}
. This
is the key point that allows us to control the formal negative-time expression
and to identify it with
h_{i}\circ H^{-1}
as a distribution.

We emphasize that this estimate is not obtained from a standard exponential
mixing estimate for the toral automorphism
A
. Standard mixing estimates
usually require global Hölder regularity of the observables in order to
obtain exponential decay. Moreover, obtaining prescribed decay rates typically
requires controlling higher global regularity norms; see

[
26
,
5
]
. In the present argument, the available regularity is
only leafwise:
h_{i}\circ H^{-1}
is
C^{1+\alpha}
along the linear
foliation tangent to
W
. Thus the required decay follows from partial
leafwise regularity together with the Diophantine property of
W
, rather
than from a standard global mixing estimate.

8.4.
Proof of Theorem
8.1

Integrating the cohomological equation (
3.9
) of Section
3.4
with respect to
\mu
,
we get

\displaystyle A_{i}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}h_{i}\,d\mu-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}h_{i}\circ f\,d\mu=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}R_{i}\,d\mu.

Since
\mu
is
f
-invariant, this gives

\displaystyle A_{i}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}h_{i}d\mu-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}h_{i}d\mu=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}R_{i}d\mu.

Therefore, replacing
h_{i}
and
R_{i}
by

\displaystyle h_{i}-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}h_{i}d\mu\quad\text{and}\quad R_{i}-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}R_{i}d\mu

respectively, preserves the cohomological equation. This normalization does not affect the
argument below. Thus, from now on, we assume

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}h_{i}d\mu=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}R_{i}d\mu=0.

8.4.1.
Step 1: Construction of the negative-time distribution

Define formally

\displaystyle h_{i}^{-}\circ H^{-1}=-\sum_{j=-1}^{-\infty}A_{i}^{-(j+1)}R_{i}\circ f^{j}\circ H^{-1}.

In this step, we show that this series converges as a distribution and that

(8.9)

\displaystyle h_{i}^{-}\circ H^{-1}=h_{i}\circ H^{-1}\qquad\text{as distributions.}

First, we show that

(8.10)

\displaystyle\sum_{j=-\infty}^{\infty}A_{i}^{-(j+1)}R_{i}\circ f^{j}\circ H^{-1}=0

as a distribution.

From equation (
3.9
) of Section
3.4

for any
l_{1}\geq 0
and
l_{2}\geq 1
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{j=-l_{2}}^{l_{1}}A_{i}^{-(j+1)}R_{i}\circ f^{j}=A_{i}^{l_{2}}h_{i}\circ f^{-l_{2}}-A_{i}^{-(l_{1}+1)}h_{i}\circ f^{l_{1}+1}.

This together with the conjugacy equation (
3.2
) of Section
3
give

\displaystyle\sum_{j=-l_{2}}^{l_{1}}A_{i}^{-(j+1)}R_{i}\circ f^{j}\circ H^{-1}

\displaystyle=A_{i}^{l_{2}}(h_{i}\circ H^{-1})\circ A^{-l_{2}}-A_{i}^{-(l_{1}+1)}(h_{i}\circ H^{-1})\circ A^{l_{1}+1}.

Let

e_{n}(z):=e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\,n\cdot z},\qquad n\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}.

To prove (
8.10
), it suffices to show that for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}
, the two endpoint terms
converge to zero on each Fourier mode. The estimates below are polynomial in
the Fourier frequency
n
, and therefore this convergence extends from
Fourier modes to arbitrary
C^{\infty}
test functions.

(8.11)

\displaystyle\lim_{l_{1}\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}A_{i}^{-(l_{1}+1)}(h_{i}\circ H^{-1})(A^{l_{1}+1}z)e_{n}(z)d\mathfrak{m}=0,\qquad\text{and}

(8.12)

\displaystyle\lim_{l_{2}\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}A_{i}^{l_{2}}(h_{i}\circ H^{-1})(A^{-l_{2}}z)e_{n}(z)d\mathfrak{m}=0,

We recall (
3.4
) of Section
3.2
. Therefore (
8.11
) follows from

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}A_{i}^{-(l_{1}+1)}(h_{i}\circ H^{-1})(A^{l_{1}+1}z)e_{n}(z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(z)\right\|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C\rho_{i}^{-(l_{1}+1)}\|h_{i}\circ H^{-1}\|_{L^{1}}\to 0.

We now prove (
8.12
). If
n=0
, then by the normalization above, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}A_{i}^{l_{2}}(h_{i}\circ H^{-1})(A^{-l_{2}}z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(z)

\displaystyle=A_{i}^{l_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}h_{i}\circ H^{-1}\,d\mathfrak{m}=A_{i}^{l_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}h_{i}\,d\mu=0.

Now assume
0\neq n\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}
.

By Theorem
7.1
, there exists an
A_{i}
-invariant subspace

W\subset E_{i}
such that
H
maps the foliation

\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{F}_{i,1}}
onto the linear foliation
W^{L}
, and

H
is a
C^{1+\alpha}
diffeomorphism along these leaves. Hence
H^{-1}
is
C^{1+\alpha}
along

W
. Moreover, since

p_{i}\circ H=p_{i}+h_{i},

we have

h_{i}\circ H^{-1}(z)=p_{i}(z)-p_{i}\circ H^{-1}(z).

This identity is understood after choosing compatible local lifts. Since we
use it only to obtain leafwise regularity along the linear
W
-foliation,
the conclusion is independent of the choice of lift.

Since
p_{i}
is linear and
H^{-1}
is
C^{1+\alpha}
along

W
-leaves, it follows that
h_{i}\circ H^{-1}
is

C^{1+\alpha}
along
W
-leaves.

It follows from Lemma
8.7
, applied to

\xi=h_{i}\circ H^{-1}
, that for each
0\neq n\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}
,

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}(h_{i}\circ H^{-1})(A^{-l_{2}}z)e_{n}(z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(z)\right\|

\displaystyle\leq C\|n\|^{(1+\alpha)N}\rho_{i}^{-(1+\alpha)l_{2}}\|h_{i}\circ H^{-1}\|_{C^{1+\alpha,W}}.

Therefore

\displaystyle\Big\|\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}A_{i}^{l_{2}}(h_{i}\circ H^{-1})(A^{-l_{2}}z)e_{n}(z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(z)\Big\|

\displaystyle\leq\|A_{i}^{l_{2}}\|\left\|\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}(h_{i}\circ H^{-1})(A^{-l_{2}}z)e_{n}(z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(z)\right\|

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{\leq}C\rho_{i}^{l_{2}}\|n\|^{(1+\alpha)N}\rho_{i}^{-(\alpha+1)l_{2}}\|h_{i}\circ H^{-1}\|_{C^{1+\alpha,W}}

\displaystyle=C\|n\|^{(1+\alpha)N}\rho_{i}^{-\alpha l_{2}}\|h_{i}\circ H^{-1}\|_{C^{1+\alpha,W}}\to 0.

Here in
(1)
we use (
3.4
) of Section
3.2
.

This proves (
8.12
). Hence, we obtain (
8.10
). On the other hand, the positive-time expansion gives (see (
3.10
) of Section
3.4
)

h_{i}\circ H^{-1}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}A_{i}^{-(j+1)}R_{i}\circ f^{j}\circ H^{-1}.

Therefore,

h_{i}^{-}\circ H^{-1}=h_{i}\circ H^{-1}

as distributions. This proves (
8.9
).

Remark 8.9
.

By (
8.9
), the distribution
h_{i}^{-}\circ H^{-1}
extends
continuously to
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{N})
. More precisely, for every

\omega\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{N})
, we have

(h_{i}^{-}\circ H^{-1})(\omega)=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}h_{i}(H^{-1}z)\omega(z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(z).

Consequently,

\|(h_{i}^{-}\circ H^{-1})(\omega)\|\leq\|h_{i}\|_{C^{0}}\|\omega\|_{L^{\infty}}.

8.4.2.
Step 2: Definition of
h_{i}^{-}

Define formally

\displaystyle h_{i}^{-}=-\sum_{j=-1}^{-\infty}A_{i}^{-(j+1)}R_{i}\circ f^{j}.

In this step, we show that this series converges as a distribution and that

(8.13)

\displaystyle h_{i}^{-}=h_{i}

as bounded linear functionals on
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{N})
.
More precisely, for every

\omega\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{N})
, we have

h_{i}^{-}(\omega)=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}h_{i}(z)\omega(z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(z).

Consequently,

\|h_{i}^{-}(\omega)\|\leq\|h_{i}\|_{C^{0}}\|\omega\|_{L^{\infty}}.

Let
\omega\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{N})
. Set

\Psi_{\omega}(z):=\omega(H^{-1}z)\bigl(\kappa(H^{-1}z)\bigr)^{-1}.

We note that
\Psi_{\omega}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{N})
since
d\mu=\kappa\,d\mathfrak{m}
with
\kappa\in C^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{N})
and
\kappa>0
.

Then we have

\displaystyle h_{i}^{-}(\omega)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{j=-1}^{-\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}A_{i}^{-(j+1)}R_{i}\circ f^{j}(x)\omega(x)\,d\mathfrak{m}(x)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{=}-\sum_{j=-1}^{-\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}A_{i}^{-(j+1)}R_{i}\circ f^{j}\circ H^{-1}(z)\Psi_{\omega}(z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(z)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(2)}}{=}(h_{i}^{-}\circ H^{-1})(\Psi_{\omega})

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(3)}}{=}(h_{i}\circ H^{-1})(\Psi_{\omega})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}h_{i}(H^{-1}z)\Psi_{\omega}(z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(z)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(4)}}{=}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}h_{i}(x)\omega(x)\,d\mathfrak{m}(x).

Here, in
(1)
we use the change of variables
z=H(x)
and

\displaystyle H_{*}\mathfrak{m}=(\kappa\circ H^{-1})^{-1}\mathfrak{m};

in
(2)
, we use the fact that
\Psi_{\omega}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{N})
and
Remark
8.9
, which allows
h_{i}^{-}\circ H^{-1}
to be evaluated on

L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{N})
test functions; in
(3)
, we use (
8.9
) together with
Remark
8.9
; in
(4)
we recall that
d\mu=\kappa\,d\mathfrak{m}
and
H_{*}\mu=\mathfrak{m}
.

Hence
h_{i}^{-}=h_{i}
as bounded linear functionals on
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{N})
, and
hence also as distributions.

8.4.3.
Step 3: Equality of the local positive and negative distributions

We recall notations in Section
6.6.2
. Fix a foliation chart
(\Gamma_{k},\,O_{k})
for
\mathcal{W}^{f}_{i}
. Let
\mathcal{Q}
be an
\alpha
-Hölder vector field on

\Gamma_{k}(O_{k})
taking values in
\mathcal{E}_{i}
. Let
C^{c,\beta}_{x}(O_{k})
denote the space of functions
compactly supported in
O_{k}
that are
\beta
-Hölder in the leaf
variable
x
, uniformly in
y
. When
\beta=1
, we interpret

C^{c,1}_{x}(O_{k})
as the space of functions compactly supported in
O_{k}

that are
C^{1}
in the leaf variable
x
, uniformly in
y
.

It follows from Lemma
8.5
that for every
\varphi\in C^{c,\beta}_{x}(O_{k})
, the series

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}^{+}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\varphi):=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\int_{O_{k}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{\Gamma_{k}(x,y)}\big(\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma_{k}(x,y)})\varphi(x,y)dxd\nu_{k}(y)

is convergent and satisfies

(8.14)

\displaystyle\lVert\mathfrak{K}^{+}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\varphi)\rVert\leq C_{\beta}\lVert R_{i}\rVert_{C^{1}}\lVert\mathcal{Q}\rVert_{C^{\alpha}}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{C^{\beta}_{x}}.

In this step, we show that the series

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}^{-}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\varphi):=-\sum_{m=-1}^{-\infty}\int_{O_{k}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{\Gamma_{k}(x,y)}\big(\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma_{k}(x,y)})\varphi(x,y)dxd\nu_{k}(y)

is convergent as well for any
\varphi\in C^{c,\beta}_{x}(O_{k})
; and

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}^{-}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\varphi)=\mathfrak{K}^{+}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\varphi).

Set

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{j}_{\Gamma_{k}(x,y)}:=D\Gamma_{k}(x,y)(\partial_{x_{j}}),\qquad 1\leq j\leq\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}.

Firstly, we show that for any
1\leq j\leq\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}
and any
\varphi\in C^{c,1}_{x}(O_{k})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}^{-}_{\mathcal{W}^{j}}(\varphi)=\mathfrak{K}^{+}_{\mathcal{W}^{j}}(\varphi).

Moreover,

\displaystyle\Big\|\mathfrak{K}^{-}_{\mathcal{W}^{j}}(\varphi)\Big\|\leq C_{\beta}\lVert R_{i}\rVert_{C^{1}}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{C^{\beta}_{x}}.

for any
0<\beta<1
.

For
\varphi\in C_{x}^{c,1}(O_{k})
, the negative-time series satisfies

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}^{-}_{\mathcal{W}^{j}}(\varphi)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{m=-1}^{-\infty}\int_{O_{k}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{\Gamma_{k}(x,y)}\big(\mathcal{W}^{j}_{\Gamma_{k}(x,y)})\varphi(x,y)dxd\nu_{k}(y)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{m=-1}^{-\infty}\int_{O_{k}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}\partial_{x_{j}}\big(R_{i}\circ f^{m}\circ\Gamma_{k}(x,y)\big)\varphi(x,y)dxd\nu_{k}(y)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{=}\sum_{m=-1}^{-\infty}\int_{O_{k}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}R_{i}\circ f^{m}\circ\Gamma_{k}(x,y)\psi(x,y)dxd\nu_{k}(y)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(2)}}{=}\sum_{m=-1}^{-\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}R_{i}\circ f^{m}(z)\chi_{\psi}(z)d\mathfrak{m}

\displaystyle=-h_{i}^{-}(\chi_{\psi})

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(3)}}{=}-h_{i}(\chi_{\psi}).

We explain steps:

(1)

Set
\psi=\partial_{x_{j}}\varphi
. Then
\psi
is uniformly bounded and compactly supported on
O_{k}
.

(2)

We recall the measure-change formula (
6.1
) in Section
6.6.2
. Define

\chi_{\psi}(z):=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{\psi(\Gamma_{k}^{-1}z)}{J_{k}(\Gamma_{k}^{-1}z)},&z\in U_{k}=\Gamma_{k}(O_{k}),\\[11.99998pt]
0,&z\notin U_{k}.\end{cases}

Then
\chi_{\psi}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{N})
.

(3)

By step 2, (see Section
8.4.2
)
h_{i}^{-}=h_{i}
as bounded linear functionals on
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{N})
.

On the other hand, using (
3.10
) of Section
3.4
, we have

\displaystyle-h_{i}(\chi_{\psi})

\displaystyle=-\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}R_{i}\circ f^{m}(z)\chi_{\psi}(z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(z)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\int_{O_{k}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}R_{i}\circ f^{m}\circ\Gamma_{k}(x,y)\psi(x,y)\,dx\,d\nu_{k}(y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\int_{O_{k}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{\Gamma_{k}(x,y)}\bigl(\mathcal{W}^{j}_{\Gamma_{k}(x,y)}\bigr)\varphi(x,y)\,dx\,d\nu_{k}(y)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{K}_{\mathcal{W}^{j}}^{+}(\varphi).

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}_{\mathcal{W}^{j}}^{-}(\varphi)=\mathfrak{K}_{\mathcal{W}^{j}}^{+}(\varphi),\qquad\varphi\in C_{x}^{c,1}(O_{k}).

Combining this equality with (
8.14
), applied to

\mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{W}^{j}
, gives, for every
0<\beta<1
,

(8.15)

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{K}^{-}_{\mathcal{W}^{j}}(\varphi)\|\leq C_{\beta}\|R_{i}\|_{C^{1}}\|\varphi\|_{C^{\beta}_{x}}.

Since
C_{x}^{c,1}(O_{k})
is dense in
C_{x}^{c,\beta}(O_{k})
in the

C_{x}^{\beta}
-topology, the estimate (
8.15
) allows

\mathfrak{K}^{-}_{\mathcal{W}^{j}}
to extend uniquely to

C_{x}^{c,\beta}(O_{k})
. Moreover,

(8.16)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}_{\mathcal{W}^{j}}^{-}(\varphi)=\mathfrak{K}_{\mathcal{W}^{j}}^{+}(\varphi),\qquad\varphi\in C_{x}^{c,\beta}(O_{k}).

Now let
\mathcal{Q}
be an
\alpha
-Hölder vector field taking
values in
\mathcal{E}_{i}
. We can write

\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma_{k}(x,y)}=\sum_{j=1}^{\dim\mathcal{E}_{i}}a_{j}(x,y)\mathcal{W}^{j}_{\Gamma_{k}(x,y)},

where the coefficients
a_{j}
are
\alpha
-Hölder in the leaf
variable
x
, uniformly in
y
. Hence, if
\varphi\in C_{x}^{c,\beta}(O_{k})
, then

a_{j}\varphi\in C_{x}^{c,\varrho}(O_{k})
, where
\varrho=\min\{\alpha,\beta\}
.
Since
a_{j}\varphi
is
\varrho
-Hölder in the leaf
variable, (
8.16
) gives

\mathfrak{K}^{-}_{\mathcal{W}^{j}}(a_{j}\varphi)=\mathfrak{K}^{+}_{\mathcal{W}^{j}}(a_{j}\varphi)

for every
j
. Therefore

\mathfrak{K}^{-}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\varphi)=\mathfrak{K}^{+}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\varphi).

The convergence and the estimate for
\mathfrak{K}^{-}_{\mathcal{Q}}
follow
from (
8.14
).

8.4.4.
Step 4: Equality of the global positive and negative distributions

We recall notations in Section
6.6.2
. Fix a foliation chart
(\Gamma_{k},\,O_{k})
for
\mathcal{W}^{f}_{i}
.
Let
\mathcal{Q}
be an
\alpha
-
Hölder
vector field on

\Gamma_{k}(O_{k})
taking values in
\mathcal{E}_{i}
. Let
\varphi
be a function compactly supported in

\Gamma_{k}(O_{k})
, and assume that
\varphi\circ\Gamma_{k}
is

\beta
-
Hölder
in the leaf variable
x
, uniformly in
y
. From Corollary
8.6
the series

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{+}(\varphi):=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{z}(\mathcal{Q}_{z})\,\varphi(z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(z)

is convergent and satisfies

(8.17)

\displaystyle\lVert\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{+}(\varphi)\rVert\leq C_{\beta}\lVert R_{i}\rVert_{C^{1}}\lVert\mathcal{Q}\rVert_{C^{\alpha}}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{C^{\beta}}.

In this part, we show that the series

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{-}(\varphi):=-\sum_{m=-1}^{-\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{z}(\mathcal{Q}_{z})\,\varphi(z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(z)

is convergent as well; and satisfies

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{-}(\varphi)=\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{+}(\varphi).

By using the measure-change formula (
6.1
) in Section
6.6.2
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{z}(\mathcal{Q}_{z})\varphi(z)d\mathfrak{m}

\displaystyle=\int_{O_{k}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{\Gamma_{k}(x,y)}(\mathcal{Q}_{\Gamma_{k}(x,y)})\varphi(\Gamma_{k}(x,y))J_{k}(x,y)dxd\nu_{k}(y).

Since
J_{k}
is Hölder in the leaf variable, we have

(\varphi\circ\Gamma_{k})(x,y)J_{k}(x,y)\in C_{x}^{c,\varrho}(O_{k}),\qquad\varrho=\min\{\alpha,\beta\}.

Then it follows from step 3 (see Section
8.4.3
) that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{-}(\varphi)=\mathfrak{K}^{-}_{\mathcal{Q}}\bigl((\varphi\circ\Gamma_{k})J_{k}\bigr)=\mathfrak{K}^{+}_{\mathcal{Q}}\bigl((\varphi\circ\Gamma_{k})J_{k}\bigr)=\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{+}(\varphi).

It remains to pass from functions supported in one foliation box to arbitrary

\beta
-Hölder functions on
\mathbb{T}^{N}
. Let

\{\Gamma_{k}(O_{k})\}_{k=1}^{M}
be a finite cover of
\mathbb{T}^{N}
by foliation
boxes, and let
\{\chi_{k}\}_{k=1}^{M}
be a
C^{\infty}
partition of unity
subordinate to this cover. For a global
\alpha
-
Hölder
vector field

\mathcal{V}
taking values in
\mathcal{E}_{i}
and for

\varphi\in C^{\beta}(\mathbb{T}^{N})
, we write

\varphi=\sum_{k=1}^{M}\chi_{k}\varphi.

Each
\chi_{k}\varphi
is supported in
\Gamma_{k}(O_{k})
, and

\|(\chi_{k}\varphi)\circ\Gamma_{k}\|_{C^{\beta}_{x}}\leq C_{k}\|\varphi\|_{C^{\beta}}.

Applying the local equality to

\mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{V}|_{\Gamma_{k}(O_{k})}
and to
\chi_{k}\varphi
, and then
summing over
k
, gives

\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{-}(\varphi)=\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{+}(\varphi).

Moreover, summing the local estimates (
8.17
) over the finite cover
gives

\|\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{-}(\varphi)\|\leq C_{\beta}\|R_{i}\|_{C^{1}}\|\mathcal{V}\|_{C^{\alpha}}\|\varphi\|_{C^{\beta}}.

Together with the corresponding estimate for
\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{+}
,
this completes the proof.

8.4.5.
Step 5: Conclusion

We now return to the distributions in Theorem
8.1
. For every

m\in\mathbb{Z}
, changing variables
z=H(x)
gives

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{H^{-1}z}(\mathcal{V}_{H^{-1}z})\,\omega(z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(z)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})_{z}(\mathcal{V}_{z})\,\omega\circ H(z)\kappa(z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(z).

Thus

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{V}}^{\pm}(\omega)=\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{\pm}\bigl((\omega\circ H)\kappa\bigr).

Recall that
H
is bi-
\eta
Hölder (see Section
3
). Since

\omega\in C^{\beta}(\mathbb{T}^{N})
and
\kappa\in C^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{N})
is
positive, we have

(\omega\circ H)\kappa\in C^{\bar{\beta}},\qquad\bar{\beta}:=\min\{\beta\eta,\alpha\}.

Moreover,

\|(\omega\circ H)\kappa\|_{C^{\bar{\beta}}}\leq C_{\beta}\|\omega\|_{C^{\beta}}.

Applying step 4 (see Section
8.4.4
) with exponent
\beta\eta
, we obtain

\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{-}\big((\omega\circ H)\cdot\kappa\big)=\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{+}\big((\omega\circ H)\cdot\kappa\big),

which is exactly

\mathfrak{D}^{-}_{\mathcal{V}}(\omega)=\mathfrak{D}^{+}_{\mathcal{V}}(\omega).

The same argument, together with (
8.17
) in Step 4, gives

\max\{\|\mathfrak{D}^{+}_{\mathcal{V}}(\omega)\|,\|\mathfrak{D}^{-}_{\mathcal{V}}(\omega)\|\}\leq C_{\beta}\|\mathcal{V}\|_{C^{\alpha}}\|\omega\|_{C^{\beta}}.

This completes the proof of Theorem
8.1
.

9.
C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
regularity of
H
along
\mathcal{W}^{f}_{i}

We recall the following notation:

(1)

\mathfrak{m}
and
\mu
are defined in Section
3.1
.

(2)

The numbers
\rho_{i}
, the Lyapunov blocks
\mathcal{E}_{i}
, and the
linear subspaces
E_{i}
are defined in Section
3.1
.

(3)

The maps
A_{i}
and
p_{i}
are defined in Section
3.4
.

(4)

H_{i}
is defined in (
7
) of Section
6.1
.

(5)

The foliations
\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f}
and
\mathcal{W}_{i}^{A}
are defined in
(
2
) of Section
3.1
.

(6)

The subbundles
\mathcal{F}_{i,j}
are defined in (
5
) of
Section
3.1
.

(7)

The subspaces
V_{i,j}
are defined in Theorem
5.1
.

(8)

The distribution
\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{+}
is defined in Theorem
8.1
.

Theorem 9.1
.

Suppose that
H(\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f})=\mathcal{W}_{i}^{A}
for some

i_{0}\leq i\leq\ell
. Then
H
is a
C^{1+\alpha}
diffeomorphism along

\mathcal{W}^{f}_{i}
.

The key step in the proof of Theorem
9.1
is the following proposition.

Proposition 9.2
.

For each
1\leq j\leq j_{i}
, there exist an

A_{i}
-invariant subspace
S_{i,j}\subset E_{i}
, a constant linear
isomorphism

L_{j}:V_{i,j}\to S_{i,j},

and a bi-
\alpha
-Hölder bundle
isomorphism

\mathcal{B}_{i,j}:\mathcal{F}_{i,j}\to S_{i,j}

such that for any
\alpha
-Hölder vector
field
\mathcal{V}
on
\mathbb{T}^{N}
taking values in
\mathcal{F}_{i,j}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{i,j}(\cdot)(\mathcal{V})=\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{+}+p_{i}(\mathcal{V})\qquad\text{as distributions}.

More precisely, for every
\omega\in C^{\beta}(\mathbb{T}^{N})
,

\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{+}(\omega)+\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}p_{i}(\mathcal{V}_{x})\omega(x)\,d\mathfrak{m}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}\mathcal{B}_{i,j}(x)(\mathcal{V}_{x})\omega(x)\,d\mathfrak{m}(x).

Remark 9.3
.

Equivalently,
\mathcal{B}_{i,j}
represents the derivative of

H_{i}=p_{i}\circ H=p_{i}+h_{i}
along
\mathcal{F}_{i,j}
.

9.1.
Proof of Theorem
9.1

We prove Theorem
9.1
assuming Proposition
9.2
. By Proposition
9.2
, applied with
j=j_{i}
, there exists an

A_{i}
-invariant subspace
S_{i,j_{i}}\subset E_{i}
, a constant linear
isomorphism

L_{j_{i}}:V_{i,j_{i}}\to S_{i,j_{i}},

and a bi-
\alpha
-Hölder bundle isomorphism

\mathcal{B}_{i,j_{i}}:\mathcal{F}_{i,j_{i}}\to S_{i,j_{i}}

such that
\mathcal{B}_{i,j_{i}}
represents the derivative of

H_{i}=p_{i}\circ H
along
\mathcal{F}_{i,j_{i}}
.

Since

\mathcal{F}_{i,j_{i}}=\mathcal{E}_{i}=T\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f}\qquad\text{and}\qquad V_{i,j_{i}}=E_{i},

we have

\dim S_{i,j_{i}}=\dim V_{i,j_{i}}=\dim E_{i}.

Because
S_{i,j_{i}}\subset E_{i}
, it follows that

S_{i,j_{i}}=E_{i}.

Therefore

\mathcal{B}_{i,j_{i}}:\mathcal{E}_{i}\to E_{i}

is a bi-
\alpha
-
Hölder
bundle isomorphism, and for every

x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
,

D(H_{i})_{x}|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}(x)}=\mathcal{B}_{i,j_{i}}(x).

Hence
H_{i}
is
C^{1+\alpha}
along
\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f}
, and its
leafwise derivative is everywhere invertible.

Since
H(\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f})=\mathcal{W}_{i}^{A}
, the components

p_{k}\circ H
,
k\neq i
, are constant along each leaf of

\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f}
. Thus, for every

u\in\mathcal{E}_{i}(x)
,

DH_{x}(u)=D(H_{i})_{x}(u)=\mathcal{B}_{i,j_{i}}(x)(u).

Consequently
H
is
C^{1+\alpha}
along
\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f}
, and
its leafwise derivative is an isomorphism at every point. The inverse function
theorem on the leaves implies that
H
is a

C^{1+\alpha}
diffeomorphism along
\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f}
.

It remains to prove Proposition
9.2
; this will occupy the rest of the section.

9.2.
Proof strategy for Proposition
9.2

We prove Proposition
9.2
by induction on
j
. The base case
j=1

follows from Theorem
7.1
; see Section
9.3.3
.

Assume that Proposition
9.2
holds for some
j<j_{i}
. Then

\mathcal{B}_{i,j}
represents the derivative of
H_{i}
along

\mathcal{F}_{i,j}
. Differentiating the conjugacy equation

H_{i}\circ f=A_{i}\circ H_{i}

along
\mathcal{F}_{i,j}
, we obtain

\mathcal{B}_{i,j}(fx)\circ Df_{x}|_{\mathcal{F}_{i,j}(x)}=A_{i}\circ\mathcal{B}_{i,j}(x).

This gives a Hölder conjugacy between
Df|_{\mathcal{F}_{i,j}}
and the
constant cocycle
A_{i}|_{S_{i,j}}
.

Using this conjugacy, together with the quotient trivialization supplied by
Theorem
5.1
, we choose a suitable trivialization

\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}:\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}\to V_{i,j+1}

in which the cocycle
Df|_{\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}}
becomes upper triangular:

\widehat{A}_{i,j+1}(x)=\begin{pmatrix}\rho_{i}M_{j}&\theta_{j}(x)\\
0&\rho_{i}L_{i,j+1}\end{pmatrix},

where the diagonal blocks have uniformly bounded normalized iterates. This
triangular form gives precise formulas for both positive and negative iterates
of
Df|_{\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}}
; see Sections
9.3.2
and
9.3.4
.
It is the main input for controlling the growth of

Df^{m}|_{\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}}
as
m\to\pm\infty
.

A key observation is that, for every
\alpha
-Hölder vector field

\mathcal{V}
taking values in
\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}
, the positive-time
normalized expression satisfies

\lim_{m\to\infty}A_{i}^{-m}p_{i}\circ Df_{z}^{m}(\mathcal{V}_{z})=p_{i}(\mathcal{V}_{z})+\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{+}

in the sense of distributions. Similarly, the negative-time expression
satisfies

\lim_{m\to-\infty}A_{i}^{-m}p_{i}\circ Df_{z}^{m}(\mathcal{V}_{z})=p_{i}(\mathcal{V}_{z})+\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{-}.

The positive-time formula has good Hölder control along stable leaves of

f
, while the negative-time formula has good Hölder control along unstable
leaves of
f
. However, the distribution-to-Hölder criterion is most
naturally applied on the linear torus, where the stable and unstable foliations
are linear and translation differences can be used.

For this reason, we trivialize the bundle using
\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}
and pass
to the linear base using
H^{-1}
. In these coordinates we work with the
conjugated distributions

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{V}}^{+},\qquad\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{V}}^{-},

and with the candidate distribution

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}

\displaystyle:=\lim_{m\to\infty}A_{i}^{-m}p_{i}\circ Df^{m}_{H^{-1}z}\circ\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}^{-1}(H^{-1}z)|_{V_{i,j+1}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\to-\infty}A_{i}^{-m}p_{i}\circ Df^{m}_{H^{-1}z}\circ\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}^{-1}(H^{-1}z)|_{V_{i,j+1}},

where the equality is proved in Section
9.3.5
. The identity

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{V}}^{+}=\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{V}}^{-}

combines the stable-direction estimates coming from the positive-time formula
with the unstable-direction estimates coming from the negative-time formula.
This gives the two-sided translation estimates required by the
distribution-to-Hölder criterion. Hence
\mathcal{T}
is represented by a
Hölder function; see Section
9.3.6
.

We then define

\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}(x):=\mathcal{T}(Hx)\circ\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}(x).

By construction, for every
\alpha
-Hölder vector field
\mathcal{V}
taking
values in
\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}
,

\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{+}(\omega)+\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}p_{i}(\mathcal{V}_{x})\omega(x)\,d\mathfrak{m}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}(x)(\mathcal{V}_{x})\omega(x)\,d\mathfrak{m}(x).

Thus
\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}
represents

D(H_{i})|_{\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}}
.

It remains to prove that
\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}
is a bi-
\alpha
-Hölder bundle
isomorphism onto a constant
A_{i}
-invariant subspace. First, one proves
injectivity on a full-measure set of generic points. The corresponding
measurable image bundle is
A_{i}
-invariant. Since
A_{i}|_{E_{i}}
has only one
Lyapunov exponent and is fiber bunched, the measurable image bundle is
Hölder by the regularity theorem for invariant subbundles of
Kalinin–Sadovskaya
[
23
]
. This regularity, together with continuity and
full support of
\mu
, promotes injectivity to every point.

Finally, using periodic points of suitable iterates of
A
, one shows that the
Hölder image bundle over the linear system is actually constant. Thus the
image is a fixed
A_{i}
-invariant subspace
S_{i,j+1}\subset E_{i}
.
Theorem 2.7 of
[
38
]
then upgrades
\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}
to an

\alpha
-Hölder conjugacy, and compactness gives
\alpha
-Hölder
regularity of the inverse. Hence
\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}
is bi-
\alpha
-Hölder.
This completes the induction step, and therefore proves
Proposition
9.2
. The details of this final step are given in
Section
9.3.7
.

9.3.
Proof of Proposition
9.2

9.3.1.
A criterion for distributions to be Hölder

Lemma 9.4
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a distribution on
\mathbb{T}^{N}
. Suppose that, for every

v\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
with
\|v\|\leq 1
, the distribution

F_{v}(x):=\mathcal{T}(x)-\mathcal{T}(x+v)

is a
C^{\alpha}
function, and assume moreover that

\sup_{\|v\|\leq 1}\|F_{v}\|_{C^{\alpha}}<\infty.

Then
\mathcal{T}
is a
C^{\alpha}
function.

Proof.

Let
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
be supported in the ball

\{\|v\|\leq 1\}
, with
\phi\geq 0
and

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\phi(v)\,dv=1.

Translations are understood modulo
\mathbb{Z}^{N}
. Define

G(x):=\mathcal{T}(x)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\mathcal{T}(x+v)\phi(v)\,dv.

Then, in the sense of distributions,

G(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\bigl(\mathcal{T}(x)-\mathcal{T}(x+v)\bigr)\phi(v)\,dv=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}F_{v}(x)\phi(v)\,dv.

By the uniform
C^{\alpha}
bound on
F_{v}
, it follows that
G\in C^{\alpha}
.
Indeed,

\|G\|_{C^{\alpha}}\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\|F_{v}\|_{C^{\alpha}}\phi(v)\,dv\leq\sup_{\|v\|\leq 1}\|F_{v}\|_{C^{\alpha}}.

On the other hand,

x\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\mathcal{T}(x+v)\phi(v)\,dv

is the convolution of the distribution
T
with a smooth compactly supported
kernel, and is therefore
C^{\infty}
. Hence

\mathcal{T}=G+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\mathcal{T}(\cdot+v)\phi(v)\,dv

is represented by a
C^{\alpha}
function.
∎

9.3.2.
Step 1: Preparatory step

We recall notations in Theorem
5.1
. By Theorem
5.1
, there exists a bi-
\alpha
-Hölder bundle
isomorphism

\mathcal{C}_{i}:\mathcal{E}_{i}\to E_{i}

such that, for every
x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
, the map

\mathcal{C}_{i}(x):\mathcal{E}_{i}(x)\to E_{i}

is a linear isomorphism satisfying

(9.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{i}(fx)\circ Df_{x}|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}(x)}=\widetilde{A}_{i}(x)\circ\mathcal{C}_{i}(x),\qquad x\in\mathbb{T}^{N},

where
\widetilde{A}_{i}(x):E_{i}\to E_{i}
is block upper triangular and its
diagonal blocks coincide with the corresponding diagonal blocks of
A_{i}
.
Moreover, for every
0\leq j\leq j_{i}
,

\mathcal{C}_{i}(x)(\mathcal{F}_{i,j}(x))=V_{i,j}.

We denote the restriction by

\mathcal{C}_{i,j}(x):=\mathcal{C}_{i}(x)|_{\mathcal{F}_{i,j}(x)}.

We prove Proposition
9.2
by induction in the following steps.

9.3.3.
Step 2: The base case
j=1
and the inductive assumption

By Theorem
7.1
,
H
is a
C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
diffeomorphism along

\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{F}_{i,1}}
. Moreover, for every

x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
and
u\in\mathcal{F}_{i,1}(x)
,

D(H_{i})_{x}(u)=B_{i}\circ\mathcal{C}_{i,1}(x)(u).

Set

\displaystyle L_{1}:=B_{i},\quad\mathcal{B}_{i,1}:=B_{i}\circ\mathcal{C}_{i,1},\quad S_{i,1}:=B_{i}(V_{i,1}).

Since

\displaystyle H_{i}=p_{i}+h_{i}=p_{i}+\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}R_{i}\circ f^{m},

we have, in the sense of distributions along
\mathcal{F}_{i,1}
,

\displaystyle D(H_{i})_{x}(u)=p_{i}(u)+\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}A_{i}^{-(m+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{m})(u).

Therefore, for every
\alpha
-Hölder vector field

\mathcal{V}
taking values in
\mathcal{F}_{i,1}
and every

\omega\in C^{\beta}(\mathbb{T}^{N})
,

\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{+}(\omega)+\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}p_{i}(\mathcal{V}_{x})\omega(x)\,d\mathfrak{m}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}\mathcal{B}_{i,1}(x)(\mathcal{V}_{x})\omega(x)\,d\mathfrak{m}(x).

Thus Proposition
9.2
holds for
j=1
.

Now suppose that Proposition
9.2
holds for some

1\leq j\leq j_{i}-1
. Then
H_{i}
is
C^{1+\alpha}
along
\mathcal{F}_{i,j}
. Differentiating the conjugacy equation

H_{i}\circ f=A_{i}\circ H_{i}

along
\mathcal{F}_{i,j}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{i,j}(fx)\circ Df_{x}|_{\mathcal{F}_{i,j}}=(A_{i}|_{S_{i,j}})\circ\mathcal{B}_{i,j}(x)|_{\mathcal{F}_{i,j}},\qquad\forall\,x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}.

Equivalently,

(9.2)

\displaystyle B_{j}^{-1}\mathcal{B}_{i,j}(fx)\circ Df_{x}|_{\mathcal{F}_{i,j}}=(B_{j}^{-1}A_{i}B_{j})|_{V_{i,j}}\circ B_{j}^{-1}\mathcal{B}_{i,j}(x)|_{\mathcal{F}_{i,j}},\qquad\forall\,x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}.

9.3.4.
Step 3: Expressions for
Df^{m}|_{\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}}

Choose an
\alpha
-
Hölder
splitting

\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}=\mathcal{F}_{i,j}\oplus\mathcal{G}_{i,j+1}.

By Theorem
5.1
, we also have the
A
-invariant decomposition

V_{i,j+1}=V_{i,j}\oplus W_{i,j+1}.

On
\mathcal{F}_{i,j}
, define

\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}(x)|_{\mathcal{F}_{i,j}(x)}=B_{j}^{-1}\mathcal{B}_{i,j}(x).

On the quotient
\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}/\mathcal{F}_{i,j}
, the map

\mathcal{C}_{i}
induces a bi-
\alpha
-
Hölder
bundle isomorphism

\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{i,j+1}(x):\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}(x)/\mathcal{F}_{i,j}(x)\to V_{i,j+1}/V_{i,j}.

Using the decomposition
V_{i,j+1}=V_{i,j}\oplus W_{i,j+1}
, we identify

V_{i,j+1}/V_{i,j}
with
W_{i,j+1}
. Also, using the splitting

\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}=\mathcal{F}_{i,j}\oplus\mathcal{G}_{i,j+1},

we identify
\mathcal{G}_{i,j+1}(x)
with

\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}(x)/\mathcal{F}_{i,j}(x)
. We then define

\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{i,j+1}(x):\mathcal{G}_{i,j+1}(x)\to W_{i,j+1}

to be the corresponding quotient trivialization. With this choice, the
induced quotient cocycle
\overline{Df}:\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}/\mathcal{F}_{i,j}\to\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}/\mathcal{F}_{i,j}
is conjugated to the next diagonal block

\rho_{i}L_{i,j+1}
of
\widetilde{A}_{i}(x)
, as follows from (
9.1
). Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}(x)(u+v)=B_{j}^{-1}\mathcal{B}_{i,j}(x)u+\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{i,j+1}(x)v,

where
u\in\mathcal{F}_{i,j}(x)
,
v\in\mathcal{G}_{i,j+1}(x)
.

Then
\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}
is a bi-
\alpha
-Hölder bundle
isomorphism

\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}:\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}\to V_{i,j+1}

satisfying

\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}(x)|_{\mathcal{F}_{i,j}(x)}=B_{j}^{-1}\mathcal{B}_{i,j}(x).

Consider the cocycle

\widehat{A}_{i,j+1}(x):=\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}(fx)\circ Df_{x}|_{\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}(x)}\circ\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}(x)^{-1}.

With respect to the decomposition

V_{i,j+1}=V_{i,j}\oplus W_{i,j+1},

this cocycle has block upper-triangular form

\widehat{A}_{i,j+1}(x)=\begin{pmatrix}\rho_{i}M_{j}&\theta_{j}(x)\\
0&\rho_{i}L_{i,j+1}\end{pmatrix}.

Indeed, the lower-left block is zero because

Df_{x}\mathcal{F}_{i,j}(x)=\mathcal{F}_{i,j}(fx).

The upper-left block is

\rho_{i}M_{j}=(B_{j}^{-1}A_{i}B_{j})|_{V_{i,j}},

by (
9.2
), where

M_{j}:=B_{j}^{-1}\circ(\rho_{i}^{-1}A_{i})|_{S_{i,j}}\circ B_{j}.

The lower-right block is
\rho_{i}L_{i,j+1}
by the choice of

\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{i,j+1}
and the conjugacy relation (
9.1
). Finally,
the off-diagonal term
\theta_{j}(x)
is
\alpha
-
Hölder
, because

Df
,
\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}
, and
\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}^{-1}
are

\alpha
-Hölder.

Therefore, for every
m\geq 1
,

(9.3)

\displaystyle Df_{x}^{m}|_{\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}(x)}=\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}(f^{m}x)^{-1}\widetilde{A}_{i,j+1,m}(x)\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}(x),

where

\widetilde{A}_{i,j+1,m}(x)=\prod_{k=0}^{m-1}\begin{pmatrix}\rho_{i}M_{j}&\theta_{j}(f^{k}x)\\
0&\rho_{i}L_{i,j+1}\end{pmatrix}.

Equivalently,

\widetilde{A}_{i,j+1,m}(x)=\rho_{i}^{m}\begin{pmatrix}M_{j}^{m}&\rho_{i}^{-1}\theta_{j,m}(x)\\
0&L_{i,j+1}^{m}\end{pmatrix},

where

(9.4)

\displaystyle\theta_{j,m}(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}M_{j}^{m-1-k}\theta_{j}(f^{k}x)L_{i,j+1}^{k}.

This together with (
9.3
) show that for every
m\geq 1
,

(9.5)

\displaystyle Df_{x}^{m}|_{\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}(x)}=\rho_{i}^{m}\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}(f^{m}x)^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}M_{j}^{m}&\rho_{i}^{-1}\theta_{j,m}(x)\\
0&L_{i,j+1}^{m}\end{pmatrix}\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}(x),

By construction, the normalized blocks
M_{j}
and
L_{i,j+1}
have uniformly bounded
integer powers. Thus

(9.6)

\displaystyle\max\{\lVert M_{j}^{m}\rVert,\,\lVert L_{i,j+1}^{m}\rVert\}\leq C,\qquad\forall\,m\in\mathbb{Z}.

Similarly, for negative iterates, writing
n\geq 1
, we have

(9.7)

\displaystyle Df_{x}^{-n}|_{\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}(x)}=\rho_{i}^{-n}\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}(f^{-n}x)^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}M_{j}^{-n}&\eta_{j,n}(x)\\
0&L_{i,j+1}^{-n}\end{pmatrix}\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}(x),

where

\eta_{j,n}(x)=-\rho_{i}^{-1}\sum_{k=1}^{n}M_{j}^{-(n-k+1)}\theta_{j}(f^{-k}x)L_{i,j+1}^{-k}.

9.3.5.
Step 4: Equality of the positive- and negative-time limits

In this part, we show that

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}:

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\to\infty}A_{i}^{-m}p_{i}\circ Df^{m}_{H^{-1}z}\circ\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}^{-1}(H^{-1}z)|_{V_{i,j+1}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\to-\infty}A_{i}^{-m}p_{i}\circ Df^{m}_{H^{-1}z}\circ\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}^{-1}(H^{-1}z)|_{V_{i,j+1}}

as distributions.

We first recall that the trivialization

\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}:\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}\to V_{i,j+1}

identifies
\alpha
-Hölder vector fields tangent to

\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}
with
\alpha
-Hölder

V_{i,j+1}
-valued functions on
\mathbb{T}^{N}
. More precisely, if

\mathcal{V}
is an
\alpha
-Hölder vector field taking values in

\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}
, then

\mathfrak{v}_{x}:=\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}(x)(\mathcal{V}_{x})

is an
\alpha
-Hölder
V_{i,j+1}
-valued function. Conversely, if

\mathfrak{v}
is an
\alpha
-Hölder
V_{i,j+1}
-valued function, then

\mathcal{V}_{x}:=\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}^{-1}(x)(\mathfrak{v}_{x})

is an
\alpha
-Hölder vector field taking values in

\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}
. Moreover, since both
\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}
and

\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}^{-1}
are Hölder bundle maps over the compact base, the
corresponding
C^{\alpha}
norms are comparable. Thus it is equivalent to test
the candidate limit against
\alpha
-Hölder

V_{i,j+1}
-valued functions or against
\alpha
-Hölder vector fields
tangent to
\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}
.

More precisely, let
\mathfrak{v}
be an
\alpha
-
Hölder

V_{i,j+1}
-valued function on
\mathbb{T}^{N}
, and let

\omega\in C^{\beta}(\mathbb{T}^{N})
. We prove that

\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}A_{i}^{-m}p_{i}\circ Df^{m}_{H^{-1}z}\circ\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}^{-1}(H^{-1}z)(\mathfrak{v}_{H^{-1}z})\omega(z)d\mathfrak{m}

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\to-\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}A_{i}^{-m}p_{i}\circ Df^{m}_{H^{-1}z}\circ\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}^{-1}(H^{-1}z)(\mathfrak{v}_{H^{-1}z})\omega(z)d\mathfrak{m}.

Let

\displaystyle J(x)=-p_{i}|_{(\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1})_{x}}.

Then
J
satisfies the following twisted cohomological equation over
f
:

(9.8)

\displaystyle A_{i}\circ J(x)-J(fx)\circ Df|_{(\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1})_{x}}=DR_{i}|_{(\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1})_{x}},\qquad x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}.

Iterating (
9.8
) forward, for every
m\geq 1
, we have

J(x)+A_{i}^{-m}p_{i}\circ Df_{x}^{m}|_{\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}(x)}=\sum_{n=0}^{m-1}A_{i}^{-(n+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{n})_{x}|_{\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}(x)}.

Now take
x=H^{-1}z
and compose on the right with

\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}^{-1}(H^{-1}z)|_{V_{i,j+1}}.

Then we have

\displaystyle-p_{i}\circ\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}^{-1}(H^{-1}z)|_{V_{i,j+1}}+A_{i}^{-m}p_{i}\circ Df^{m}_{H^{-1}z}\circ\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}^{-1}(H^{-1}z)|_{V_{i,j+1}}

(9.9)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{m-1}A_{i}^{-(n+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{n})_{H^{-1}z}\circ\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}^{-1}(H^{-1}z)|_{V_{i,j+1}}.

On the other hand, iterating (
9.8
) backward, for every

m\geq 1
, we obtain

J(x)+A_{i}^{m}p_{i}\circ Df_{x}^{-m}|_{\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}(x)}=-\sum_{n=-1}^{-m}A_{i}^{-(n+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{n})_{x}|_{\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}(x)}.

Now take again
x=H^{-1}z
and compose on the right with

\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}^{-1}(H^{-1}z)|_{V_{i,j+1}}.

Then
we get

\displaystyle-p_{i}\circ\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}^{-1}(H^{-1}z)|_{V_{i,j+1}}+A_{i}^{m}p_{i}\circ Df^{-m}_{H^{-1}z}\circ\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}^{-1}(H^{-1}z)|_{V_{i,j+1}}

(9.10)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{n=-m}^{-1}A_{i}^{-(n+1)}D(R_{i}\circ f^{n})_{H^{-1}z}\circ\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}^{-1}(H^{-1}z)|_{V_{i,j+1}}.

Now define an
\alpha
-Hölder vector field

\mathcal{V}_{y}:=\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}^{-1}(y)(\mathfrak{v}_{y}),\qquad y\in\mathbb{T}^{N}.

Then
\mathcal{V}
takes values in
\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}
. By using (
9.3.5
) and the notation of Theorem
8.1
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}A_{i}^{-m}p_{i}\circ Df^{m}_{H^{-1}z}\circ\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}^{-1}(H^{-1}z)(\mathfrak{v}_{H^{-1}z})\omega(z)d\mathfrak{m}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}p_{i}(\mathcal{V}_{H^{-1}z})\omega(z)d\mathfrak{m}+\mathfrak{D}^{+}_{\mathcal{V}}(\omega).

Similarly, by using (
9.3.5
) we have

\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}A_{i}^{m}p_{i}\circ Df^{-m}_{H^{-1}z}\circ\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}^{-1}(H^{-1}z)(\mathfrak{v}_{H^{-1}z})\,\omega(z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(z)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}p_{i}(\mathcal{V}_{H^{-1}z})\omega(z)\,d\mathfrak{m}(z)+\mathfrak{D}^{-}_{\mathcal{V}}(\omega).

By Theorem
8.1
, we have

\mathfrak{D}^{+}_{\mathcal{V}}=\mathfrak{D}^{-}_{\mathcal{V}}.

Therefore the positive- and negative-time limits are equal as distributions.

9.3.6.
Step 5: Hölder regularity of
\mathcal{T}

In this step, we show that the distribution
\mathcal{T}
is
a
C^{\alpha\eta}
function (see Section
3
). Let

\displaystyle F_{v}(z):=\mathcal{T}(z)-\mathcal{T}(z+v),\qquad v\in\mathbb{R}^{N}.

We first prove that

(9.11)

\displaystyle\lVert F_{v}\rVert_{C^{0}}\leq C\lVert v\rVert^{\alpha\eta},\qquad v\in\mathbb{R}^{N},\quad\lVert v\rVert\leq 1.

We recall for any
v\in E^{s,A}
and any
w\in E^{u,A}
and any
m\geq 0
we have

(9.12)

\displaystyle\lVert A^{m}v\rVert\leq C\nu_{0}^{m}\lVert v\rVert,\quad\lVert A^{-m}w\rVert\leq C\nu_{0}^{m}\lVert w\rVert,

see (
3.5
) of Section
3.2
.

First, we show that (
9.11
) holds for any
v\in E^{s,A}
. Using (
9.5
), the
positive-time expression for
\mathcal{T}
is

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\to\infty}A_{i}^{-m}p_{i}\circ Df^{m}_{H^{-1}z}\circ\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}^{-1}(H^{-1}z)|_{V_{i,j+1}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\to\infty}\rho_{i}^{m}A_{i}^{-m}\big(p_{i}\circ\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}\circ H^{-1}\big)(A^{m}z)\begin{pmatrix}M_{j}^{m}&\rho_{i}^{-1}\theta_{j,m}(H^{-1}z)\\
0&L_{i,j+1}^{m}\end{pmatrix}.

Then we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(z)-\mathcal{T}(z+v)=\mathcal{Y}_{1}+\mathcal{Y}_{2},

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{1}

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\to\infty}\rho_{i}^{m}A_{i}^{-m}\big(p_{i}\circ\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}\circ H^{-1}\big)(A^{m}z)\begin{pmatrix}M_{j}^{m}&\rho_{i}^{-1}\theta_{j,m}(H^{-1}z)\\
0&L_{i,j+1}^{m}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle-\lim_{m\to\infty}\rho_{i}^{m}A_{i}^{-m}\big(p_{i}\circ\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}\circ H^{-1}\big)(A^{m}(z+v))\begin{pmatrix}M_{j}^{m}&\rho_{i}^{-1}\theta_{j,m}(H^{-1}z)\\
0&L_{i,j+1}^{m}\end{pmatrix}

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{2}

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\to\infty}\rho_{i}^{m}A_{i}^{-m}\big(p_{i}\circ\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}\circ H^{-1}\big)(A^{m}(z+v))\begin{pmatrix}M_{j}^{m}&\rho_{i}^{-1}\theta_{j,m}(H^{-1}z)\\
0&L_{i,j+1}^{m}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle-\lim_{m\to\infty}\rho_{i}^{m}A_{i}^{-m}\big(p_{i}\circ\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}\circ H^{-1}\big)(A^{m}(z+v))\begin{pmatrix}M_{j}^{m}&\rho_{i}^{-1}\theta_{j,m}(H^{-1}(z+v))\\
0&L_{i,j+1}^{m}\end{pmatrix}.

Now we estimate
\mathcal{Y}_{1}
. It follows from (
9.4
) and (
9.6
) that

\displaystyle\Big\|\begin{pmatrix}M_{j}^{m}&\rho_{i}^{-1}\theta_{j,m}(H^{-1}z)\\
0&L_{i,j+1}^{m}\end{pmatrix}\Big\|\leq Cm,\qquad\forall\,m\geq 1.

Since
H^{-1}
is
\eta
-Hölder and

\mathcal{C}_{i,j+1}^{-1}
is
\alpha
-Hölder, the map

p_{i}\circ\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}^{-1}\circ H^{-1}

is
\alpha\eta
-Hölder. Then we have

\displaystyle\left\|\bigl(p_{i}\circ\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}^{-1}\circ H^{-1}\bigr)(A^{m}(z+v))-\bigl(p_{i}\circ\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}^{-1}\circ H^{-1}\bigr)(A^{m}z)\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\big\|p_{i}\circ\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}^{-1}\circ H^{-1}\big\|_{C^{\alpha\eta}}\|A^{m}v\|^{\alpha\eta}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{\leq}C\nu^{m\alpha\eta}\|v\|^{\alpha\eta}.

Here in
(1)
we use (
9.12
). The above discussion, together with (
3.4
) of Section
3.2
, gives

\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{Y}_{1}\rVert\leq\lim_{m\to\infty}C\cdot C\nu^{m\alpha\eta}\|v\|^{\alpha\eta}\cdot Cm=0.

Next, we estimate
\mathcal{Y}_{2}
. From (
9.4
), we have

\displaystyle\big\|\theta_{j,m}(H^{-1}(z+v))-\theta_{j,m}(H^{-1}(z))\big\|

\displaystyle=\Big\|\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}M_{j}^{m-1-k}(\theta_{j}\circ H^{-1})(A^{k}(z+v))L_{i,j+1}^{k}

\displaystyle\qquad-\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}M_{j}^{m-1-k}(\theta_{j}\circ H^{-1})(A^{k}(z))L_{i,j+1}^{k}\Big\|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\|M_{j}^{m-1-k}\|\cdot\big\|(\theta_{j}\circ H^{-1})(A^{k}(z+v))-(\theta_{j}\circ H^{-1})(A^{k}z)\big\|\cdot\|L_{i,j+1}^{k}\|

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(1)}}{\leq}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}C\cdot\lVert\theta\circ H^{-1}\rVert_{C^{\alpha\eta}}\lVert A^{k}v\rVert^{\alpha\eta}\cdot C

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(2)}}{\leq}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}C_{1}\lVert\theta\circ H^{-1}\rVert_{C^{\alpha\eta}}\nu^{k\alpha\eta}\|v\|^{\alpha\eta}

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}\|v\|^{\alpha\eta}.

Here in
(1)
we use (
9.6
); in
(2)
we use (
9.12
).

The above discussion shows that

\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{Y}_{2}\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{m\to\infty}\lVert\rho_{i}^{m}A_{i}^{-m}\rVert\cdot\lVert p_{i}\circ\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}\circ H^{-1}\rVert_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\cdot\big\|\theta_{j,m}(H^{-1}(z+v))-\theta_{j,m}(H^{-1}(z))\big\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|v\|^{\alpha\eta}.

This proves (
9.11
) for
v\in E^{s,A}
. Using the negative-time expression
for
\mathcal{T}
and the second estimate in (
9.12
), the same argument
proves (
9.11
) for
v\in E^{u,A}
.

Now let
v\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
,
\|v\|\leq 1
. Write

v=v^{s}+v^{u},\qquad v^{s}\in E^{s},\quad v^{u}\in E^{u}.

Since the projections onto
E^{s,A}
and
E^{u,A}
are bounded,

\|v^{s}\|+\|v^{u}\|\leq C\|v\|.

Then

\displaystyle F_{v}(z)=F_{v^{s}}(z)+F_{v^{u}}(z+v^{s}).

Therefore

\|F_{v}\|_{C^{0}}\leq C\|v^{s}\|^{\alpha\eta}+C\|v^{u}\|^{\alpha\eta}\leq C_{1}\|v\|^{\alpha\eta}.

Thus (
9.11
) holds for every
v\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
with
\|v\|\leq 1
.

We next show that
F_{v}
is uniformly
C^{\alpha\eta}
. Let
z,z_{1}\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
with
\lVert z-z_{1}\rVert\leq 1
. Then

\displaystyle F_{v}(z)-F_{v}(z_{1})

\displaystyle=F_{z_{1}-z}(z)-F_{z_{1}-z}(z+v).

Using (
9.11
) with the translation vector
z_{1}-z
, we obtain

\|F_{v}(z)-F_{v}(z_{1})\|\leq C\|z-z_{1}\|^{\alpha\eta}.

Together with (
9.11
), this gives

\sup_{\|v\|\leq 1}\|F_{v}\|_{C^{\alpha\eta}}<\infty.

Therefore, by Lemma
9.4
, applied componentwise,

\mathcal{T}
is represented by a
C^{\alpha\eta}
function.

9.3.7.
Step 6: Conclusion

By the definition of
\mathcal{T}
(see Section
9.3.5
) and the Hölder regularity of

\mathcal{T}
(see Section
9.3.6
), the distributional derivative of
H_{i}
along

\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}
is represented by a Hölder bundle map.
Thus
H_{i}
is
C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
along
\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}
.

More precisely, define

\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}(x):=\mathcal{T}(Hx)\circ\mathcal{O}_{i,j+1}(x),\qquad x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}.

Then
\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}:\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}\to E_{i}
is

Hölder
, and for every
\alpha
-
Hölder
vector field

\mathcal{V}
taking values in
\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}
, we have

\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathcal{V}}^{+}(\omega)+\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}p_{i}(\mathcal{V}_{x})\omega(x)\,d\mathfrak{m}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{N}}\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}(x)(\mathcal{V}_{x})\omega(x)\,d\mathfrak{m}(x).

Therefore
\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}
represents

D(H_{i})|_{\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}}
, and
H_{i}
is

C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
along
\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}
.

Since
H(\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f})=\mathcal{W}_{i}^{A}
, the components

p_{k}\circ H
,
k\neq i
, are constant along
\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f}
. Hence the
regularity of
H_{i}=p_{i}\circ H
implies the corresponding leafwise regularity
of
H
along
\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}
.

Next, we show that
D(H_{i})|_{\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}}
is injective.
Differentiating the conjugacy equation

H_{i}\circ f=A_{i}\circ H_{i}

along
\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}
, we obtain

(9.13)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}(fx)\circ Df_{x}|_{\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}(x)}=A_{i}\circ\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}(x),\qquad x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}.

Consequently,

(9.14)

\displaystyle Df_{x}^{n}\bigl(\ker\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}(x)\bigr)=\ker\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}(f^{n}x),\qquad n\in\mathbb{Z}.

Choose a generic
x_{0}
with respect to
\mu
. Suppose, for
contradiction, that

\ker\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}(x_{0})\neq\{0\}.

Since
x_{0}
has dense
f
-orbit, (
9.14
) implies that

\ker\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}(f^{n}x_{0})\neq\{0\}
for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
By continuity of
\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}
, we have

\displaystyle\ker\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}(x)\neq\{0\},\qquad\forall\,x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}.

Choose a point where
\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}
is maximal. On a
small neighborhood of this point, the rank is locally constant, and hence

\ker\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}
is a nontrivial continuous subbundle. Therefore, we
may choose a nonzero continuous vector field
\mathcal{X}
tangent to

\ker\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}
on this neighborhood. Let
\gamma(t)
be a
nonconstant integral curve of
\mathcal{X}
. Then

D(H_{i})_{\gamma(t)}(\gamma^{\prime}(t))=0.

Since the other components
p_{k}\circ H
,
k\neq i
, are constant along

\mathcal{W}_{i}^{f}
, we also have

DH_{\gamma(t)}(\gamma^{\prime}(t))=0.

Thus
H(\gamma(t))
is constant. This contradicts the injectivity of
H
.
Hence

\ker\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}(x_{0})=\{0\}.

Let
\mathcal{R}
denote the set of
\mu
-generic points. On
\mathcal{R}
, the
map
\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}
is injective. Define the measurable
image bundle over
\mathcal{R}
by

\mathcal{S}_{x}:=\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}(x)\bigl(\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}(x)\bigr).

Then (
9.13
) gives

A_{i}(\mathcal{S}_{x})=\mathcal{S}_{fx},\qquad x\in\mathcal{R}.

Thus
\mathcal{S}
is a
\mu
-measurable invariant subbundle of the constant
cocycle
A_{i}
on
E_{i}
.

Since all eigenvalues of
A_{i}
have modulus
\rho_{i}
, the constant cocycle

A_{i}|_{E_{i}}
has only one Lyapunov exponent. Hence

\lambda^{+}(A_{i}|_{E_{i}},\mu)=\lambda^{-}(A_{i}|_{E_{i}},\mu).

Moreover,
A_{i}|_{E_{i}}
is fiber bunched. Therefore, by Theorem 3.3 and
Corollary 3.8 of
[
23
]
, the measurable invariant subbundle
\mathcal{S}

coincides
\mu
-almost everywhere with a Hölder continuous invariant
subbundle. We still denote this Hölder subbundle by
\mathcal{S}
.

We now show that
\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}(x)
is injective for every

x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
. Since
\mathcal{S}
is Hölder and
\mu
has full support,
the equality

\mathcal{S}_{x}=\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}(x)\bigl(\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}(x)\bigr)

extends from
\mathcal{R}
to every
x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
. Since

\dim\mathcal{S}_{x}=\dim\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}(x),

it follows that
\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}(x)
has full rank, and hence is injective,
for every
x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
.

Define a subbundle
\tau\subset E_{i}
by

\displaystyle\tau_{x}=\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}(H^{-1}x)(\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}|_{H^{-1}x}),\qquad\forall\,x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}.

Using (
9.13
), we see that
\tau
is
A
-invariant. Since
\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}
,
\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}
, and
H^{-1}
are
Hölder, the bundle
\tau
is Hölder. Moreover, by
the injectivity of
\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}
,

\dim\tau_{x}=\dim\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1},\qquad x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}.

Let

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}=\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\,A\text{ and }A^{n}\text{ have exactly the same invariant subspaces}\}.

Choose a sequence
s_{n}\in\mathbb{S}
such that
s_{n}\to\infty
. Let

\displaystyle P_{s_{n}}=\{x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}:\,A^{s_{n}}(x)=x\}\quad\text{and}\quad P=\bigcup_{n}P_{s_{n}}.

By Lemma
3.1
,
P
is dense in
\mathbb{T}^{N}
. The
A
-invariancy of
\tau
implies that

\displaystyle A_{i}^{s_{n}}(\tau_{x})=\tau_{x}.

Since
A
and
A^{s_{n}}
have the same invariant subspaces, it follows that

\displaystyle A_{i}(\tau_{x})=\tau_{x},\qquad\forall\,x\in P.

Since
A
has no repeated eigenvalues,
A_{i}
has only finitely many
A_{i}
-invariant subspaces of dimension

\dim\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}
inside
E_{i}
. The Hölder continuity of the bundle
\tau
and density of
P

implies that

\tau
is constant. Hence there exists
an
A_{i}
-invariant subspace
S_{i,j+1}\subset E_{i}
such that

\tau_{x}=S_{i,j+1},\qquad x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}.

Thus, for every
x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}
,

\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}(x):\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}(x)\to S_{i,j+1}

is a linear isomorphism. Next, we show that
\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}
is
\alpha
-Hölder as a bundle map.
The cocycle
Df|_{\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}}
is
\alpha
-Hölder and fiber bunched,
while the target cocycle
A_{i}|_{S_{i,j+1}}
is uniformly quasiconformal, indeed
conformal after choosing an adapted inner product. By (
9.13
), the measurable bundle map
\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}
conjugates
the cocycle
Df|_{\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}}
to the constant cocycle

A_{i}|_{S_{i,j+1}}
. Since
\mu
is ergodic, has
full support, and has local product structure, Theorem 2.7 of
[
38
]

implies that the measurable conjugacy
\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}
coincides

\mu
-almost everywhere with an
\alpha
-Hölder continuous conjugacy. Since

\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}
is already continuous and
\mu
has full support, the two
conjugacies agree everywhere. Hence
\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}
is
\alpha
-Hölder
on
\mathbb{T}^{N}
.

Since
\mathbb{T}^{N}
is compact and
\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}(x)
is injective for
every
x
, there exists
c>0
such that

\|\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}(x)u\|\geq c\|u\|,\qquad x\in\mathbb{T}^{N},\quad u\in\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}(x).

Equivalently,

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}^{N}}\|\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}(x)^{-1}\|<\infty.

This implies that the inverse bundle map is
\alpha
-Hölder, and so

\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}
is bi-
\alpha
-Hölder.

Since
\mathcal{C}_{i,j+1}(x):\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}(x)\to V_{i,j+1}
is a
linear isomorphism, we have

\dim S_{i,j+1}=\dim\mathcal{F}_{i,j+1}=\dim V_{i,j+1}.

Therefore we may choose a linear isomorphism

L_{j+1}:V_{i,j+1}\to S_{i,j+1}.

Hence the triple

\bigl(S_{i,j+1},\,L_{j+1},\,\mathcal{B}_{i,j+1}\bigr)

satisfies the conclusion of Proposition
9.2
for
j+1
. This completes the
induction step.

10.
Proof of Theorems
1.2
and
1.3

We first recall the following form of Journé’s lemma.

Lemma 10.1

(Journé
[
8
]
)
.

Let
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
be manifolds, and for
j=1,2
, let

\mathcal{F}_{j}^{s}
and
\mathcal{F}_{j}^{u}
be continuous transverse foliations
on
M_{j}
with uniformly smooth leaves. Suppose that

h:M_{1}\to M_{2}
is a homeomorphism such that

h(\mathcal{F}_{1}^{s})\subset\mathcal{F}_{2}^{s},\qquad h(\mathcal{F}_{1}^{u})\subset\mathcal{F}_{2}^{u}.

Assume moreover that the restrictions of
h
to the leaves of

\mathcal{F}_{1}^{s}
and
\mathcal{F}_{1}^{u}
are uniformly
C^{r+\nu}
, where

r\in\mathbb{N}
and
0<\nu<1
. Then
h
is
C^{r+\nu}
.

The final induction-and-Journé argument is standard and follows the same
strategy as in
[
30
]
. We include the details for completeness.

It follows from (
3
) in Section
3.1
that

H(\mathcal{W}_{i_{0}}^{f})=\mathcal{W}_{i_{0}}^{A}.

Hence, by Theorem
9.1
,
H
is a

C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
diffeomorphism along
\mathcal{W}_{i_{0}}^{f}
. This gives
the base case of the induction.

We now proceed by induction. Suppose that, for some

i_{0}\leq j<\ell
,
H
is a
C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
diffeomorphism along

\mathcal{W}_{i_{0},j}^{f}
. By (
4
) in Section
3.1
, applied successively inside
\mathcal{W}_{i_{0},j+1}^{f}
, we have

H(\mathcal{W}_{j+1}^{f})=\mathcal{W}_{j+1}^{A}.

Therefore, by Theorem
9.1
,
H
is a

C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
diffeomorphism along
\mathcal{W}_{j+1}^{f}
.

The foliations
\mathcal{W}_{i_{0},j}^{f}
and
\mathcal{W}_{j+1}^{f}
are transverse
subfoliations inside
\mathcal{W}_{i_{0},j+1}^{f}
. Similarly,

\mathcal{W}_{i_{0},j}^{A}
and
\mathcal{W}_{j+1}^{A}
are transverse subfoliations
inside
\mathcal{W}_{i_{0},j+1}^{A}
. Since
H
maps each of these foliations to
the corresponding linear foliation and is
C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
along both
subfoliations, Journé’s lemma implies that
H
is

C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
along
\mathcal{W}_{i_{0},j+1}^{f}
. Applying the same
argument to the leafwise inverse, we obtain that
H
is a

C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
diffeomorphism along
\mathcal{W}_{i_{0},j+1}^{f}
. This
proves the induction step.

Thus
H
is a
C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
diffeomorphism along

\mathcal{W}^{u,f}=\mathcal{W}_{i_{0},\ell}^{f}.

Applying the same argument to
f^{-1}
and
A^{-1}
, and using the fact that

H
also conjugates
f^{-1}
to
A^{-1}
, we obtain that
H
is a

C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
diffeomorphism along

\mathcal{W}^{s,f}=\mathcal{W}^{u,f^{-1}}.

Finally, since the stable and unstable foliations are transverse and have
uniformly smooth leaves, Journé’s lemma implies that
H
is globally

C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
on
\mathbb{T}^{N}
. Applying the same reasoning to the
inverse conjugacy gives the same regularity for
H^{-1}
. Hence
H
is a

C^{1+\text{H\"{o}lder}}
diffeomorphism of
\mathbb{T}^{N}
. This completes the
proof.
